“You always have a choice, Ms. Bacchus.” Bourn paused long enough for that to finish being technically true. “But if you want answers about what we know, what’s happening with Kain and the police, and what your parents were actually doing in Montana—you’ll come with us.”

Montana. Nineteen years of no rangers, no wreckage, no theory that survived daylight, and this woman had dropped the word into a sentence as casually as a room number. CADENS knew something about her parents. Bourn had turned that something into bait and she knew it and Ahdia knew she knew it. She hated that it worked. It worked completely.

“Fine,” Ahdia said, standing before her legs could be consulted. “But if I throw up on your fancy facility floor—”

“We have protocols,” Mack said.

Somehow that was scarier than anything else on the roof. The drone drifted closer and its light climbed all three of them head to foot, brightening toward business.

“Oh no,” Ahdia managed. “Not again—”

“Deep breath,” Bourn advised. “It doesn’t help, but people feel better if they try.”

She tried. Space inverted anyway.

* * *

The second trip was worse, second-bee-sting worse: this time she knew what the universe planned to do with her and dread made a lousy anesthetic. Reality pulled her file, crossed out “rooftop” and penciled in “somewhere else” instead and every cell she owned received the correction at a slightly different moment. Star Trek still owed her an apology.

She reassembled on her hands and knees on concrete polished to such a corporate shine that she got to watch her own reflection throw up. They both did a thorough job.

“Protocol Sigma,” Mack told his radio. “Command center, level three. Standard cleanup.”

A janitor appeared unbothered inside the minute, steering a mop cart around her with the serenity of a man doing a familiar chore. Her vomit had a protocol. This happened here often enough to need a name and she was too busy shaking to decide how she felt about being a recurring event.

Once she could lift her head the room was every underground command center she had ever watched from her couch, except real and humming. Holographic displays hung in the air with nothing holding them up. People in tactical gear crossed paths with people in lab coats, and nobody saluted anybody. One whole wall ran live feeds: a storm rotating off some coastline, a sinkhole collecting a crowd, something burning under a headline in a language she couldn’t read.

“Welcome to CADENS,” Bourn said. “This is where we actually work.”

“You said better coffee.”

“I said we had coffee. Better is relative.” Bourn was already moving. “Conference room this way. The footage is already queued.”

The conference room offered a long table, a wall-sized display, and chairs with opinions about posture. The drone floated in after them and settled at head height like a housecat claiming a shelf.

“Water?” Mack held one out and she drained half the bottle in one pull, and sitting stopped being optional.

“The police ambush. Three simultaneous engagements across the city. Every member of the Go Squad kettled by coordinated police response. We were monitoring their communications. They knew exactly where your brother’s team would be.”

“How?”

“That’s what we wanted to know.” Bourn tipped her head toward the drone. “Show her.” She looked back at Ahdia. “Before the ambush makes sense, you need to see what you were doing that night.”

* * *

Gray light rose off the drone’s shell and a street she knew assembled itself over the table, and then the conference room stopped existing, because memory has better production values than any hologram.

The night of the warehouse. Hours after the crate, after the light, after the ice moved into her chest and unpacked. She ran for home, ten blocks uphill at a dead sprint, because the alternative was waiting at the docks to explain the artifact-shaped hole in somebody’s inventory. Home meant the couch. The couch was where she intended to have her breakdown, in comfort, with the TV on.

The weirdness arrived on an installment plan.

First a dog hung mid-bark, silent, all four paws off the ground and staying there. She filed it under adrenaline. A man stood checking a very expensive watch whose numbers never moved, and she filed that under broken watch and rich guy and not her problem. On the next corner a couple had their argument on pause, the woman’s finger parked an inch from the man’s sternum, neither of them blinking. A taxi idled at a green light with its driver slumped over the wheel and she nearly stopped for him, and some deep animal instinct voted no and she kept running.

And always behind her, strictly peripheral, the world kept twitching back on. The dog landed. Horns resumed. People snapped their heads down at their phones, hunting for the thirty seconds they could swear they’d just lost. She was carrying a bubble of stopped time the size of a modest living room and didn’t know it, and everyone she passed got their evening docked half a minute for standing too close.

Her working theory was hallucination. Her backup theory was that she was the Flash now, which genuinely troubled her for half a block on jurisdictional grounds, because the Flash was DC and the X-Men were Marvel, and whichever universe had drafted her hadn’t sent any paperwork.

“Wait, I was freezing time while running? I didn’t even know I was doing that.”

The conference room reassembled around her question. Bourn glanced at the drone.

“FAERIS,” she prompted. “Duration of unconscious freeze during subject’s return trajectory.”

“23 minutes continuous,” the drone reported. “Radius: approximately 6.1 meters. Subject maintaining temporal field unconsciously while in motion.”

“Twenty-three minutes,” Bourn repeated. “Maintaining a time freeze while running at full speed. Without even realizing it.” From her, flat delivery was what awe looked like.

The gray light swelled again, and Ahdia let it pull her back under.

Her building. The doorman stood frozen mid-wave and she waved back out of reflex, a courtesy she could feel guilty about at her leisure. Inside she jabbed the elevator call button and got nothing, jabbed it again in the special rhythm people reserve for elevators and the button stayed dark while the truth put itself together in pieces she did not want. Electricity was a thing that happened over time. Time, at present, was her fault. She lived on the thirty-fourth floor.

“You’ve got to be kidding me.”

The stairwell door accepted her without comment and shut behind her onto perfect dark—the emergency lights were electricity too, and electricity was hers now. The first eight floors went fine anyway, hand over hand on the railing, which she would later understand was the cardio equivalent of the first eight seconds of a fall. By ten her thighs had opened negotiations. By twelve she was hauling herself up the railing hand over hand, past the door she took for fifteen, where a sign she couldn’t see went on advertising the residents’ gym she had never once visited, information her body received as pure sarcasm regardless. By fifteen she was dying, not decoratively but actually dying: a heart attack at twenty-seven built from years of gas-station food and twelve-hour television days. Her heart had given up on rhythm. A hummingbird in a jar.

On the landing of twenty-two, folded over the railing, she had the idea that saved her life and complicated everything after it. The bubble had an edge; she had felt it dragging behind her all night like a bridal train made of stopped clocks. A thing with an edge had a size. A thing with a size could shrink. Just her, maybe. Just her heart. She pressed a hand over the cold spot where the Seed sat and asked her own heart, silently and sincerely, to slow down. Please. Just a little.

The cold answered. Her pulse stuttered, then settled into a calm she had done nothing to deserve.

“Oh,” she said as the stairwell swam gently sideways. “Oh that’s—”

She hadn’t fixed anything, was the thing. Heart calm, legs suffocating: she had redistributed the crisis and called it a fix. Silver linings. Floors twenty-three through thirty-four happened on willpower and railing and her own voice out loud in the dark. At the top she released her heart like a held breath, and her pulse came slamming back with interest, every postponed beat delivered at once, worse for the delay. She sat on the top step until her legs re-enlisted.

Her key found the lock on the third try. The penthouse was dark and the TV was off, and the TV was never off; the wrongness of that reached her before the shape on the couch did. Firas lay on his side, one arm trailing to the floor, still in yesterday’s clothes.

He wasn’t breathing.

“Firas?”

It came out at a pitch she didn’t recognize as hers. She crossed the room with no memory of crossing it.

“Firas!”

Nothing moved. Her brain, helpful to the end, laid out the math. She had run home inside a bubble of stopped time. She had carried it through her own front door. She had parked it on her brother, and her stupid cosmic time powers had done to Firas what a shotgun couldn’t and she had killed the only—

The clock. The wall clock above the TV, the one that ran six minutes fast on purpose, held its second hand still.

“Oh you’ve got to be kidding me.”

Apparently panic pulled the same trigger as everything else. Her fear for him had frozen him, frozen the room and the clock and the air, and kneeling over her maybe-dead brother she could not work out how to let go. She tried the mental equivalent of Ctrl-Z. She tried unfreeze, undo, cancel, whatever the opposite of a scream was. She ordered the clock to move and the clock declined, and the ice in her chest sat there, patient and bright, waiting for something she didn’t know how to hand over.

“Please,” she whispered, and the word carried none of the command she’d been attempting, which turned out to be the trick. “Please please please I can’t—I can’t do this again, I can’t lose—”

The clock ticked.

Firas pulled in a breath, long and rude, and let it go. Asleep. He was asleep on her couch, dead to the world in the ordinary reversible way, and she knelt there with her hands hovering over him, relearning how to exist.

So that was the lesson, delivered at the maximum available cost because that was the only tuition she ever paid: emotion pulls the trigger. Calm releases it. A device keyed to panic and unlocked by serenity, issued to a nervous system already running hot. What could possibly go wrong.

Firas mumbled something with no consonants in it and rolled over into the cushions.

She stood up on legs with strong opinions and went to the kitchen, and there on the counter sat that morning’s coffee mug, an inch of cold coffee poured by a woman who had never frozen a city block or a heart or a brother. She had to learn to control this thing. Because next time he startled her, dropped a glass or banged a cabinet or laughed too suddenly behind her, the freeze might not ask permission and might not let go, and she could freeze him to death for the crime of saying good morning. She stood in the dark kitchen a long while, staring at the mug.

* * *

The mug had survived the night, and the next afternoon Ahdia came back for it, rested and reasonably caffeinated and ready to negotiate.

“Freeze,” she whispered at it.

The mug declined.

She squared her shoulders and worked down the full menu. “Stop. Pause. Time out. Za Warudo!”

The last one honestly felt the most promising, since her only working reference for stopped time was an anime villain, and the universe had already shown a taste for her references. The mug sat there. She tried the Force-choke hand next, fingers clawed at empty air, elbow cocked, and held the pose long enough to catch her reflection in the microwave door and feel as stupid as she looked.

Under the old bullet wound the Seed hummed along, a cold ember, awake and interested and useless. Beneath the hum ran the instinct that had ridden with her since the docks: this costs you. She didn’t know what it took from her; she only knew the feel of something running somewhere out of sight.

She dumped yesterday’s coffee and brewed a fresh cup, reasoning that if she meant to spend the afternoon threatening a beverage, the beverage should be worth drinking. Then she threatened it for another hour. Commands. Gestures. Meaningful eye contact. Nothing worked, and the not-working stacked up in her until it hit the floor of her patience and went through.

“Come ON!” Her palm cracked down on the counter—

—and the world stopped.

The steam over the fresh cup hung mid-curl, a white ribbon parked in the air. The stove clock said 2:47 PM and went on saying it. The refrigerator hum was gone, and the silence where it had lived had a texture, thick and total. Dust hung in the window light and did not fall.

“Oh,” she said.

Nothing on Earth moved except her.

“Oh shit.”

She tried to un-slam the counter. She tried the commands again in reverse order, then in a calm indoor voice, then in the wheedling tone she saved for dying electronics. The freeze ignored all of it as the mug had ignored her. Panic arrived punctually, because the first freeze, at the docks, had ended on its own timetable, like weather, and she’d had no vote. This one might never end at all: the steam might never land, and the clock might read 2:47 PM for the rest of a very long life, hers, alone in a world-sized diorama.

The panic crested, broke, and rolled back out, and it dragged the anger with it, and for one hollow second she was empty of everything but tired.

Time snapped back, the steam finished its curl, and the refrigerator resumed its low argument with itself. The clock still said 2:47 PM. The entire terror had cost the world nothing.

She stood very still and did the math, because math was a thing you could hold. Mad pulled the trigger. Calm released it, or whatever hollowed-out cousin of calm she had fallen into.

She practiced until midnight. Getting angry on purpose turned out to be the easy half, since she owned years of material; letting go on purpose was the work. The holds ran five to ten seconds, seventeen freezes by the end of it, each release slightly less of an accident, and when she quit she felt scraped hollow, and the cold spot under the old wound ached like a pulled tooth.

* * *

The theory arrived with the next morning’s coffee, fully formed: buy time by spending time. If frozen seconds cost the world nothing, then frozen time was free practice space, a private gym the size of the universe, and she was apparently the only member.

By midmorning she could hold past a minute, and shortly after that she got scientific about it. She froze the kitchen and lobbed a pillow across it, and inside the stillness the pillow flew and flopped as pillows do, obeying her private physics without complaint. When she released, nothing dramatic happened at all; the pillow was somewhere new, with no history of having traveled.

Then the variation. She threw the next one hard and let the freeze go while it was still mid-flight.

The pillow crossed the living room like it had been fired out of something military, cracked into the wall, and brought the framed photo down with it in a burst of glass.

“Oh shit!” She was already moving toward the wreckage. “Yes!”

Both reactions were true, and neither one canceled the other. She swept up the glass and logged the finding underneath it: whatever went in gentle came out enormous. A fingertip push inside the freeze arrived, in real time, as a shove from something far bigger than her. She spent the afternoon calibrating on furniture that couldn’t sue, cushions and cereal boxes and one long-suffering ottoman, until she could dose it, tap to thump to launch.

She worked it out by the clocks: an hour inside ran to three minutes outside, which she wrote down and declined to understand. By day’s end she had banked something near six frozen hours, she could drop into the stillness at will and hold it for minutes at a stretch, and the cold under the old wound had crossed the border from temperature into pain. Her sleeve cuffs had gone soft and pale too, the fibers furred like rope just before it parts, and she could not name a thing she had done to them. She filed it under later and kept going. The meter she couldn’t see ran on.

* * *

What she did with the stillness next was go visiting.

She told herself it was reconnaissance, which was true, and that it was for their sake, which was also true, and that it had nothing to do with wanting to stand inside other people’s lives for a while, which made her two for three.

Ruth first, obviously, because Ruth was the only one of them she actually knew. The lobby guard was frozen mid-yawn when she walked in, a yawn wide enough to count fillings by, and she went past it at her leisure. Upstairs, the apartment door was unlocked. The woman fought crime on a volunteer basis and did not lock her front door, and Ahdia stood in the hallway a moment feeling offended on her behalf. Inside: The New England Journal of Medicine on the nightstand, bristling with sticky notes, a trauma surgery text underneath it. Tactical gear hung behind the winter coats, squared away with terrible neatness. The fridge wore photographs of Ruth and Firas, years of them, and Ahdia lifted one free, held it, and set it back precisely where it had been, corner to corner, and did that until her hands were steady and nobody living could have said she’d come through.

The tiny studio across town had its fire-escape window cracked two inches, so she went in that way. Its tenant was asleep on the futon in her work clothes. The pharmacy bottle on the bathroom sink read WHITFORD, TESS, and under the name, Sertraline 100mg. Ahdia knew that one; it hadn’t done much for her either. Another depressed girl making rent, sleeping where she landed. Recognition, not pity. She eased the window back to its two inches on her way out.

The community center said “nonprofit” from every surface, in the dialect that translates to “we’re one grant away from closing.” She came in past a rubber doorstop wedged for the evening crowd, past a janitor frozen mid-mop, past flyers for a food drive and ESL classes taped up at heights suggesting the ladder had been optional. The donation jar by the front desk held six dollars, all of it coins. In the back office a framed wedding photo stood on the desk, and the woman’s face in it had been scratched out, gouged deep enough to mark the glass. Ahdia didn’t touch that one; she left it where it lived and went back out through the propped door.

The last apartment belonged to a barista, per the name tag on the dresser: the Bean Post, then LEAH, then a smiley face added in Sharpie. The helmet on the wall hook was stickered over every inch and carried a star for jammer, a fact Ahdia possessed for one reason: that roller derby movie, 3 AM, a box of Thin Mints, no witnesses. A punching bag hung in the corner, held together with duct tape and spite. On the shelf sat anger-management books, and under them workbooks with actual handwriting in the margins, page after page of somebody doing the assignment anyway.

She froze the room, set two fingers against the bag, pushed like you’d test a door, and released from across the apartment. The bag went off. It swung like it had taken a full baseball swing, the chain snapped taut, and a ceiling bolt bent out of true.

“Oops,” she told the empty apartment.

By the end of the second day she could hold a freeze for thirty minutes, come and go without leaving a fingerprint of herself anywhere, and dose force from a nudge to a wrecking ball. She carried out one other finding, the kind she hadn’t gone looking for. Sticky notes, six dollars in coins, duct tape, handwriting in a workbook: none of these people were special because of what their bodies could suddenly do. They were special because they kept showing up.

* * *

Two nights after the warehouse, she learned what the practice had been for.

The Frederick Douglass Bridge, from her side of it: floodlights, a helicopter hammering the air white, and Gloom Girl up on the rail with the whole department closing in. Then she was off the rail, one middle finger raised on the way down.

Ahdia froze the night with her still in the air above the river.

She was most of the bridge away when it happened, so she sprinted the deck flat out in the stillness, hooked one arm through the cables, and hauled a frozen, folded woman in out of the dark, which mostly took nerve. The carry was the education: four blocks inland with a grown adult over her shoulders, then up a fire stair toward a roof where Ruth stood fixed at the parapet, binoculars up. Halfway up the climb her lungs went from burning to bargaining, and her body remembered the stairwell, the trick with the heart, how narrowly that trick had missed being her last idea. Wrong dial. She didn’t need the whole engine slowed, only the burn. So she put her attention into her own screaming legs as she’d put two fingers on a punching bag, and asked time to run cold there, and only there.

“Oh,” she said.

The burn in her legs folded up obediently and went quiet mid-stride.

“Oh that’s way better.”

She topped the climb sweating through everything she wore, because apparently effort still had to go somewhere no matter what the muscles agreed to.

“For such cosmic power,” she said, “it sure is a sweaty one.”

She left Gloom Girl hanging a foot above the rooftop, feet down, arms arranged, close enough that Ruth’s binoculars would be beside the point. Then she crossed three rooftops and let the night go. Somewhere behind her a girl dropped out of an empty sky, unhurt, and the story would assemble itself without help from anyone. Teleportation. The vanishing girl. Let them have it.

A low whistle brought the conference room back, and the whistle was Mack’s.

“She figured out selective time dilation. Her own muscles. Mid-climb.”

Ahdia’s hands had found the water bottle without consulting her. “Is that . . . hard to do?”

“FAERIS,” Bourn said. “Previous Temporalist development timeline for selective biological manipulation.”

“Average development time: 12 to 47 years post-bonding,” the drone reported.

“You did it in under an hour,” Bourn told her. She let the number finish landing before she went on. “That’s what made us decide you were worth recruiting. You problem-solve with cosmic abilities the way most people problem-solve with everyday tools.”

It was a compliment shaped like a lab result, and Ahdia filed it away to have feelings about later.

The display split into three feeds, three gray miniatures of one long night.

“We had eyes on all three ambush sites,” Bourn said. She nodded at the feeds in turn. “Ruth on the Frederick Douglass Bridge. Battlea and Sable at the construction site in Little Poland. Gloom Girl and Night Knight near the waterfront.”

In one feed a police dog hung mid-lunge and then was elsewhere, aimed at nothing, while a small gray Ahdia leaned her weight into other people’s punches half a second before they landed. In the second she watched herself gather Gloom Girl out of the air like fruit off a high branch. In the third, Night Knight left a rooftop at a doomed angle, and a hand closed around his ankle in the stillness and moved him two degrees.

“Coordinated perfectly,” Mack said, and he wasn’t praising anyone. “Someone fed Chief Whitford their locations. All three teams kettled at the same time.”

“Then you showed up,” Bourn resumed.

“Three simultaneous interventions within twenty minutes,” she added. On the middle feed the river hung in gray pleats below the bridge. “Ensuring no one died—police included.”

Mack turned to the drone. “FAERIS,” he said. “Total power usage duration for police ambush intervention.”

“Six hours continuous temporal manipulation across multiple sites,” the drone answered. It paused, for effect or for math. “Force amplification applied 47 times. Life force expenditure: 23% of total reserves.”

The blood left her face and took its time coming back.

“Twenty-three percent? In one night?”

Nobody in the room softened it for her, and she sat back slowly until the chair took her weight.

“I wanted them to be okay. I wanted them to think they could do this without me.”

“You gave them confidence,” Mack offered. “Made them believe in themselves.”

“While exhausting yourself,” Bourn said.

The display had moved on to an alley, where a small gray figure slid down a wall between dumpsters, folded up, and cried into her sleeve while the timestamp in the corner kept counting anyway.

“Ten minutes before you could stand,” Mack murmured. “You limped home. Took eight ibuprofen. Passed out on the couch.”

“And woke up the next day to do it again,” Bourn finished.

The feed changed again and gave her the Academy, the day after: the whole floor stopped at midmorning, Firas caught mid-sentence beside a mat, Victor frozen nodding along. A small gray Ahdia leaned in and stuck her tongue out at her brother, the full childhood version. Then the figure stumbled. A hand went flat against her chest, and even in grayscale the color went out of her.

In the conference room the gray light lay across her hands, and Ahdia leaned forward into it.

“I could feel it. Like something was burning through me. Every time I used the powers, it got colder.”

“Colder?” Bourn asked.

“The Seed. In my chest. When I freeze time, it’s cold. Really cold. But after—when I let go—it feels like it’s taking something from me. Like I’m emptying out.”

“That’s when you realized,” Mack prompted.

“That the power wasn’t free,” Ahdia said. Her eyes stayed on the small gray figure. “That it was costing me. I didn’t know how much or what exactly, but I could feel it. Like . . . like running on a battery that doesn’t recharge.”

Bourn turned to the drone. “FAERIS, bio-sign analysis from that session.”

“Cumulative degradation detected across all power usage instances. Pattern consistent with permanent cellular depletion, not temporary fatigue.”

“Permanent,” Ahdia repeated.

On the display, the gray Ahdia sat down on the Academy floor and stayed there a while. Then she stood, looked for a long moment at her brother, still frozen in the middle of whatever he was telling Victor, unfroze the room, and walked away before he could turn around.

* * *

The display went dark. For the first time since the roof, nothing was playing anywhere, and the quiet had intentions.

“That was two days ago,” Bourn said. It didn’t sound like an accusation. That made it worse. “You figured out your powers were draining you. That you couldn’t keep using them without consequence. And then you hid behind a foam obstacle to stick your tongue out at your brother.”

“I was processing,” Ahdia managed.

“You were regressing,” Bourn corrected. There was no cruelty anywhere in it. “After everything you’d accomplished—saving your brother’s team twice, mastering abilities that should’ve taken decades, preventing multiple deaths—you heard what he really thought of you.”

Her hands, she noticed from a slight distance, were still shaking. She flattened them on the table and reached for the one question that outranked the rest.

“My parents,” she said. The words needed a running start. “You’ve been monitoring this thing—the Hyper Seed. Did they . . . was their work in Montana connected to this?”

“Your parents,” Bourn said, and for the first time she seemed to weigh each word before spending it, “were working with CADENS when they disappeared in Montana. Or rather, working adjacent to us. They were tracking reports of a Hyper Seed manifestation. We were monitoring from a distance.”

“They died looking for this thing? For the thing that’s inside me now?”

“They died protecting it,” Mack answered. “From people who wanted to use it. People like Kain.”

“The Montana excavation wasn’t an accident,” Bourn told her. “Your parents discovered the Seed’s location. Someone else did too. There was a confrontation. An explosion. By the time our team arrived, your parents were gone. The Seed was gone. We thought it was destroyed.”

“Until Kain had it,” Ahdia finished.

“Until Kain had it,” Bourn agreed.

The echo sat on the table between them, and Bourn collected it before it could cool. “We don’t know how he acquired it. But we’d been tracking him for months before the warehouse. When you bonded with it—when you became it—you inherited your parents’ legacy.”

“Lucky me.”

Bourn rose and crossed to the long interior window, the one that looked down into the well of the facility: levels of it descending, screens tracking storms and sinkholes and every other way a planet can go wrong, people moving between them with somewhere to be.

“Our analysis suggests you’re running on a finite energy source,” she said to the glass.

Then she turned around, and whatever was coming had enough mass that the room felt it before she spoke. Mack went still. The drone’s light held where it was, and the only sound left was the building keeping itself alive.

“The Hyper Seed bonding replaced approximately half your life force with . . . something else. Every time you use your powers, you’re draining what remains. At a survivable rate of expenditure—short freezes, small corrections, nothing heroic—you have maybe eighteen months. The ambush was not a survivable rate. That one night cost you a quarter of everything you had left. Four more like it and we are not having this conversation.”

“Eighteen months,” Ahdia said. The number came out flat, from somewhere far away.

“Unless we can find another way,” Mack put in. “CADENS has been studying Temporalists for decades. We have resources, research, ancient texts. Maybe there’s a way to slow the drain, or restore what’s been lost.”

Bourn sat. “But we need your cooperation. We need you to work with us, not hide from us. We need you to trust us.”

“Trust the shadow government that’s been spying on me?”

“Trust the organization that knew you were at critical energy expenditure and intervened before you killed yourself helping people who don’t even know you exist,” Bourn shot back. Then the edge went out of her, all at once. “We extracted you from that academy because if we’d waited any longer, you would’ve spent the rest of your life—however short—watching your brother mourn you while you hid in the shadows.”

The drone hummed at its shelf height while Ahdia looked at her hands until she could ask it.

“So what do you want from me?”

“First, we want you to stop killing yourself. Second, we want you to let us help you figure out how to survive this. Third—” Bourn let the third one stand in line. “Kain is still out there. Chief Whitford is still hunting your brother’s team. And you’re the only one who can help them without exposing what they really are.”

“No pressure,” Ahdia muttered.

“Exactly the right amount of pressure,” Bourn returned. “The question is: are you going to keep hiding and burning through your life force alone? Or are you going to work with us?”

“Work with you how?”

“Training. Research. Mission support.” Bourn stood. “Kain is escalating. Chief Whitford is escalating. We need eyes on both of them, and you’re the only asset who can operate in ways they can’t detect or counter. In exchange, we give you every resource we have to extend your life expectancy beyond eighteen months.”

“You want me to spy for you.”

“We want you to stop Kain from acquiring more artifacts. Stop Whitford from weaponizing the police against enhanced individuals. And let us study your capabilities so we can understand what’s coming.” Bourn opened one hand. “Your personal life—your brother, his team, whatever family drama you’re processing—that’s yours to handle or not handle. We don’t care. We care about preventing the next cataclysm.”

Mack leaned forward. “But we do need an answer. Are you in or not?”

“What happens if I say no?”

“We keep monitoring,” Bourn said, unhurried. “We intervene when you’re about to do something that gets you killed. We track Kain and Whitford without your help. And when whatever cosmic threat is coming finally arrives, we hope we’re prepared enough without a Temporalist on our side.” She let that settle before she spent the rest. “But you keep burning through your energy alone. And you die in eighteen months, give or take. Alone.”

There it was. Alone was not a threat you could aim at her; alone was the apartment she had lived in for years. She thought of the alley and the sleeve. She thought of the thirty-four flights she had climbed on ruined legs rather than spend one minute of herself on her own behalf, and of every person she had saved who would never know she existed, who would never get the chance to show up for her as they kept showing up for each other.

“I’m in,” she said.

Nobody moved. The drone’s light held on her, recording, presumably for some future footage session she would also get to sit through.

“But I have conditions.”

## Chapter 15

“What conditions?” Bourn asked.

Ahdia set the empty water bottle down and squared it to the table’s edge. That bought her two seconds, and two seconds was all the rehearsal she was going to get.

“You’ve been watching Caledonia. You know what it’s like down there. Police state. Whitford’s thugs beating people for existing while Black. Kain in bed with all of it. People are terrified.”

“We’re aware of the sociopolitical climate,” Bourn said, in the tone of a form letter.

“The Go Squad is the only thing giving them hope,” Ahdia continued. “Real people standing up to corruption. Not some government agency. Not some shadowy organization with acronyms. Just . . . people. Who decided they’d had enough.”

“They’ve become symbols.”

“Exactly. And if Caledonia finds out it was all me—that my brother’s team doesn’t actually have powers—that hope dies. They become frauds. The city goes back to accepting that nothing can change.”

Bourn considered that for a long moment, without any visible change of expression. “You want us to help you maintain a deception so a handful of parkour vigilantes can continue street-level operations against corrupt municipal authorities.”

“Yes.”

“And you believe this matters.”

“It matters to them. That’s enough.”

The pause ran long enough that Ahdia braced for the no.

“The sociopolitical stability of authoritarian regimes,” Bourn said, “has strategic implications. Populations under systematic oppression become . . . vulnerable to external influences. Maintaining indigenous resistance movements can prevent larger destabilization events.”

Ahdia waited for the rest of it. There was no rest of it. She had asked this woman to protect her brother’s hope, and the woman had agreed because hope was a stabilizing asset, and the agreement was somehow colder than a refusal would have been. “So . . . yes?”

“We’ll provide support,” Bourn said, and the terms came out in a list she had already drafted. “FAERIS surveillance of Go Squad operations. Real-time threat assessment. Translocation to intervention points. Extraction afterward. You provide tactical assistance during frozen time. They maintain belief in their capabilities. Caledonia maintains hope. We gather operational data on your power usage and limitations.”

“Everyone wins,” Mack added.

“Except I’m still dying in eighteen months.”

Mack looked into his coffee instead of at her. Nobody in the room offered a correction.

“Which brings us to our research division,” Bourn said, already up and moving for the door. “Follow me.”

* * *

The route ran along a railed walkway above the command floor, past the wall of live feeds with its storms and sinkholes and burning things in patient rotation. Side corridors branched off at intervals, every one of them labeled and every label an acronym. Ahdia watched the feeds slide by underneath her sneakers. “How many of these places are being watched?” Ahdia asked, keeping her eyes on the slow parade of disasters below.

“Currently? 247 active monitoring sites,” Bourn said, without slowing down or consulting anything. “Down from 890 during the Cold War. Cataclysm activity has decreased since the 1960s.”

“Why?”

“Unknown. Working theory: dimensional barriers strengthened after atmospheric nuclear testing stopped. Or testing weakened barriers and they’re slowly recovering. Or complete coincidence. We don’t have enough data.”

She offered all three theories with identical investment, and Ahdia was beginning to understand that Bourn kept her beliefs like other people kept spare batteries, interchangeable and in a drawer.

The walkway ended at a heavy door stenciled in yellow:

RESEARCH DIVISION - AUTHORIZED PERSONNEL ONLY

Beyond it the facility changed dialect: whiteboards instead of status boards, cables duct-taped across the floor in bundles, equipment she couldn’t name crowded against equipment she couldn’t guess at. In the middle of it all, a man in a wrinkled button-down was wheeling his chair between two monitors, loudly arguing both sides. “—can’t be right, the temporal variance should be inverse not proportional unless the field radius affects decay rate which would mean—”

He looked up, saw Bourn, and rode the chair into a filing cabinet. “Director! I didn’t—sorry, I was just—the readings from the warehouse event were—”

“Dr. Ryu,” Bourn said. “This is Ahdia Bacchus. Our Temporalist.”

He came upright fast enough to knock his glasses crooked. Japanese and somewhere in his late twenties, her height, wearing the look of a man thirty hours past his last sleep and running on enthusiasm instead.

“Yes! I know! I mean, I’ve been studying your—not YOU, obviously, that would be—I mean the data from your power usage, which is fascinating, not that you’re just data, you’re a person, obviously a person, a very—” He ran out of runway and pushed the glasses back up his nose. “Sorry. I talk too much when I’m excited. Also when I’m nervous. Also in general.”

“Relax. I understood maybe forty percent of that, which is my baseline for most conversations.”

“Forty percent? That’s actually above average! Most people stop me at ten. My last roommate used to throw pillows at me when I—” Bourn’s attention shifted onto him by a single degree, which was apparently enough. “Sorry. Doing it again.”

“Dr. Ryu will be your handler,” Bourn said. “He manages FAERIS operations and will serve as your tactical support during missions.”

“I’m your guy in the chair,” Ryu said. “That’s a Spider-Man reference. You probably don’t—”

“I know,” Ahdia said. “I’ve seen Homecoming like six times.”

“Seven for me. Well, eight if you count the time I fell asleep and had to rewatch—” He caught the Director’s stillness at the edge of his vision. “Right. Professional. I can be professional.”

“Dr. Ryu has been monitoring your power usage since the warehouse incident. He’ll brief you on what we know, what we don’t know, and begin baseline testing to understand your capabilities and limitations.”

“I’ve prepared a presentation,” Ryu said, rallying. “It’s only forty-seven slides. I cut it down from ninety.”

“We’ll skip the presentation,” Bourn said, already turning to go. “Run the physical assessments first. I want baseline biometrics before she uses powers again.”

“Right. Yes. Testing. I can do testing.”

Bourn was gone with a nod to Ryu, and Mack followed her out, giving Ahdia a look on the way that might have been sympathy or might have been a warning about the slides. The lab door sealed behind them, and just that fast the entire federal apparatus shrank down to one over-caffeinated scientist bouncing slightly on his heels. “Medical bay is this way.”

* * *

The medical bay was white and quiet, and the machine in the middle of it looked like a dentist’s chair and a tanning bed had pooled their ambitions. Screens ringed it on articulated arms. Ryu moved between them, waking each one with a tap.

“Okay,” Ryu said, cracking his knuckles at the console. “So. Baseline biometrics. We need to understand what the Hyper Seed bonding did to your physiology before we can figure out how to slow the degradation. Or stop it. Ideally stop it.” He patted the chair hopefully. “If you could just—”

“What exactly are you looking for?”

“Everything?” He spread his hands to indicate the size of everything. “Heart rate, blood pressure, cellular composition, temporal field resonance, dimensional signature, neurological patterns—basically if it can be measured, I’m measuring it.”

“That sounds deeply invasive.”

“It’s non-invasive! Completely! The scanners are passive. You just sit there. Well, lie there. It works better if you lie flat.”

A changing room hid behind a curtain in the corner, gray scrubs folded on its shelf in a stack of guessed sizes that included hers. She changed and came back and lay down on the not-quite-dentist-chair, which reclined beneath her with a hum.

“There we go. Comfortable?”

“Not remotely.”

“Great! That’s—I mean, sorry. Do you need a pillow or—”

“I’m fine. Let’s just get this over with.”

The scanner arm made its first slow pass, and data climbed the screens while Ryu leaned into them, fingers drumming the edge of the console as he read. “Okay,” Ryu muttered, half to the screens. “Heart rate elevated but that’s probably stress from translocation. Blood pressure . . . hmm. Cellular degradation markers are . . . oh.” The drumming stopped.

“‘Oh’ what?”

“Sorry. Just—the Seed integration is more thorough than I expected. It’s not just sitting in your chest like a foreign object. It’s connected to your cardiovascular system, your nervous system, your skeletal structure. It’s . . .” He turned one of the screens so she could see it. Her body rendered in gray sections with fine bright threads running through the gray. They followed her ribs and wrapped the long bones of her arms. They gathered at the center of her chest, thick around a point of light.

“It’s replacing parts of you. Slowly.”

She looked at the threads.

“Replacing me with what?”

“With itself.” He kept his eyes on the screen. “Every time you use your powers, it spreads a little more. Converts your biological tissue into something else. Something that can channel temporal energy.”

“So I’m turning into a Hyper Seed.”

“Essentially. The process is slow—eighteen months at current usage rates before you’re more Seed than human. But . . .” He stopped. His hands lay flat on the console now, holding still on purpose. “The tissue it converts isn’t dead. It’s still you. It still functions. It’s just . . . different.”

“Can you stop it?”

“I don’t know. Maybe? CADENS has been studying Temporalists for decades, but most of them died before we could collect meaningful data. You’re the first one we’ve had access to this early in the bonding process.”

She watched the threads. He watched her.

“But I’m going to try. That’s what all this is for—understanding the mechanism so we can intervene.”

“And if you can’t?”

“Then we make the eighteen months count.”

The scanner arm made its second pass. She lay flat and let it read her.

* * *

The full battery took another hour. She was back in her own clothes, sitting on the edge of the chair, by the time he had finished making the screens agree with each other.

“Okay,” he said, wheeling his stool around to face her. “Good news and bad news. Good news: your baseline physiology is adapting remarkably well to the Seed integration. Your body isn’t rejecting it, which is—well, you’re alive, so obviously it’s not rejecting it, but I mean at the cellular level there’s no immune response, no inflammation, no—sorry. Rambling.”

“And the bad news?”

“The temporal field generation is inefficient. You’re burning way more energy than you need to for the same effect. It’s like—” He looked around the medical bay for an analogy and located one somewhere behind her. “Like running a car engine at maximum RPM to go thirty miles per hour. You’re getting where you need to go, but you’re destroying the engine in the process.”

“Can you fix it?”

“Maybe. If we can teach you to control the field more precisely. Right now you’re doing everything by instinct—which is impressive, don’t get me wrong—but instinct isn’t efficient. It’s like the difference between flailing in water and actually swimming. Both keep you afloat, but one uses way less energy.”

Ahdia thought about the fire stair and the trick with her own burning legs, and kept the thought where it was.

“I want to run some controlled tests. See if we can refine your technique. But first—” He was already moving, waving her up and out into the corridor, talking happily the whole way about interface design and video game HUDs while she let it wash over her like weather. Two doors down he badged them into a long room lined with racks. “We need to get you equipped.”

* * *

The armory ran deep, racks down both sides and a firing range opening off the far end. Front and center, on a mannequin, stood a suit: matte black from collar to boot, armor plates laid over flexible fabric in overlapping segments. It had very clearly been waiting for her. “CADENS is really going all-in on the superhero aesthetic, huh?” Ahdia said.

“It’s designed specifically for you. Well, for Temporalists. The fabric is—” He stopped, upgraded his plan, and crossed to a rack drawer. “Here, let me show you something. Cover your ears.”

“What?”

He came back holding a handgun. “Trust me. Cover your ears.”

She got her hands up just before he shot the mannequin in the sternum. The report slammed around the racks, a spark jumped off the plate, and the round went whining away into the range. The mannequin rocked on its stand and settled, and the suit carried no mark that mattered. Her ears rang anyway.

“Bulletproof,” Ryu said, setting the gun down gingerly with both hands, a man returning a borrowed thing. “The armor plates, anyway. The fabric itself is impact-resistant but not bullet-stopping. It will, however, handle the temporal shear when you freeze time. Normal clothes deteriorate—fibers can’t handle the stress. This can.” He peeled the suit off the mannequin and held it out. “Temperature regulation, sensor-transparent for our monitoring equipment, and surprisingly light. Try it on.”

“Changing room?”

“Same one as before.”

The suit fit her. Every measurement was right, down to the length of her arms and the width of her shoulders, and nobody at CADENS had ever taken a tape to her, which meant they had extrapolated her dimensions from surveillance footage. She filed that under efficient and creepy in that order, and walked back out to the armory.

“Okay,” he said. He said it to a point slightly to the left of her, then found something urgent to check at a console. When he came back he was carrying a visor, holding it out with both hands. “This is your HUD. Augmented reality display. Put it on.”

She put it on. The armory grew a second layer: range markers and air temperature, a compass ribbon, small patient readouts attaching themselves to everything she looked at. “Whoa.”

“Right?” He bounced once on his heels. “I designed the interface. Video game HUDs, like I said. You can track FAERIS-monitored individuals, see threat assessments, magnify your vision up to 20x, access tactical data during operations. It’s all gesture and eye-tracking controlled. Very intuitive.” He tapped his own temple. “It’s also field-hardened like the suit—ordinary electronics stop dead inside your bubble, which is how I ended up watching you climb thirty-four floors instead of taking the elevator.”

She looked at him. The display obligingly tagged him:

[DR. RYU MATSUDA - CADENS PERSONNEL - CLEARANCE LEVEL 7 - HEART RATE: 82 BPM]

When she had first put the visor on, the last line had read 78. “I can see your heart rate went up.”

“Yeah, well, I get excited about tech.”

“I’m also detecting,” Ahdia said, “elevated levels of awkwardness. It’s over 9,000.”

“Did you just—did you make a Dragon Ball Z reference?”

“I contain multitudes.”

He looked like a man whose birthday had arrived early.

“Also I watched a lot of anime at 4 AM when sleep wouldn’t come.”

“Okay, I’m raising your clearance level.”

She raised an eyebrow at him.

“Just kidding, I don’t have that authority. But we need to test the gear with your powers. The display should stay functional even in frozen time. I’m also going to test something experimental—the HUD’s AR communication protocol. Step into the testing circle.”

The testing circle turned out to be a ring of yellow paint on the floor near the range, and she stepped into it.

“Now freeze time,” Ryu said, backing up two steps and straightening his glasses. “And don’t freak out when you see me.”

She froze it. The room’s hum cut out, and Ryu stopped mid-fidget with one hand still at his glasses. Then light gathered itself beside the console, and a second Ryu stood there, edges faintly luminous, grinning at her through the stillness.

“If you can see this, wave your hand,” AR-Ryu said.

She waved.

“Excellent! Okay, this is going to sound weird, but I’m not actually me right now. I’m a cybernetic mapping of my brain running through the HUD’s processors. The system operates on picosecond framerates, so it can function even in your temporal field. I can talk to you, answer questions, provide tactical support—and when you unfreeze, everything we discuss gets uploaded to real-me’s brain instantly. He’ll—I’ll—remember this conversation like it just happened. Which, from my perspective, it did. Or will. Time travel grammar is confusing.”

“This is deeply weird.”

“Oh absolutely. But functional! Try asking me something only I would know.”

She glanced at the real Ryu fixed mid-adjustment, then back at the glowing one. “What show did I reference earlier?”

“Dragon Ball Z. Over 9,000 joke. See? I have all of real-Ryu’s memories up to the moment you froze time.”

She looked at it a moment longer. It stood there glowing, pleased with itself. “Okay, you can unfreeze now. Watch what happens.”

She let the stillness go. The hum returned, and the real Ryu finished adjusting his glasses and then caught up with himself all at once. “Did the AR work? Did it—oh, you asked about Dragon Ball Z. Yeah, I remember that now. It’s like I was there, but also I wasn’t? This tech is so cool.”

“That,” Ahdia said, “is the weirdest thing that’s happened to me. And I have a cosmic artifact in my chest.”

“Fair. But it means you’ll never be alone during operations. I’ll always be there. Sort of.” He checked the console readouts, nodding at each in turn. “Everything else maintained integrity too—suit, HUD, communication. You could hold a freeze for hours and we could have full conversations.”

He looked up from the console, straight on. “How does it feel?”

“I feel like I’m either cosplaying,” she said, “or I look like I’m about to brood on a rooftop.”

“You’re equipped,” Ryu corrected. “There’s a difference.”

“Does it have to be black?”

“Just for dinner,” he replied, without looking up from the console, and it sat there being a joke or possibly not, and she decided not to ask.

He strapped a heavy watch onto her wrist next, cinching the band with tech-support briskness. “This is your panic button. If something goes wrong—if you’re injured, if you’re stuck, if you just need immediate extraction—you press this and the nearest FAERIS unit will paint you for translocation. Emergency protocol. Bourn authorized it specifically for you.”

“Anything else?”

“One more thing. It’s—you don’t have to take it if you don’t want to. But . . .” He took a flat case from the end of the rack and held it a moment before he offered it. “For anonymity. I know you’re invisible during operations, but in case someone sees you before you freeze time, or after you unfreeze—”

She opened it. “A mask.”

“A mask,” he confirmed. “I know it’s very comic book, but—”

“I’ll take it.”

It was a black domino, light as paper and shaped for the top half of a face. She put it on and turned to the mirror at the end of the racks. “I look ridiculous,” she said.

“You look dangerous,” Ryu corrected. “In a good way. In a ‘don’t mess with me because I can stop time and you’ll never see it coming’ way.”

The woman in the mirror stood straight, armored and masked and built for a job. She looked capable. She looked above all like someone who wasn’t Ahdia, and Ahdia had no complaints about the casting. “What now?” she asked.

“Now,” Ryu said, “we test your temporal control. See if we can make you more efficient. Buy you more than eighteen months.”

* * *

The training room was mostly empty space under a high ceiling: padded floor, a pendulum hanging on a long cable from a gantry, and a ball suspended from a rig beside a wall display. Ryu set the pendulum swinging and set the wall timer running, red digits climbing. “Okay,” Ryu said, walking backward toward the console. “I want you to freeze time, but this time, try to feel what you’re doing. Where the energy is coming from, where it’s going. The more aware you are of the mechanism, the more control you’ll have.”

She reached for the cold. The pendulum halted mid-arc. The ball hung dead on its cable. The timer held at 00:03.87 and declined to move again, and for once she stood inside the stillness and paid attention to it instead of spending it: the cold flowing outward from the center of her, the far edge of the field somewhere beyond the walls and wastefully wide. She let go.

“Good,” he said, eyes on his tablet. “Now try to shrink the field. Make it smaller. Just the room instead of the whole facility.”

She froze it again. This time she went hunting for the edge. It was there, as the burn in her legs had been on the fire stair, a thing she could set her attention against and move. She hauled it inward until it sat just outside the walls and released.

“Perfect!” The tablet came up like a trophy. “You just reduced energy expenditure by thirty percent. Try it again, even smaller.”

She did it again. The edge drew in closer, snug against the room, and now she could feel the whole shape of the thing, a bubble she wore instead of a weather system she dragged. “I felt it,” she said. “I could feel the shape of the field. Like it was part of me.”

“You just cut your energy usage in half. If you can maintain that level of control during field operations, you’ve bought yourself another year. Maybe more.”

Her legs were shaking, and she sat down on the padded floor before they could decide the matter for her. Another year. She turned the number over, looking for the catch. “Really?”

“Really.” He crouched to her level, tablet against his knees. “You’re a remarkably fast learner. Most Temporalists took years to achieve this level of control. You did it in an hour.”

“I watch a lot of training montages.”

“Well, it’s working.” He stood. She got herself up in stages. “You should rest. Tomorrow we’ll start coordinating with FAERIS, practicing translocation protocols, running through intervention scenarios. But for now—you’ve earned a break.”

“Where am I supposed to sleep?”

“Right. Quarters.” He led her out into a corridor where the lights had gone over to a dimmer cycle, evening by facility decree. “Basic but functional. Probably better than the penthouse you’ve been hiding in for months.”

“Hey. My penthouse has excellent TV.”

“Our quarters have TV too. Also running water, a bed that’s not a couch, and food that didn’t come from a gas station.”

“Sold.”

They walked. Somewhere below them the command floor went on tracking every wrong thing on the planet, while up here it was quiet enough to hear the air system breathe.

“Oh, almost forgot.” He stopped and dug in a pocket and came out with a small flat case.

“What’s this?”

She opened it. Inside was a silver crest—a shield with a crack down its center—CADENS embossed across the top, and below it a single word: AUERBACH

“Your codename. If you choose to accept it.” She looked at the word. “Everyone operational gets one—Howitzer, Greyhound, Mercury. Director Bourn picked yours herself. But if you want something else—”

“Auerbach,” she said. The word meant nothing she could trace. It was not an acronym she could unpack, or a joke she could catch. Bourn had chosen it, and Bourn explained nothing, and somehow that made it easier to hold. It was a name with no history attached. She could be the history herself.

“You don’t have to use it,” Ryu said. “We can come up with something else. Chronos, Tempus, um . . . Freeze Frame? That one’s terrible, sorry—”

“No.” She closed the case and kept it in her hand. “Auerbach is fine. It’s . . . it’s good.”

“Yeah?”

“Yeah.”

The corridor ended at a door stenciled TEMPORARY QUARTERS 7.

“This is you,” he said, opening the door with a tap of his badge. “Door locks from inside, bathroom has toiletries, there’s a tablet on the desk with facility maps and protocols. If you need anything, the comm system is—” He caught himself. “Actually, you probably just want to sleep.”

“Desperately.”

“Right. Sleep. Good. I’ll—I’ll let you do that.” He got three steps down the corridor and turned around. “Hey. For what it’s worth? You’re doing really well. I know this is all overwhelming—new powers, new organization, dying slowly—but you’re handling it better than I would.”

“You don’t know how I’m handling it.”

“I’ve been watching the surveillance footage,” he said. “I know how you were handling it before. This is better.”

Then he was gone around the corner before she could build an answer. She stood in the doorway a moment, holding the case and not building one.

The room was small and clean and belonged to no one, and it held a bed, a desk, a chair, and a lock she controlled. She sat down on the bed, and the bed was softer than her couch, a finding she was uniquely qualified to certify. She set the case on the nightstand where she would see it first.

The day had held one deal, one handler, one suit, one mask, one panic button, one codename, thirty percent and then half, another year and maybe more. She had spent the whole day doing something, and the something was slowly turning her into a cosmic artifact, but it was doing, and doing was new.

Small victories. She was asleep before the lights finished dimming.

## Chapter 16

The movies had promised her a “high-tech command center,” and the movies had lied again. Real surveillance had no soundtrack and no quick cuts, and nobody helpfully leaned in to say enhance. It had an elevator ride down that lasted long enough to pop her ears twice, twelve-hour shifts, and chairs built by somebody who actively hated the human back. On paper, the whole facility was a “government office building that happened to have alien technology in the basement.” The paper seriously undersold the basement.

Days blurred down there until she quit tracking which was which. The Academy feeds ran in night-vision green from four angles, and she watched her brother’s team for entire shifts, as she used to watch television. Except that now she was also the network.

The Seed kept its cold under the old wound, the Hyper Seed, Power Extempore, whichever name the files were using that day. It never warmed. She had stopped waiting for it to.

Ryu held down the next chair, fingers drumming between commands. His badge hung crookedly from his shirt pocket: “Dr. Ryu Matsuda - FAERIS Operations.” He was the only person in the building who could interface with FAERIS without the drones going haywire, neurodivergent pattern recognition shaking hands with fourth-dimensional tech, which the rest of the staff treated as a miracle and Ryu treated as a Tuesday. She got that. Her brain didn’t work like brains were supposed to either.

The week came with a deadline. CCPD paperwork sat open on the far screen: a raid on a community center in the Old Caledonia district, filed under “probable cause,” which in Caledonia translated to “we don’t like the people who gather here.” The squad already patrolled three blocks from it most nights, on Ruth’s idea that the smart place to walk was wherever the police were hunting. So the bunker had two jobs. Teach the squad its triggers, and be ready when the paperwork grew boots.

* * *

Tess went first, and Tess failed.

The bridge story had hardened into squad canon by then: she had “teleported.” Off the rail, into thin air, out of an empty sky four blocks inland. On the feeds she spent two nights trying to do it again. She jumped off the vault with her eyes shut and held her breath until she wobbled. She clicked her heels three times, the full Dorothy method, no ruby, no Toto, and landed squarely in Kansas. The heels worked strictly as shoes.

Nothing worked, because nothing could work while her trigger sat unchosen in a bunker under a government office building, between two people with a checklist, and the flashiest power was getting the most care. Ahdia watched her drop off the climbing wall and stalk away, done with the universe, and felt the full Oz of it. She was the wizard. She was also the man behind the curtain. She was also, per the biometrics, paying for every ticket in the house.

* * *

Ben was easier. Ben already had a habit.

On the feed he reset into his athletic crouch, fixed on a point above the rafters, and jumped for a ceiling he had no business reaching. He had been doing it for days, and Ryu pulled the clip and let the crouch loop endlessly.

“That one?”

She nodded; it was simple, repeatable, already his.

On Ben’s next attempt she reached for the cold and put a bubble around him. A dilation, not a freeze: time ran slow and thick in a sleeve of air with Ben inside it, and FAERIS leaned into the sleeve and amplified. Three feet became thirty. He rose past the rafters like a man on wires nobody had rigged and came down whooping on the high mats.

“That actually worked.”

“You create the time bubble,” Ryu said, already making notes. “FAERIS amplifies within it. Symbiotic.”

“This is much better than what I thought I’d have to do.”

“Which was?”

“Much sweatier.”

Nights later the payoff arrived at the community center. Ben came through a second-story window into a room of drawn weapons, landed in the middle of the cops, and scattered them like pins, then went up into rafters no flashlight ever thought to check.

In the bunker the pull arrived in her chest, low and slow. Her fingers gripped the armrest until it creaked. The armrest held, and so did she.

* * *

Leah tried comics logic first. She stood very still on the feeds and concentrated at a foam block, Jean Grey style, then tried running starts and cape physics and dramatic fists. The universe politely took a pass on all of it.

Then a sparring drill caught her wrong, an elbow found her ear, and the sound she made was older than any comic. It was a battle cry, rage under it and forward momentum behind it. Ahdia answered before she had properly decided to. The bubble took Leah mid-stride and velocity multiplied inside it, and the training dummy left its chain and met a wall. The gym went reverently quiet.

Ryu typed it into the log: “Vocalization plus forward momentum.”

At the raid, a cop swung his barrel toward the corner where the kids were. The cry reached him out of the dark first, and then Leah did, at freeway speed, shoulder first, so that the rifle went one way and the cop went the other.

A vein rose at Ahdia’s temple and kept its own time.

* * *

Victor took a full evening in the weight room, stacking plates, lifting what a strong man lifts and nothing more. Then he quit adding weight and stood a while working one hand, and the gesture he settled on came out of memory: fingers curled, wrist cocked, a grip shaped for rebar. He closed it on the loaded machine.

“That works,” Ryu said.

He typed: “Specific hand position plus grasping motion.”

The next grip tore the machine off its bolts, all six hundred pounds relocated like patio furniture. Victor stared respectfully at his own hand.

At the raid, that hand closed around a rifle barrel. The barrel folded like foil. The cop who owned it got lifted, carried, and set down gently on the gym mats, and behind the whole transaction an organizer sprinted a clear lane to the exit.

Sweat came up on her everywhere at once. She gritted her teeth and held. The bubbles held too.

* * *

Tess stayed unsolved until Ruth asked the right question. Everyone else had asked what she’d tried; Ruth asked what she had been doing the one time it worked.

Tess remembered the bridge, went still on the feed, and then, solemnly, raised her middle finger to the sky.

Ryu turned from the console and gave Ahdia the look he did with both eyebrows. She shrugged and reached for the cold, because the gesture was already the truest sentence Tess owned.

The mechanism was almost respectable. Inside the bubble, FAERIS read her iris and her focal depth, took the exact place she was looking as a destination, and folded the space between. Tess vanished off the mats and reappeared at the top of the climbing wall, screaming happily.

Ryu typed: “Gesture plus eye-tracking.”

At the community center the lead cop had the worst night of his career. Tess, unbound, appeared at his shoulder and was gone before he turned, arrived at his left elbow and left it, the finger raised first every time, a targeting ritual he could not have invented. By the time he understood anything he was zip-tied to a bench and she was three rooftops away.

The ladder topped out there. Warmth slid over Ahdia’s lip. Her fingers came away red.

* * *

Ryu was holding out a box of tissues, and he had also been holding her biometrics on his center screen for days without mentioning it.

“Your hemoglobin is dropping,” he said. “Temporal manipulation is causing microbleeds. Capillaries can’t handle the stress.”

“I’m fine.”

“You’re not.”

He put a graph up. The line went down and did not come back. “Each use accelerates breakdown. Your body is aging microseconds faster than it should. Compounds over time.”

She looked at the line instead of at him.

“How much time do I have?”

Ryu’s fingers stopped. He pulled the ledger up beside the biometrics, and for once he didn’t soften it with a reference. “You bought yourself a year in the training room. This week you spent it on them.”

“At this rate? Eighteen months baseline. Maybe two years if you’re conservative with usage. But . . .”

He let the rest of it stand there.

“Quality of life degrades faster than lifespan. You’ll feel it before you die from it.”

The tissue in her hand had stiffened. She folded it smaller.

“Can you fix it?”

“I’m not a doctor. I can monitor, warn you when you’re approaching critical thresholds. But stop it?” He shook his head. “Only you can do that. By not using the powers.”

“That’s not an option.”

“I know.” He watched the graph a moment longer. “I’ll keep monitoring. Tell you when to stop.”

“And if I don’t stop?”

His fingers went back to drumming the console edge. “Then I’ll document what happens. For science.”

* * *

Ruth got no trigger, because Ruth had no power. Everyone agreed on that, Ruth included: whatever the rest of them had caught, hers hadn’t manifested. The feeds kept disagreeing: on her callouts the team moved half a beat early, tighter than the drill, correct before the correction.

Ryu watched four sessions of it, then typed: “Leadership vocalizations plus team proximity.” He didn’t type a question mark, but she could hear it anyway.

Maybe FAERIS was treating Ruth’s commands as targeting data. Maybe Ruth was that good and always had been. Ahdia ran it both ways, got two clean answers, and left them both standing.

Firas was on the feeds too, every session, at the edge of the mats where the light thinned, present for all of it and part of none. He wore an expression she recognized from mirrors: somebody being left behind by the people he loved. She knew it intimately.

* * *

Assembled, the raid ran thirty seconds end to end.

Five cops came through the front doors at 2 AM with weapons drawn, on a room that still held kids and organizers from a program that should have ended hours before. Ruth stood a rooftop two blocks out and calmly called the play, FAERIS threading her voice into every earpiece half a breath ahead of need. Ben came through the window and the cry came out of the dark. The rifle folded. Tess was everywhere and nowhere, finger first. Ahdia held bubbles layered across the whole team at once, the expensive mode, fields stacked like panes of slow glass, and the cold poured out of her for the entire thirty seconds.

It played like a Hong Kong action movie somebody had shot in one long take. Nobody died. The five cops ended the night zip-tied in a neat row on the gym mats, breathing and humiliated. The civilians saw only blur and salvation. The squad was gone before the radios finished asking their questions.

In the bunker her nose had opened again. This time it did not close. Her hands shook against the console, and Ryu handed her tissues without a word and kept handing them.

He typed the mission log one-handed: “Full combat application successful. Team synchronization optimal. Power reliability: 100%.”

Then he opened the assessment file, and the screen sat angled so that she could read every line from her chair: “Subject demonstrates increasing physical deterioration. Continues operation despite clear signs of life force depletion. Pattern consistent with vindication-seeking behavior superseding self-preservation.”

He didn’t tell her to stop. He only documented. She filed that under kindness and left the rest of it alone.

* * *

Tess cut the footage that night in her apartment, hood up, lit by the laptop, a FAERIS feed watching her watch herself, and the post went up while Ahdia watched it go: “They said we couldn’t make a difference. They said Caledonia was lost. They were wrong. #GoSquad #CaledoniaHope #WhenNobodyElseWill”

The comments came in hundreds inside the hour. Then they came in thousands. Somewhere in the scroll a stranger asked why she kept risking it, and Tess typed back instantly: “People need to see this. They need to know someone’s fighting for them.”

#GoSquad trended locally by morning and regionally by the next night. And in other buildings, on other screens, people with titles ran the same videos frame by frame, closely, and took notes of their own.

* * *

By the end of the week, three beliefs were running at once. The squad believed in its powers. The city believed in its heroes. Her brother believed he wasn’t good enough to stand with either one. She had manufactured all three, and none of the customers knew the factory existed.

Then Ryu pulled up a new file, and it held mission parameters, a target, and a risk column long enough to need scrolling. Something big was coming, bigger than a community center, bigger than anything the squad had trained for. The Seed sat colder and heavier under the old wound, and the blood kept up its thin patient trickle at her lip.

They were going to be fine, she told herself.

On the screens the team was laughing at something Ben had done, loose and in sync, easier together than they had ever been before her. She watched them in night-vision green. The dread kept its seat through the whole celebration.

## Chapter 17

SETTING THE TRAP

The footage was on its fourteenth viewing, and Harding Kain read it like a quarterly report: from the back, hunting the one number that refused to reconcile. On the screen a figure went up the face of a parking structure and arrived at the roofline, thirty feet of sheer concrete in under two seconds. Halfway up, the image smeared, as if the camera itself had flinched. There. Every viewing, right there.

He cut into the lamb without looking down at it. The tray had come up at seven; the office smelled of rosemary and old leather. Forty floors below, the city glittered on services his companies billed it for monthly. His dinner rings clicked against the knife while he chewed, and the blur arrived again where it always arrived. “Run the scanning thing again,” he said.

Reed touched the console without comment, a young man who was expensive and precisely as useful as he cost, which in Kain’s experience made him rare. “Spectral analysis shows the same result, sir. Electromagnetic anomaly during the movement. Military-grade frequency signature.”

Military-grade. That phrase had a catalog price, and TRIOMF wrote the catalog. Kain set the knife down and gave the big signet a slow half turn on its finger.

The money under him ran three generations deep, and none of it had started in security. His grandfather pulled diamonds out of ground that fought him, and broke three strikes to keep pulling; the family albums called it grit. His father built the armored cars that moved the stones, and somewhere on those highway runs he noticed that the fear of losing a thing billed better, year over year, than the thing itself. Harding had taken that public—TRIOMF, the contracts, the candidacy. Monetize fear and call it safety. The stones were the blood, and the armor was only ever the arm. So the signature ruled out amateurs. That was the fourteenth viewing’s dividend: hobbyists did not smear on federal equipment. Somebody with resources had put capital into these people, and unaccounted capital was the only kind that had ever interested him.

The intercom announced Chief Whitford at half past eight, and Reed let himself out before being asked. Whitford came in with a folder held against his thigh. Kain appraised him across the desk: still a serviceable asset, but depreciating, the face above the uniform worn well past its book value. “You look troubled, John.”

“Read it.” The folder landed on the blotter, its CLASSIFIED faded from red to pink, which meant copies of copies and years of hands.

Kain did not touch it. He laced his fingers over his waistcoat and waited like a man at the top of a backswing. “Summarize.”

Whitford’s jaw tightened, and then he recited. “Tech signatures. Military-grade surveillance drones detected during seventeen separate Go Squad operations over the past six weeks.”

He turned a page he knew by heart. “My people were right. Someone’s watching them. Federal-level equipment.”

“FBI? CIA?”

“Neither.”

Kain looked up from the screen for the first time since the chief had come in. “Then who the hell are they?”

“CADENS.” The name landed on nothing. Kain had spent thirty years mapping federal money down to the last classified dollar, and this one had never crossed his desk. Either irrelevant or terrifying. He kept his face shut and waited for the rest.

“Cataclysm Activity Detection and Engagement Network Sentry.”

“Explain.” The rings resumed their slow turn while Whitford found the page.

“Shadow group funded through Special Access Programs. Black budget. Completely autonomous operational authority. Only the Secretary of Defense has direct contact with their leadership, and even he can’t give them orders.”

Every budget had an owner, and every chart had a top box. “Someone can.”

“One person. Director. Codename: Overseer.”

A person above the Secretary of Defense, with no name and no budget line. Kain reached for a price. Nothing attached. The list of things he could not price was a short list, and he disliked every item on it.

“And how,” Kain asked, the rings going still on the desk, “did you acquire classified intelligence on a shadow government program that doesn’t officially exist?”

“Golf.”

“Excuse me?”

Whitford shrugged like the answer embarrassed him. “The Secretary of Defense and I golf regularly. Montclair Country Club, every third Thursday when he’s in town.”

Whitford reported it in the tone of a business expense. “He was less than pleased about learning that CADENS tech was being used to help vigilantes beat up local precincts.”

The signet stopped again on its finger. “He told you this.”

“He implied it. Heavily. After his third whiskey.”

That part Kain believed, having bought better information for less in the same clubhouse weather. Or the Secretary had wanted it carried. That was likelier. Men that senior did not slip. They delivered.

Whitford turned the folder face down before he went on. “Apparently the Overseer doesn’t clear operational details with the Pentagon. Just does what they want. And what they want, currently, is to monitor enhanced individuals in urban environments. Go Squad qualifies.”

Whitford looked at the paused blur for a moment and seemed to dislike where his own sentence was going. “They’re using us as a test case.”

Kain considered the frozen figure on the screen and finished the thought for him. “We’re the rats in the maze. Go Squad are the subjects. CADENS gets to watch how enhanced individuals operate in real-world scenarios against organized opposition.”

“We’re opposition now? Not law enforcement?”

Kain let him keep the question, and the chief paced to the window and answered it himself. “My officers look like fools on every news cycle. ‘Vigilantes outsmart police again.’ ‘Community says Go Squad more effective than CCPD.’ ‘Where are our tax dollars going?’”

Kain watched the chief’s reflection in the dark glass and let him finish. “Your ego is showing, John.”

Whitford’s color was fully up now. “It’s not about ego. It’s about institutional credibility. Every time Go Squad makes us look incompetent, people ask why we can’t do what they do. Why we need military equipment and qualified immunity if a bunch of parkour enthusiasts can do it better.”

There it was, under the budget language. Obsolescence, not ego. A man afraid the market had found a way to route around him—and Kain recognized that fear from the inside and priced the recognition for later use.

“So the Secretary of Defense is angry, but powerless. And in his powerlessness, he shared information he shouldn’t have.”

“Can’t be helped,” Whitford allowed.

He came back from the window. “CADENS operates under Overseer’s complete control, not his. But he did slip some information that could be useful.”

From inside his jacket he drew a second document, thinner, unstamped, deniable: specifications, operating parameters, and a last page with a single heading. VULNERABILITIES. Kain slowly read that page twice. Nothing reached his face.

Kain closed the second document and squared it with the first. “This changes things.”

“That’s what I thought.”

* * *

That morning had belonged to a different file. Reed had delivered it at seven: three hundred pages on Ruth Carter, bound like a prospectus, and Kain had gone through every page over breakfast and finished both at the same time. “Their leader,” Kain said, squaring the pages on the blotter.

Reed answered without opening his copy of the file. “The medical one. She’s the tactical center. Every operation flows through her decisions.”

Kain found the tabbed sheet and read from it. “Johns Hopkins medical degree,” he read, and tapped the line below it with one heavy ring.

He read on down the page. “Top of her class. Published groundbreaking research on cellular regeneration therapy three years ago. Presented at international conferences. Should be running a research department or leading clinical trials.”

Kain set the page down on the squared stack. “Why is she in an ER?”

Reed had the answer ready, which was why Kain kept him. “Politics, looks like. She challenged the chief of surgery over patient care protocols. Got sidelined. Been stuck in emergency medicine ever since.”

Which was not, Kain noted, what the woman herself would say. Her published account blamed the science—the conference, the reception, the doors that stayed shut. She had been told the reason was her work. The file knew it was a man she had crossed.

Kain knew the mechanism. He had rented it more than once. A committee seat. A quiet lunch. And a career went into storage with no document worth a subpoena.

“She’s wasted where she is,” Kain concluded. An appraisal, not sympathy. Mispriced assets offended him on principle.
