Reed took the sheet back and squared it with more care than it needed. “Yes sir. But she’s also the tactical center of Go Squad. She’s the weak point.”

Kain turned back to the research summary and ran the figures without a pen: cellular regeneration, published, shelved, unprotected, worth eight figures to the medical subsidiaries if the patents were drafted with any appetite at all. Conservatively eight.

“I want her research,” Kain told him, turning the folder back to its first page.

“Sir?”

Kain laid the file flat and spread one ringed hand on it. “The cellular regeneration protocols. The experimental treatments. All of it. Once we’ve eliminated Go Squad, we acquire the research. Patent it under TRIOMF subsidiaries. She won’t be in a position to object.”

Reed’s pen stopped, and he looked at the carpet and then at a spot in the middle distance, choosing his footing. “Sir . . . she’s Black. You really want to—”

“I want to win.” The room held still around it. His grandfather would have refused her work on principle and died owning less. His father would have stolen it and paid a lawyer to call the theft providence. Harding acquired.

The rings were motionless while he said it. “Personal feelings are expensive luxuries. Her research is valuable. The fact that she’s Black is irrelevant to its value.”

Reed wrote it down, the pen moving now. “Once the trap is set and Go Squad is neutralized, we move on her research. Have legal prepare acquisition documents. Medical board complaints. Patent filings. Make it look clean.”

He closed the folder on the badge photograph, a woman who looked into lenses, and slid it back across the desk. “She built something valuable while they kept her in an ER,” Kain went on.

He stood and buttoned his jacket over the waistcoat. “That’s the problem with institutional punishment. They suppress talent without capturing it. We’ll do better.”

* * *

By nine that evening Whitford had heard the shape of it. He stood at the glass with a scotch he had barely touched, watching the city light itself district by district below. Subtracting things.

“You want to pressure her.”

Kain laid it out in deliverables. “I want her to make a choice. Save herself or save her team. Make it public enough that CADENS can’t intervene without revealing themselves. Make it brutal enough that Go Squad questions whether they’re actually helping or just getting people killed.”

“Break the leadership, break the team.”

Kain turned his chair to face him fully. “Break the team, break the narrative. Break the narrative, remind people why they need order. Why they need someone strong enough to protect them from chaos.”

“You’re going to run on this.”

“I’m going to win on this.”

He said it in the tone he used for confirming wire transfers. Then he nodded at the screen, where the blur still hung mid-climb. “Set the trap. I want them confident. I want them thinking they’re protected. I want them walking into it believing they’re invincible.”

“I’ll coordinate with tactical.” The chief nodded slowly at the carpet.

“You’ll have what you need.” The screen light sat on his rings.

Whitford collected his folders and got as far as the door before he stopped. “There’s one more thing.”

“What?”

Whitford did not turn around when he said it. “The anomalies. The impossible movements. Even with CADENS watching them, they’re too good. Something else is happening.”

Kain waited, because the discomfort on the chief was worth watching; it meant the thought had been carried around for days. “Maybe they are enhanced,” Whitford offered.

He said it like a man testing ice. “Maybe CADENS gave them more than surveillance.”

“Or maybe someone else did.”

Kain heard his own sentence land and disliked it. Two unknowns were operating in his city now, a federal program with no address and whatever lived inside that blur, and he had built his fortune on being the man who knew. He preferred markets with fewer entrants. He preferred names.

Kain rose and came around the desk at his own pace. “If they’re enhanced, we’ll need more than conventional force to stop them.”

“What did you have in mind?”

Kain pressed the intercom with one heavy knuckle. “Get Dr. Jericho from holding. It’s time we had another conversation about his collection.”

* * *

The vault sat four levels under the tower, behind a door labeled CLIMATE ARCHIVE. Sixty-two degrees, forty percent humidity—Jericho’s own specifications, written years ago for the museum, and there was a certain economy in letting the man’s rigor furnish the man’s cage. Kain’s grandfather’s union men had taught the family that you did not break a strike, you broke the man the other men watched, and that isolation did the work of violence at a fraction of the unit cost. Six weeks of clean quiet had been doing that work here. Two guards brought Jericho in and sat him at the steel table. Six weeks ago the man had curated the sub-basement of the Natural History Museum. He had lost weight since, and his collar stood away from his neck. His hands shook. He flattened them on the table and kept them there while he spoke.

“Walk me through them again,” Kain said. The vault door had closed behind the guards on its slow hinge. The cases stood in two rows, twenty-three of them, lit from below. Some of the objects inside sat at angles that did not properly resolve. Kain had stopped trying to make them resolve.

Jericho kept his eyes on the table. “Most of them are inert. Or appear to be. We documented electromagnetic anomalies. Temperature fluctuations. But no active properties we could test safely.”

“And the others?”

Jericho looked at the corner case before he answered, then looked away from it, and Kain walked to the corner case and stood beside it. “The Heart of Tamois,” Jericho answered.

“Tell me.”

Jericho recited it like catalog copy. “Recovered from a dig site in the Maghreb. Private expedition funded by collectors who wanted answers the academic establishment wouldn’t provide. Carbon dated at 4.7 billion years.”

“Older than Earth.”

“Older than the solar system. The object shouldn’t exist.”

The thing in the case was the size of a fist, and crystalline. It pulsed. Red, then amber, then red again, like a slow heartbeat.

“What does it do?”

The shaking had reached his shoulders. “We don’t know. Not exactly. The electromagnetic readings were off the scale. Temperature fluctuations. Temporal distortions in its immediate vicinity.”

“Temporal,” Kain repeated.

“Time moves differently near it. Milliseconds. Nothing significant. But measurable.” Thirty feet of concrete in under two seconds. A camera flinching at nothing. He did not need it explained twice.

“Figure it out,” Kain told him.

“What?”

“What it does. How it works. Whether it’s useful.”

He set one ringed knuckle on the glass, above the pulse. “You have everything you need here. Equipment. Monitoring systems. Time.”

“I can’t just—we documented these artifacts for years without understanding them. You’re asking for answers we don’t have.” The flat hands had come up off the table.

“Then find them. You’re an anthropologist. You study dead civilizations, decode their technology. Do that.”

Jericho’s glasses had slid down; he did not fix them. “In how long?”

“Forty-eight hours.”

“That’s impossible.”

“Then you’d better work quickly.”

He was at the vault door when he stopped and turned around. Jericho had not moved. The red light crossed the man’s glasses and left them, crossed them and left them. “Dr. Jericho? You’ve spent your career studying dead civilizations. Objects from species that no longer exist. Now you get to discover if their solutions still work.”

The door sealed on the collection. In the corridor the air ran warmer, and Whitford stood waiting with the guards, out of earshot of the cases. “He’ll do it?” Whitford asked.

“He’ll do it.” Kain moved toward the elevator without waiting.

“And after?”

Kain did not slow down for the answer. “After, we test the Heart. See if the transformation can be controlled. See if we can create something strong enough to stop enhanced individuals.”

“You’re talking about human experimentation.”

“I’m talking about leveling the playing field. Go Squad has powers they shouldn’t have. We’re going to match them.”

“With artifacts from civilizations that died billions of years ago.”

“With tools that work. The source doesn’t matter. Results do.”

“When do we deploy?”

“Once we have the Heart. Once we’ve confirmed it functions. Then we set the trap.”

“And Carter?”

“Carter gets to watch her team fall apart. Gets to choose between saving them and saving herself. Gets to learn that powers and federal protection don’t mean invincibility.” The elevator carried them up in silence, and Whitford watched the floor numbers the whole way. Kain watched nothing at all.

* * *

The office was empty when he came back to it. He poured nothing and ate nothing. He sat with the skyline until the phone on the credenza rang at eleven, the one with no line into the building. His engineers had opened that wall twice and found no wire behind it, and it rang anyway when it chose to. This was the third call in six months. The first two had each carried one piece of information. Both pieces had been perfect.

He picked it up on the second ring and waited. “Mr. Kain.” A woman’s voice, precise and clinical, statements where questions should be, like someone who had learned English as a technical specification.

“I’m listening,” he said.

“The Heart of Tamois. You have acquired it.”

The vault was four levels down, sealed, on no network he had ever ordered built. “I have.”

“And Dr. Jericho is attempting to unlock its properties.”

That decision was an hour old, made behind a steel door in a room that recorded nothing. “He is.”

“He will fail,” she said. He turned the signet once and did not answer.

“The Heart requires specific activation protocols. Protocols his civilization does not possess.”

His civilization. He let the phrasing go past.

“You’re offering to help.”

“I am offering partnership. You require tools to neutralize enhanced individuals. I require the artifacts gathered and their properties tested. Our interests align.”

“What do you want in return?”

“The subjects eliminated. Permanently. No capture. No interrogation. Complete removal from the board.”

She kept calling the Go Squad “subjects.” He filed the word where she had set it, flat.

“Why?”

“That is not your concern. You wish to win your campaign. I wish to see these individuals removed. The Heart provides both outcomes.”

He weighed it like any position: cost, return, counterparty. No other bidders. The cost would be carried by other people, the return was everything he wanted, and the counterparty had been right twice already without once explaining herself, and that was itself a data point.

“What are the activation protocols?”

“I will provide them when Dr. Jericho has completed his preliminary analysis. Forty-eight hours, as you specified. After that, you will have what you need.”

As you specified. He had specified it an hour ago, underground, to a man and two guards.

“And you’ll have what you need.”

“Correct.” A pause opened. He waited it out.

“The subjects display capabilities beyond conventional enhancement. This complicates elimination.”

“CADENS?”

“No. CADENS observes. They do not enhance.” He had known that name for three hours. She used it without any weight at all.

“The source of their abilities is irrelevant to your objectives. Focus on elimination, Mr. Kain. Not comprehension.” He had not asked the question she was answering.

“You know something about them.”

“I know they must be removed. That is sufficient.” The rings lay still against the blotter.

“I’ll have Jericho proceed as planned,” he said.

“Excellent. I will contact you in forty-eight hours. The Heart will be functional. Your trap will be ready. The subjects will be eliminated.”

The call ended without a click. The line stopped carrying her. He set the receiver down and filed her where he filed all capital with unclear terms: usable, until the terms arrived. Then he restarted the footage. Fifteenth viewing. The figure went up the face of the concrete, and the image smeared where it always smeared, and the roofline arrived too early.

Problematic, she had called them on the first call, months ago. He was coming to think that was an understatement.

## Chapter 18

The staging roof was four stories of cold tar paper with a clean line on the warehouse, and they had reached it on foot, over rooflines, in the dark. Ruth knelt at the parapet with the schematics folded on her thigh. Wind came off the water and went through the armor like it wasn’t there. She had pulled enough winter shifts in her life to know the trick: quit negotiating with cold and itemize it. Ben crouched beside her and delivered the tip from memory, flat as a chart note.

“Weapons cache in warehouse district. District 7, near the docks. Kain’s people moving product tonight, 2100 hours. Minimal security. Window’s tight.”

“Thoughts?” Ruth kept her eyes on the warehouse while she asked it.

“Fits the pattern. Kain’s been moving operations since the warehouse raid. Makes sense he’d use the docks.” Ben laid it out as settled fact, which was how his precinct contact always sold things, and the man had been right about enough of them that nobody in the room asked twice.

Leah lay stretched along the parapet with the binoculars. “Three heat signatures inside. No vehicles in the lot yet. If they’re moving product, we’re early.”

“Good,” Victor agreed, cracking each knuckle in turn. “Hit them during setup. Before they’re organized.”

“I like it when they’re not organized.” That was Tess, hood up against the wind.

Ruth ran the assignments worst first and nearest second. Victor and Ben would take the loading dock. Tess and Leah would hold the roof. She would keep the perimeter and call it from comms, because somebody had to see the whole patient. Twenty minutes of planning and it sat right. She checked her watch against the window and found nineteen to spare.

“Oh. Oh no.” Tess had her phone out, the screen lighting her chin blue.

“What?” Victor leaned in from her other side.

“Wait,” Ben said, looking over her shoulder. “How do they know our codenames?”

“There’s like . . . hundreds of posts using them. Battlea this, Night Knight that.” Leah had her own phone out now, thumb moving quickly.

“Did you—” Victor let the accusation finish itself.

“What? No!”

“Gloom Girl.” Leah turned her screen around as evidence.

“I didn’t leak anything,” Tess insisted, and then honesty caught up with her, a block behind. “I just . . . may have used them. In a few posts. Months ago. Hypothetically.”

“Firas is going to love this,” Ruth said.

“Or kill me,” Tess muttered, pocketing the phone.

Ruth let them keep the laugh, because a laugh before an op was cheap sedation and she genuinely believed in sedation. What was underneath it she kept for herself. Strangers had names for them now, and a named thing draws eyes, and eyes make every mistake bigger. She noted it and filed it and went back to the window they were burning.

At forty minutes to the hour she brought them onto the net.

“Everyone check in,” Ruth said. “Comms clear?”

They came back crisply and one at a time, and under the check-ins ran the feeling she had privately quit questioning: the team was right tonight. Everyone half a step early, tight on her callouts, like a good trauma bay gets ten minutes before a bad arrival, when the room already knows its shape. Whatever her own part in that was, she had stopped asking. It worked, and tonight working was the whole chart.

“No better place than here,” Ruth said, because Firas had built the call four years ago and traditions were load-bearing.

“No better time than now.” It came back from all of them at once and in step.

“Moving in,” Victor reported, and the entry was clean: two shadows crossed the lot, took the loading dock door, and were gone inside it while the night stayed quiet enough that she could hear her own pulse under the earpiece.

“Clear inside,” Victor said. “Setting up position.”

Eight minutes to the window. The lot stayed empty, and empty had stopped feeling early.

“Something’s wrong,” Tess reported from the roof. “There’s nobody here. Warehouse is empty.”

Three heat signatures, and nobody home. Ruth trusted the patient over the chart, every time, in every building.

“Abort. Everyone out. Now.”

“Too late,” Ben answered from the dark.

The floodlights arrived all at once, three sides of the lot going white as an operating room. Fifteen units at minimum, SWAT vans seeded among the cruisers, deployment already out and moving, every exit her plan had liked already spoken for. Nobody stages that in eight minutes. That force had been parked in the dark since before her team crossed its first rooftop, which meant the tip had never been intelligence. It had been an invitation, and she had RSVP’d for five.

“This is CCPD. You are surrounded. Exit the building with your hands visible.” The megaphone managed to sound bored.

“Shit,” Leah breathed.

“Smoke and scatter,” Ruth snapped. “Formation Delta. IR goggles. Move.”

The canisters went down and the lot became weather. She held her position and called the lanes, and the team threaded them as drilled, four shadows moving through white on choreography they owned, crossing gaps the flashlights kept guessing at and kept getting wrong. All four out of the cordon clean, on training. She allowed herself one full second of professional pride, because that was a thousand hours of dark gym and the pride was earned.

Then her goggles died. Full charge, mid-sweep, dead glass. She toggled the power twice, as you re-check a flat line before you believe it, and got nothing back. The GPS unit followed it down. The radio lived, barely, gone thin and scratchy.

“Anyone else losing equipment?” Tess sounded more puzzled than scared. That would not last.

“IR’s down,” Victor confirmed. “GPS too.”

“What the hell—” Victor again, and the rest of it went wherever it went.

“Powers are down,” Leah said, and the static could not hide what rode in on it. “I can’t—they’re just gone.”

Gone was nearly the wrong word for what Ruth heard after that; it was more like altitude, four strong people still moving and still trained but suddenly breathing thinner air, heavier inside their own bodies than they had been thirty seconds before. She caught it in the footfalls and in the half beat everything now landed behind. In herself she felt no change at all, and she had no idea what that meant, and no time to spend on it.

“You don’t need them. You’re trained. Stay focused.”

“Fall back to secondary positions,” Ruth ordered. “Stay in smoke. Use training. You’ve got this.”

They steadied, and she could actually hear it happen, which mattered more than whatever had been subtracted, because panic killed faster than anything else the night had shown her yet.

“Contact. North side. What the hell is that?” Ben called, and the smoke gave her nothing to confirm it with.

Through a seam in the smoke she found it. Seven feet of armored figure walked out of the floodlight wash at a stroll, wrong in scale against the cruisers. An officer broke cover to run, and the figure caught him one-handed and threw him, and the man crossed fifteen feet of air and folded a patrol car door around himself. Her eye read the landing automatically: scapula, ribs, spleen if he was unlucky. He stayed down, and nobody went to him.

“New player,” Ruth warned. “Enhanced. Real enhanced. Everyone stay clear.”

“It’s a goddamn tank!” Victor yelled, already flanking left with his staff up, because staying clear had never once been Victor’s discipline.

The Tank let him commit, took the staff on a forearm, and closed one hand over Victor’s wrist. The turn he made was small and terrible. Ruth read the arm as she would a film in a lightbox, elbow driven past its stop, shoulder rolling out of station, and then Victor was on the concrete and not using that arm to get up.

“Victor!” Tess screamed it from the roofline.

Leah was already in. Two textbook strikes with full hips behind both, and then she was through a stacked pallet and down among the broken boards, and the whole sequence had used less time than the naming of it.

Ben went low and never finished. The Tank lifted him by the throat one-handed, boots clear of the ground, and held him there like a man holding a clipboard he meant to read later.

“Put him down!” Ruth shouted, and she was off her perch and dropping through the fire escape before the sentence ended.

The Tank turned toward her and opened his hand. Ben hit the ground breathing. That was the trade on offer, and she took it.

The board sorted itself in one pass, the last clear thinking the night allowed her. Victor down, arm gone. Leah down, status unknown. Ben down, airway bruised at minimum. Police tightening from three sides, and one enhanced problem standing in the middle of her wounded. The math had only the one answer.

“Everyone fall back. Get clear. I’ll draw him off.”

“Ruth, no—” Tess again.

“That’s an order.”

She ran east and the docks took a quarter mile and she gave it everything, lungs tighter every block, cold air coming down her throat like blades. Behind her he followed at his own pace, and the sound of him never fell away. The container yard let her into its steel canyons, right and left and right on memory and guess. Then the canyon quit, and there was no more east. Black water at the toe of her boots, breathing against the pilings. It would be forty degrees this month. Ten minutes to hypothermia for a strong swimmer, less in armor, and she knew it before she finished stopping.

“Team, sound off. Everyone clear?”

Static.

“Anyone copy?”

The channel hissed, empty. Alone then at the edge of the water, with the sound of him coming through the containers, unhurried as a man walking his own property.

He came around the last stack at a walk, and under the yard lights he undid the helmet and lifted it off, and the face underneath was the worst thing the night had produced. Thirty or so, and clean-shaven. A face assembled from features nobody would swear to, offering nothing to remember. She would have lost it in a waiting room inside a week.

“Dr. Carter,” the Tank said.

Her name. She held her feet still and her face closed while it landed, because armor that knew her name changed the size of everything behind it.

“You should have stayed in the ER.”

“How do you know my name?” Ruth asked, and hated needing to.

“Everyone knows your name now.”

Which answered nothing. The city knew the codenames—Tess had seen to that months ago, and none of them were hers. Somebody had put a file together, and this man had read it.

He set the helmet on a bollard, taking his time, a man on shift with the job well in hand.

“Mr. Kain sends his regards. Says your research will be very valuable. Once you’re out of the way.”

Her research. Three years on a shelf, and it surfaced here at the edge of black water, in a hired mouth. She put it away with everything else she could not use yet.

“You’re enhanced,” Ruth said. “Real enhanced. Not like us.”

“Finally figured that out?” He sounded mildly interested. “Your tricks stopped working the moment the EMP hit. Ours didn’t.”

An EMP, then. She set it beside the dead goggles and the thin radio and left it there, because it bought her nothing at this range.

“What did they give you?” Ruth asked, stalling with the only thing she had left. “What kind of enhancement?”

“The kind that matters.” He rolled his shoulders once, loosening them. “The kind that can’t be turned off with a magnet.”

“You know what happens next,” the Tank said, and he said it without any weight at all. “Make it easy. Don’t run.”

She looked at the water and then at the man. The water was forty degrees, ten minutes, and a current she did not know, and the man was certain. She went for the gap between the containers, low and fast, and she was fast.

His hand closed on her arm before her third step landed. He turned her effortlessly, walked her backward, and set her at the dock edge with her heels on the last inch of it.

“Brave,” the Tank said. “Stupid, but brave.”

The fist came back. The water waited.

## Chapter 19

Ruth closed her eyes. Nothing in front of her was worth watching, and some old animal part of her preferred the dark for this. The fist came. She heard the wind of it arrive at her ear as a low whistle, the sound heavy things make once they have finished deciding.

Then the whistle stopped in the middle of itself.

The water quit slapping the pilings behind her heels. The yard lights held their hum on one flat note and stayed there. She waited three seconds for the pain, strictly out of habit, and the pain declined to arrive. So she opened her eyes.

The fist hung four inches from her jaw. Above it the snarl had locked into place, upper lip drawn off the teeth, and the eyes behind it stayed open and dry and still. Her own breath left her and hung in the cold air in a shape that would not drift. Twenty feet off, at the mouth of the container canyon, a small figure stood with one hand out.

Ruth knew that posture. Shoulders squared against something, chin up, one arm extended as though holding a door for somebody slower.

“Ahdia?”

The figure took a step, stopped, and took another, and the walk was wrong in a way Ruth catalogued before she could stop herself: weight loaded onto the right leg, the left arm swinging dead. “Don’t move,” Ahdia said, and the words crossed the yard flat and certain.

* * *

Ruth turned her head slowly, because slowly seemed to be the only speed the world would still accept. Six meters out from the small figure the air changed. She could see the seam. Inside it everything held; past it the night went on being a night, a light bar sweeping red across the far container tops, a gull tilting and staying tilted only until it crossed the line. Standing at the boundary was the difference between being underwater and surfacing, and she was standing at it. Her heels were on the last inch of the dock. Her shoulders, by perhaps four inches, were inside the seam.

“You’re at the edge of the . . . the freeze. If you step outside, you’ll unfreeze too. Just . . . stay still.”

Ahdia crossed to her, and the crossing cost. Blood from the left nostril, tracked to the jaw and dried once already. Tremor in the extended hand, fine and fast. Tactical gear, matte black and torn open at the shoulder, and a black domino mask hanging off one corner of her face. Six meters of field traveled with her, and the seam slid forward over Ruth like a tide coming in, and the dock edge stopped being a border.

She made herself look at the fist again. It had moved. A hair, perhaps less, in the time she had spent looking away and back. The world was not stopped at all. The world had been slowed to a rate at which a man’s whole punch would take a week, and her mind refused the number and accepted the fist.

Ahdia walked around him. She set two fingertips against the outside of his right knee, held them there, and lifted them away. She did it again under his left shoulder blade. She did it a third time at the solar plexus, and a fourth at the other knee, and by the second one Ruth had it, because she had done it herself a hundred times with a violet pen on a shaved abdomen. The girl was marking incision points.

Then the yard came back on.

For half a second the whistle resumed and the water slapped and the lights buzzed, and the fist crossed an inch of the four it had left. Then it locked again. Ahdia had a hand flat on a container and her head down.

“Sorry. Can’t hold it as steady as I used to.”

Nothing in Ruth’s training covered a reply to that. She kept her heels where they were.

“This is going to be weird,” the girl said.

“When I unfreeze him, he’s going to keep punching. But you won’t be there anymore. I’ll move you. Just . . . don’t freak out, okay?”

“Okay,” Ruth said.

“On three,” Ahdia said.

Ahdia stepped in and laid her palm against the man’s sternum and leaned on it, the exact pressure you use on a door you know is unlocked. “One . . . two . . .”

There were hands on Ruth’s shoulders. Then there was nothing, an interval with no content whatsoever, and then she was ten feet to the left with her back against cold steel, and her body reported no memory of having traveled. The gap frightened her more than the fist had.

The world let go.

The punch finished into empty air and carried him a stumbling half step, and in that instant the four touches landed together. His right knee folded sideways. Something across his back gave with a sound Ruth felt in her own teeth. He came off his feet, and the gentle hand that had leaned on his sternum arrived as a truck, and he went backward through the wall of a container and did not come out of it.

Then Ahdia dropped.

Ruth got to her before her knees landed, which was reflex. She went to the ground with her and turned her and the doctor took over, because the doctor always did. Pulse thready and far too fast. Skin burning through the torn suit. Pupils blown wide and slow to answer the light. Respirations shallow, fast, and getting worse while she watched.

“Ahdia. Look at me. Focus.”

“Saved you . . . like I saved all of them . . .”

Ruth’s mind did what it did with any presentation that made no sense, which was to quit arguing with the symptoms and go hunting the one disease underneath them.

Tess off the bridge rail into open air and delivered unhurt onto a rooftop four blocks inland. Somebody carried her. Victor’s hand closing on a rifle barrel and folding it like foil, in a man who could not deadlift what that takes. Somebody amplified him. Ben leaving a roof at three feet and arriving at thirty, always on the same crouch. Somebody put him there. Leah at freeway speed, shoulder first, in a woman Ruth had treated for a hamstring.

Her own callouts, the team a half beat early on every one of them. She reached that item, turned it twice, and set it down unsolved, because the rest of the list was answering.

Tonight an EMP had gone off in that lot and taken four powers off the board inside one second, and left her precisely as she had always been. She had wondered what that meant about her, and the answer was that it meant nothing about her. It meant the other four had been running on something outside their own bodies.

That something was lying in her lap, bleeding from the nose, twenty-seven years old, and supposed to be dead since August.

“The powers,” Ruth said.

Her hand had gone still on the girl’s wrist. “Their powers. That was you.”

“They needed . . . to believe . . .”

“You’ve been dying to give us powers we never had,” Ruth said.

Ahdia’s eyes closed. “Worth it.”

Ruth put the back of her hand against the girl’s forehead, a mother’s gesture and not a physician’s, and did it anyway. “You’re burning up.”

“How long have you been doing this?”

“Since the warehouse. Since the explosion. Since you all . . .”

The sentence quit and restarted somewhere else, as sentences do when the blood pressure has opinions. “Needed to matter. Needed to help. He said I was already dead inside, so . . . might as well be useful while . . .”

Ruth had been standing in that room. She had watched Firas say it and had taken him apart for saying it, and the whole time there had been nobody behind the blue foam vault, and the air in that corner had stayed empty.

Ahdia stopped breathing.

“Ahdia!”

“Stay with me. Come on.”

Her kit held a tourniquet, gauze, shears, and one ampule of epinephrine, and she had never been so out of ideas. There was no wound. There was no bleed she could compress and no airway she could open. This wasn’t an injury. This was collapse, a whole system shutting down, and the only intervention that mattered had already been used on a woman kneeling at the edge of the water.

The radio at her collar came up out of its static. “Dr. Carter.”

“Who is this?”

“My name is Ryu. I’m here to extract the asset.”

“The ‘asset’ is a person,” Ruth said. She had two fingers under the jaw, hunting a pulse that kept declining to be found. “And she’s dying.”

“I’m aware.”

“CADENS can help her. But we need to move. CCPD three minutes out.”

“What the hell is CADENS?”

“Come with her and find out. Or stay and watch her die. Your choice. Clock’s ticking.” Something came down the canyon at shoulder height with running lights on it, unhurried and openly visible, towing a stretcher that carried itself. Behind it a man in a windbreaker jogged into the light, badge on a lanyard, a scientist doing fieldwork badly.

She made the drone show her the feeds first. Four green shapes north of the cordon, upright, moving together, away. Victor’s shoulder would want setting. Ben would breathe wrong for a week. All four had airways. The woman on the dock did not.

“I’m not leaving her side,” Ruth said.

“Then you’re coming with us. Hold on.”

Ruth got both hands under the girl and did not let go, and then the light climbed past white and the docks folded inward like origami, and every cell she owned received the correction on a slightly different schedule. She arrived on her hands and knees on polished concrete and threw up twice, while above her a man said something calm about protocols.

The room was a medical bay built by people with no budget ceiling. Articulated arms lifted Ahdia off the floor and laid her on a table without a human hand touching her, and screens came down on their own, and Ruth got herself upright by the wall and made her hands stop. “Tell me what’s wrong with her.”

“She’s dying. But you already knew that.” The woman had come in behind her without noise. Silver-gray, past fifty-five, dressed for a boardroom at an hour when boardrooms are dark.

“Who are you?”

“Director Overseer Bourn. CADENS. And you’re Dr. Ruth Carter—emergency medicine, Johns Hopkins, cellular regeneration research that conventional medicine laughed out of conferences.”

Ruth had spent three years learning to hear that sentence without flinching, and mostly she had succeeded.

“We need to talk. But first, let’s stabilize your friend.” The nearest screen wrote itself in white while the arms worked.

“Cellular Degradation Rate: Accelerating”

“Temporal Phase Variance: 47%”

“Telomere Integrity: 18% (CRITICAL)”

“Projected Viable Function: 18 months ± 6 months”

Telomere analysis took a lab three weeks and a grant application. This machine had done it in under a minute, and Ruth registered the professional awe, filed it for a night she could afford, and went straight at the number instead. “Eighteen months,” she said. “Best case scenario.”

“That’s if she stops using her powers completely,” Bourn said. “Every use accelerates breakdown. Tonight’s intervention alone depleted another 4%.”

Four percent. A fist, a wall, ten feet of a woman moved sideways out of its way. She made herself hold the number where she could see it. “At her current rate . . .”

“Weeks. Maybe less.”

“Can you stop it?”

“Can you un-shoot someone? Put aged cells back together?”

“No.”

“Neither can we. We can only slow it. Make the time she has left . . . matter.”

Bourn indicated a chair against the wall with two fingers, like a surgeon indicates an instrument. “Sit,” she said. “I’ll explain everything. And then you can decide if you want to help her, or watch her burn herself out trying to save people who don’t even know she exists.”

* * *

An hour later Ruth stood at an observation window above a lit table with a small figure on it, and the broad strokes had come in the same flat register. An artifact in a warehouse. Drones that amplified whatever she gave them. Six weeks of monitoring, timestamped, unbroken, beginning the night Ruth herself had been dragged out of a burning building believing she had lost her.

“You saw what she can do,” Bourn said. “Imagine that scaled up. Imagine using it against actual threats. Cosmic-level incidents. Things that would make Kain look like a playground bully.”

“You want to use her,” Ruth said.

“We want to keep her alive long enough to transition the team to truth. FAERIS drones are learning. Evolving. Eventually they’ll be able to provide real enhancements without her temporal manipulation. But we need time.”

“How much time?”

“Months. Maybe a year. If we’re lucky.”

Bourn stood at the glass beside her and did not look through it. “Your research,” she said. “Cellular regeneration protocols. Could they work on temporal degradation?”

Ruth ran it before she answered, honestly, as she would for a resident. “Possibly,” she said. “The principle is similar. Accelerate cellular repair. Reduce breakdown rate. I’d need to adapt the protocols. Test dosages. Monitor effects.”

“You’d have access to our resources. Full lab. Synthesis equipment. Isotopes you can’t get anywhere else.”

Three years of grant applications for isotopes, offered at two in the morning like a mint on a pillow. “You’ve been watching my research.”

“For two years. Since your first paper. We knew you’d intersect with our work eventually.”

“You knew I’d be useful.”

“We knew you were brilliant. And that brilliance would be needed.”

The word went in somewhere old and sat there. A room of men had decided years ago what her work was worth, and this organization had disagreed and never said so.

“We just didn’t know how.”

“What’s the offer?”

“Keep her alive as long as possible. Help transition Go Squad to truth when FAERIS achieves independence. Be our liaison—they trust you.”

“You want me to manage the lie.”

“Manage the transition. There’s a difference.”

Ruth let that sit, because there was a difference, and it was smaller than either of them was pretending. “I need to see your lab first,” she said. She put her back to the glass and negotiated as an equal or not at all. “And I need full disclosure on what you know about her condition. Everything. No secrets.”

“Deal. Dr. Ryu will give you the tour. Then we’ll discuss terms.”

“One more thing,” Ruth said. She sorted it worst first, and the worst was on the table behind the glass. “I tell my team I got separated during the fight. I tell them I’m clear. They can’t know about this. Not yet.”

“Agreed. We’ll translocate you back near the Academy. Make it look like you escaped on your own.”

Her phone had survived the night. Her goggles had not. She typed it with her thumb.

“Had to split off. I’m clear. Meet at Academy.”

Then she looked once more through the glass, at the girl the whole city had already buried, and got nothing back from her own face in the reflection.

## Chapter 20

The corridor ran a hundred yards without a window. Ruth walked it close to one wall, a habit from buildings she had not chosen to enter. An hour ago a woman in a boardroom suit had used the words “shadow government facility” in the tone other people save for parking garage. Then, in the same flat delivery, “cosmic threats.”

Ryu walked ahead of her at a pace that kept turning into a jog. He talked over his shoulder for most of it.

“—so the cellular regeneration protocols you published three years ago were genuinely groundbreaking,” he said. “The way you approached accelerated healing through localized metabolic manipulation—that’s exactly the kind of thinking we need. Most researchers get stuck on macro-level solutions but you went micro, which is brilliant, and I know I’m talking too much but I get excited about this stuff and—”

He stopped walking suddenly enough that she nearly took his heels off.

“Sorry.”

He pushed his glasses up and squared the badge on his shirt pocket with both hands, tidying whatever happened to be nearest.

“I do that. The talking thing. My brain moves faster than my mouth and then my mouth tries to catch up and it just . . .”

“It’s fine,” Ruth said. It was mostly true. She had been running three words behind him since the elevator, still turning over “shadow government facility.”

He badged them through a door that took two full seconds to decide about him. “This is the main research lab.”

The room went sixty feet back, and it was the quiet that costs money: four synthesis benches down the left wall, pharmaceutical grade, the good ones. Behind leaded glass stood the isotope libraries, labeled in a hand nobody had rushed, and over the center table a molecular model turned slowly, amber and white, unbuilding and rebuilding itself like weather. Ruth had written grant applications for one item on that wall. Three years of them. She never got it.

A desk stood in the corner with its surface cleared and its chair squared to it, and there was no nameplate on it, no dust, nothing on the blotter at all. Somebody had been keeping it ready.

“This is where you’d work,” Ryu said. He took his hands out of his pockets and put them back. “If you agree. Full access to everything. Synthesis capabilities, isotope libraries, computational modeling. Whatever you need.”

“The temporal degradation data,” Ruth said, already crossing to the display. “Can I see the full workup?”

He brought it up without sitting down, and Ahdia assembled herself in gray above the center table, life-size and slowly turning. Fine threads ran through the gray, gathered thick at the center of the chest, then went outward along the vessels, root-like, into both shoulders and down the long bones. Ruth walked around it once. She stopped at the hands, where the threads thinned and stopped short of the fingertips.

“The Hyper Seed integration,” she said.

“It’s converting her. Slowly. Every time she uses her powers, it spreads a little more. Replaces biological tissue with something else. Something that can channel temporal energy.”

She asked for the panels, and he stacked every one they had beside the body while she read down the columns. Apoptosis rates, telomere lengths, mitochondrial output falling off a shelf. She had read these exact columns, in this order, in men who had been on fire.

“The mechanism,” Ruth said. The certainty arrived a full second before the reasoning that properly justified it. “It’s cellular stress. Just like gunshot trauma or burns. The stressor is different—temporal manipulation instead of physical damage—but the cellular response is identical. Accelerated apoptosis. Telomere degradation. Metabolic collapse.”

Ryu’s fingers went still on the console edge. “Exactly.” He came quickly around to her side of it. “We’ve tried conventional interventions. Nothing works. The temporal nature of the stress makes it impossible to treat with standard medicine.”

They had gone at it as a temporal problem, which was the honest mistake of people who owned the only instruments capable of measuring one. Ruth looked at the threads and saw a burn.

“My CR-7 protocol,” she said. “The cellular regeneration treatment I’ve been developing. It’s designed for trauma patients. Gunshot wounds. Burns. Severe tissue damage.”

“I’ve read your papers. It’s revolutionary.”

“It never got institutional approval. Too experimental. Too risky.” The sentence had a groove worn into it by now, and it came out smooth. “I’ve been working on it in stolen hours between ER shifts. Testing on small samples. Never had the resources for proper trials.”

“You have them now.”

He said it, a man reporting a temperature, and then he stood there and waited while she took in the row of benches, one at a time.

“The principle is the same,” Ruth said. “Accelerate cellular repair. Provide metabolic support. Reduce the degradation rate. I’d need to modify the isotope ratios—temporal stress probably affects different cellular pathways than physical trauma. And the dosage would need careful calibration. Too little won’t help. Too much could accelerate the transformation.”

“I can synthesize whatever formulation you need. We have isotopes you can’t get anywhere else. Computational models to predict cellular response. We can have a test batch ready in hours.”

“Hours?”

“This is CADENS. We don’t wait for grant approval.”

She had spent her professional life waiting on committees that met quarterly, and this man had just quoted her a turnaround in hours. “It won’t be a cure,” she said. “Best case, it buys time. Extends the timeline from weeks to months. Maybe a year or two if we’re lucky.”

“That’s more than she has now.”

“I’ll need Ahdia’s full cellular workup. Blood samples. Tissue samples if you have them. Baseline metabolic data. I need to understand exactly how the Seed is affecting her before I can counter it.”

“Already compiled.” He turned a second display toward her, and the index on it ran past the bottom of the frame and kept going. “Everything we’ve collected since the warehouse. It’s all here.”

Weeks of samples, drawn and logged and kept faithfully current by people who could not read them, waiting for somebody who could. The somebody had turned out to be her. “You said you’ve been monitoring my research for two years,” Ruth said.

“Since your first paper on metabolic acceleration. We knew it would be relevant eventually.”

“You knew I’d be useful.”

“We knew you were brilliant. The world just hadn’t figured that out yet.”

She set the panel down and squared it to the bench edge, and she left the compliment lying where he had put it. There was work. “Okay,” she said. “Show me the synthesis lab. We’re going to need to move fast.”

* * *

Four hours later the vial stood upright in a rack under the bench light, four milliliters of it, pale blue and faintly cloudy at the bottom until you turned it. Ruth had built it in a room where nothing had to be begged for, and the whole of it had taken less time than a single shift.

“This is it,” Ruth said.

“You’re sure?”

“No.” She did not dress the word up. “But it’s the best shot we have. Weeks to eighteen months, if it works. Two years if we’re lucky.”

“And if it doesn’t?”

“Then she dies faster.”

Ruth looked at the vial and not at him. “I’ll need to be present for every dose. Real-time adjustments. Clinical judgment.”

“How often?”

“Every three weeks to start. Monthly once she stabilizes.” She capped the rack and stripped her gloves. “This only works if she stops using her powers every night.”

“Good luck with that.”

“Yeah.”

He wheeled his stool back from the bench and sat on it wrong, one leg up, the tablet forgotten against his knee. “Why couldn’t we figure this out? We tried everything. Standard protocols, gene therapy, metabolic support. Nothing worked.”

“You tried what’s in the literature,” Ruth said. “I’ve been working an ER for three years while I built this. You learn things about bodies that never make it into papers. The papers I published were incomplete.”

“On purpose?”

“Hospital administrators don’t approve experiments. So I didn’t tell them everything.” She nodded at the synthesis rig behind him, four hundred pounds of machinery that had done in four hours what she herself could not have done in four years of trying. “You can synthesize anything. But you don’t know which patients need which dosages. When to push. When to pull back. That’s not science. That’s art.”

“That’s why we needed you.”

She washed her hands at the sink and dried them, and she did not turn around immediately. “Before we do this,” Ruth said. “Tell me the truth. Why does CADENS really need her alive?”

“Cosmic threats,” Ryu said. “Dimensional breaches. Things we can’t stop. Ahdia might be the only Temporalist who can.”

“And if she dies?”

“We hope FAERIS learns fast enough.”

Ruth lifted the vial out of the rack. She closed her hand around it. “I’m not doing this for CADENS,” she said. “I’m doing this because she’s my friend.”

“I know.” He got off the stool. “That’s why you’ll succeed where we failed.”

* * *

“There’s something you should see,” Ryu said at the door. “It’s relevant to the long-term plan.”

Two levels down, a window looked into a test chamber with a padded floor and a training dummy on a weighted stand, and one FAERIS unit hung at head height in the middle of it, doing nothing. Ryu called what the drones did to her team “enhancements,” and Ruth had watched that word fold a rifle barrel like foil.

“Watch,” he said, and then, to the console: “Execute enhancement protocol.”

The unit dropped four feet and held. Nothing in the chamber changed color or made a sound. The dummy’s arm came around on its stand at an ordinary speed. Then the air in a two-meter sleeve went thick and slow and slightly wrong to look at, and the arm inside the sleeve traveled four times as far as its own hinge permitted. The stand rocked and settled, and Ruth read the numbers along the bottom of the glass twice.

“No temporal field,” Ryu said. “No Ahdia. The drone did that by itself.”

“It’s replicating her powers?”

“At about 10% effectiveness. But yes. The drones have been learning. Observing how Ahdia manipulates time, how she creates the enhancement effect. They’re developing the capability independently.”

“How long have they been doing this?”

“Three days. Started small—minor accelerations, barely measurable. But the effect is strengthening.” He put a curve up on the glass, and the line left the flat, climbed, and then kept on climbing. “At current progression, they’ll reach 50% effectiveness in six months. Full capability in a year, maybe eighteen months.”

Ruth arrived at the end of it before he did. “When Ahdia dies, the team won’t lose their powers.”

“Eventually. If FAERIS evolution continues at this rate. If we can keep Ahdia alive long enough for the transition.”

“They’re becoming sentient,” Ruth said.

“We don’t use that word. Not yet. But . . . maybe.”

“What happens when they don’t need her anymore?”

“I don’t know. The drones are learning from her technique, but they’re also developing beyond it. Adapting. Finding more efficient methods.” He watched the unit hold its station in the empty chamber. “In theory, they could eventually provide enhancements that are safer, more sustainable than what Ahdia does through sheer force of will.”

“In theory.”

“In theory.”

The curve stayed up on the glass, climbing over its own numbers. “Does Ahdia know?”

“Bourn told her. Part of the original recruitment pitch. ‘Help us long enough for FAERIS to learn, then you can step back.’” He shrugged with one shoulder only. “But Ahdia can’t step back. Every time someone’s in danger, she intervenes. Burns through her life force like it’s infinite. The treatment you just developed? It buys her time. But only if she stops trying to save everyone every night.”

“And she won’t.”

“Would you?”

Ruth thought about twelve-hour shifts. She thought about the nights she had stayed past them, and about the four people searching the docks for her body. “No,” she said. “I wouldn’t.”

Below them the FAERIS unit came about on its axis, unhurried, and stopped facing the observation window. It stayed that way. “That’s new,” Ryu said.

“What?”

“That level of environmental awareness. The drones weren’t doing that yesterday.”

He watched it a while. His fingers took up their drumming on the rail, faster than before. He collected his tablet off the sill.

“Come on,” he said. “Let’s get you to medical. Ahdia should be awake by now.”

* * *

The ceiling was white. It had no water stain on it, which was how she knew she was not home.

Ahdia lay still and did the rest of it in order. The light came from a strip along the wall, and there was no window anywhere. Underground, then, or close to it. The equipment beside the bed belonged in a hospital. It made no sound. It had no wheels. It carried no brand name on any surface that she could see. A line ran into the back of her hand, taped down twice.

Then the pieces came back. Containers, black water, a fist stopped four inches off Ruth’s jaw, four points on a man who never once felt them being marked. After that, concrete, and Ruth’s hands, and nothing else.

Her mouth tasted like metal. She turned her head. Ruth stood in the doorway with a hard case under one arm.

“Hey,” Ruth said.

“Hey.”

The word came out thin and used. She tried again on something longer.

“I’m alive?”

“For now.” Ruth set the case on the tray table and unlatched it. “How do you feel?”

“Like I died and came back wrong.”

“Close.” Ruth took out a vial of something pale blue. She stood it upright in the tray. “You almost did die. On the docks. In the translocation. Twice in medical before we stabilized you.”

Ahdia looked at the ceiling for a while.

“Why didn’t you let me?”

“What?”

“I was done. I saved you. That’s all I needed to do. Why bring me back?”

Ruth stopped what her hands were doing. She left them stopped. “Because you’re my friend.”

“You barely know me.”

“I know enough.” She pulled the chair over and sat down. “I know you’ve been dying to give my team powers they never had. I know you’ve been burning through your life force every night because you can’t stand watching people get hurt. I know you think that makes you useful instead of worthy.”

“It’s the same thing.”

“It’s not.”

Ahdia turned her hand over on the blanket and looked at the tape.

“If I’m not useful, what am I?”

She waited for Ruth to answer that. Ruth did not. The rest of it came out on its own.

“I’m not . . . I don’t have anything else. No job. No life. I watch TV twelve hours a day and order gas station food and hide in my apartment because going outside means I might have to be a person and I don’t know how to do that anymore. The only thing I’m good at is stopping time. Take that away and I’m just . . .”

“Just what?”

“Nothing.”

Ruth did not move in the chair. “You saved my life tonight. You’ve saved Victor. Ben. Tess. Leah. Firas. Every person we’ve helped for months. You’ve been dying by inches to give them hope. How is that nothing?”

“Because they don’t know.” Ahdia kept her eyes on the ceiling. “They think they’re heroes. They think they have powers. They don’t know it’s me. Invisible. In the background. Fixing everything while they get to be . . . people.”

“You’re a person too.”

“I don’t feel like one.” The next part came out level, because it was only a description. “I feel like a tool that wears skin. Something that gets used until it breaks. And then what? Then I’m just taking up space.”

The room stayed quiet. Somewhere behind the bed a machine changed its note and changed back.

“I used to think that too,” Ruth said. She sat back. “Hopkins wanted me for research. Brilliant work. Groundbreaking. Then I challenged the chief of surgery and suddenly I’m in the ER. Twelve-hour shifts. Gunshot wounds. Overdoses. Watching people die because the system’s too slow or too cheap or too corrupt to save them. I thought—if I’m not doing the research, if I’m not changing medicine, what’s the point? I’m just another body in scrubs.”

“What changed?”

“I realized the ER was where I could actually help people. Not publish papers. Not collect accolades. Just keep someone alive who shouldn’t make it through the night.” She leaned forward with her forearms on her knees. “You think you’re nothing without your powers. I think you’re someone who’s been dying to save people who don’t even know your name. That’s not nothing. That’s everything.”

Ahdia looked at the vial in the tray instead of at her.

“What’s in the vial?”

“Hope.” Ruth picked it up. “I developed a treatment. CR-7 Temporal Variant. It should slow your degradation. Buy you time. Eighteen months, maybe two years.”

“Should?”

“I’ve never treated temporal stress before. But I’ve spent three years learning where this protocol bends. This is the same principle.” She drew the dose and held the syringe up to the light. “Different mechanism. Same art.”

“And if it doesn’t work?”

“Then you die faster.” Ruth found the port on the line and cleaned it. “But I’m not letting you die without trying. Someone should fight for you as hard as you fight for everyone else.”

“Why?”

“Because you’re worth saving.” She looked up from the port. “Not because you’re useful. Because you’re a person who deserves to live. Even if you don’t believe it yet.”

Ahdia had nothing to put against that. She tried for a while and found nothing.

“I don’t know how to be worth anything except this,” she said.

“Then I’ll teach you.” Ruth seated the syringe. “But first, let’s keep you alive long enough to learn.”

“How long?”

“Until it works or doesn’t.” The plunger went down slowly, in stages. Ruth watched the line and not the syringe. “We’ll know in a few hours. If your cellular markers improve, we continue treatments every three weeks. If they don’t . . .”

“Then I had weeks anyway.”

“Yeah.”

It went in cold. It came up her arm as cold and stayed cold across her shoulder. She waited for it to cross over into the pain the Seed always charged, and it did not. Somewhere under the collarbone the cold turned over and came back warm, and the warmth went out along the same roads, slowed down, and settled. The cold under the old bullet wound sat where it always sat, and it was not fixed, not healed, just less wrong.

She breathed in. The breath went further down than it had gone in weeks. “Thank you,” she said.

“Get some rest. I’ll be back to check on you in an hour.” Ruth latched the case and stood up with it.

She was three steps toward the door when Ahdia spoke.

“Ruth?”

Ruth stopped and waited.

“Don’t tell him,” Ahdia said. “Please. Not yet.”

Ruth nodded once and did not ask who. The door sealed behind her, and the room was down to its machines. Ahdia lay with her hands flat on the blanket. Above her the monitors went on with their numbers, which she could not read and did not try to, tracking something in her that was either turning around by now or not. In a few hours somebody would come and tell her which. She looked at the white ceiling and waited to find out.

## Chapter 21

The first rule of losing your superpowers was that nobody talked about losing their superpowers.

They had been not-talking about it downstairs since the docks, so Tess came up here. The room over the Academy office held a mattress, a duffel, and nine energy-drink cans. She left the cans in frame. She squared the phone in the tripod. “Hey guys, it’s your girl, Gloomy. If you’re watching this, I’m either dead or I finally got the guts to post it, and I genuinely don’t know which is more terrifying.”

She sat on the mattress. Nobody was coming up. “It’s been one day since the docks. Since the tank cop almost killed us. Since Gale disappeared and our powers just . . . stopped working.” She let it sit two seconds and then ruined it. “Well, most of them.”

She stood and raised her middle finger at the lens, slowly, like a magician showing both sides of a coin, and aimed her eyes at the door frame. Nothing happened. It used to be instant. Then the room moved a step to the left and she was at the door with the tripod six feet away, her stomach arriving late. She held the jamb until the floor agreed to be a floor. Outside the window a drone hung at roof height with its lights off, as always, and it stayed there a while after she had stopped moving. “See? Mine still works. Knight thinks it’s because mine was always tech-based—FAERIS drones doing the actual teleporting. Which means maybe all of it was tech. Maybe we were never actually super. Just regular people with really expensive invisible support.”

She sat back down. “That’s what’s breaking everyone. Not that we lost our powers. That we maybe never had them to begin with.”

Watching people was the one skill she owned that predated the mask, and she turned it on her own team now, quickly and mercilessly. “Sable’s strength? Gone. He’s dealing with it by being pissed at literally everything. Battlea’s speed? Gone. She’s gone quiet, which is way worse than when she’s yelling. Knight’s jumps? Gone. He’s over-planning every detail because if he stops thinking he’ll have to start feeling and I think that might actually kill him.”

Then the one she had been circling. “And Gale . . . Gale sent one text saying she was clear and then went dark. Nothing since the docks. She won’t answer and we don’t know if she’s hurt or hiding or—” Her thumb found the stop button and did not press it. “Can’t think about that. Focus on what we can control.”

The list of things they could control had gotten very short. Firas had made one anyway. “Firas suggested we rescue Dr. Jericho. The artifact expert who got kidnapped from the museum. The guy who was researching the same artifacts that gave Kain his powers. The Tamois Heart. Turned him into whatever that thing at the docks was.” She pulled her knees up. “If anyone knows what’s happening to us, it’s Jericho. Problem is, Firas is downstairs right now bleeding through his bandages because he won’t admit his chest wound isn’t healed. He suggested the plan, worked out half of it, but he can barely walk. So it’s just us. Four people. Three without powers. Going after a billionaire with private security and whatever cosmic bullshit Kain’s got now.”

“Great plan, right? Super tactical. I’m sure nothing will go wrong.” The laugh she hung on the end of it did not survive the trip out. “If I don’t make it back . . . tell my dad I tried. Tell him I was doing something good even if I sucked at it. And tell him there’s a photo folded inside that picture frame on his desk. Maybe he should actually look at it sometime.”

She stopped. She wiped her face with the heel of her hand, once, hard. She did not say his name. She never did. Folded behind her school photo in that frame was another one, both her parents in it and a cake. She had put it there years ago. He had never mentioned it. “Team’s meeting downstairs in five. Time to find out if we’re brave or just idiots.” She reached for the phone. “Probably both.”

* * *

The camera came downstairs with her, because holding it gave her hands a job. Documentary mode. Nobody objected, which told her more than anything anybody said.

Firas had the laptop and a chair he was obviously pretending not to need. Victor sat on a plyo box, taking each item out of the bag and putting it back. Leah had the couch and her arms crossed. She had not spoken since breakfast, and Tess had read enough rooms to know precisely what that meant on Leah. It was not attitude. It was terror with absolutely nowhere to go.

“Okay,” Ben said, and turned the laptop around. A house assembled itself in gray, drone-mapped. “Kain’s hosting a gala event at his mansion tomorrow night. Wealthy donors, politicians, the usual corrupt assholes. Heavy security on the perimeter, but inside? Just catering staff and house staff.” He set three fingers on the schematic and moved them one by one. “Three entry points. Front door—obviously not happening. Service entrance here, kitchen access. And loading dock basement entrance here.”

Firas leaned in, and the lean cost him something across the ribs, and Tess watched him spend it silently. “We need someone inside distracting Kain,” he said, and he was already looking at the couch. “Someone who can pass as a wealthy donor. Someone who can keep him occupied while the rest of us search for Jericho.”

“No.”

“Leah—” Ben tried.

“I said no.”

“You’re the only one who can pull it off.” Victor delivered that to the gear bag, which was cowardice, and Tess filmed it anyway.

“Pull off what, exactly? Playing dress-up for a billionaire creep while you guys search his mansion? Hard pass.”

“We need you,” Firas said.

“You need me to put on a tight dress and smile while Kain ogles me for an hour. That’s what you need.”

Nobody rushed to fill that. It was accurate and everybody heard how accurate it was, and Firas took the weight of it and declined to set it down anywhere clever. “Yeah. That’s what we need. And I hate asking. But Jericho might be our only shot at understanding what’s happening to us. What happened to Gale. Why our powers are gone.”

Leah studied the ceiling. “Fine,” she said, and uncrossed her arms. “But I’m picking the dress. And if he touches me, I’m breaking his hand.”

“Deal,” Firas said.

* * *

She picked red.

“Are you kidding me?”

Tess had the phone up before Leah cleared the doorway. Floor-length, cut with a slit that was clearly a tactical decision, and Leah walked the mats in it like a fighter.

“I look like I’m auditioning for a spy movie.”

“You look hot,” Tess said.

“I look like bait.”

“You look like hot bait.”

“You know you can’t post this if you shoot my face.”

“That’s what face blurring is for, duh.”

Leah stopped at the mirrors and studied herself like a stranger’s ID. “I hate this. I hate that this is the plan. Distract the creepy billionaire with my tits while you guys do the actual work.”

“Hey.” Tess lowered the phone, which she hardly ever did. “You’re not just bait. You’re getting us in. You’re the reason we have access. That’s not nothing.”

“It’s also not punching cops in the face, which is what I’d rather be doing.”

“We can punch cops after we rescue Jericho.”

Victor had come in with the zip ties and had forgotten what they were for. “Wow.”

“Don’t,” Leah said, to the mirror.

“I’m just saying—”

“I know what you’re saying. Don’t.”

“You look good. That’s all I’m saying.”

“I look like I’m about to seduce a Bond villain.”

Victor considered it honestly. “Well,” he said. “Technically you are.”

Ben came through with a comms case, mouth already open. “We need to go over the comms plan—” He stopped. “Oh.”

“Yeah,” Victor agreed.

Leah turned from the mirror with her weight on one hip, and Tess got all of it, and would eventually cut two frames after the eyebrow. “Oh. Everyone get a good look? We done objectifying me?”

Firas was in the doorway by then, one shoulder on the frame, standing at a cost nobody would itemize. “You’re not being objectified,” he said. “You’re being weaponized. There’s a difference.”

Leah breathed out slowly through her nose. “I hate that that makes me feel better.”

“We work with what we have,” Firas said. “Right now what we have is Knight’s planning, Sable’s muscle, Gloom Girl’s teleportation, and your ability to make Kain forget we exist. That’s the play.”

“If this goes wrong and I die in this dress, I’m haunting all of you.”

“Noted,” Ben said.

“I may be fine with that,” Victor said, and took a shoe for it.

“Okay,” Firas said. He pushed off the frame and stood unsupported, because some sentences could not be delivered leaning. “Let’s rob a billionaire.” He gave them the call, as he had for four years. “No better place than here,” he said.

“No better time than now.”

It came back from all of them at once. Then Leah turned to the mirror and said the whole thing herself, both halves, to her own face.

“No better place than here. No better time than now.”

Tess kept filming after the rest of them turned away.

* * *

The parking structure east of the mansion was empty after nine. Tess had its northwest corner: three laptops on a milk crate, and a sightline to the mansion’s front elevation. Four feeds: Ben’s apron cam and Victor’s, both dark and jouncing; the house system, ninety dollars and one of Ben’s favors; and Leah’s clutch, a pinhole behind the clasp, showing a car ceiling.

Twenty minutes to showtime, and Tess did what she always did with them. The bodega clip was still running: a ceiling camera at Rodriguez’s Bodega on Morrison Street, a blur in the doorway, a man with a bat sitting down abruptly beside the chip rack. She read the comments like other people take vitamins. “These are the real police.” Under that, “Squad saved my cousin last week. We owe them.” Under that, “Why aren’t we funding these people?”

Somebody had cut a compilation, scored to strings. “Go Squad Saves Caledonia - 47 Confirmed Interventions This Month.”

@CaledoniaNights had the one that got her: Mr. Rodriguez holding out a paper cup, Battlea half turned away, caught taking it. “Stopped a robbery. Wouldn’t take payment. I gave her coffee anyway. #GoSquad #CaledoniaHeroes”

None of it was true this morning. Some of it, apparently, had never been true. The city loved them honestly for a power they could not use anymore and possibly had never owned. She was the one who would have to write that post.

The last was from @WestSideWatch. “Go Squad spotted entering vehicle near Academy. Think something’s happening tonight. Stay safe, Squad. We got your backs.”

The reply count under it read 47 and climbed like a fare meter. She screenshotted it to show Leah after, because Leah would need it after. On the clutch feed the hired car swung through the gates and stopped.

“You’re on,” Tess said. “Looking good, by the way.”

“Feeling like bait,” Leah said, barely moving her mouth.

“Hot bait.”

“Not helping.”

* * *

Two nights back Ben had laid it out on a table with a marker. “Battlea enters through the front as Sophie Clement, wealthy French donor. Sable and I go in through the service entrance as catering staff. Gloom Girl stays on the parking structure two blocks out, monitors comms and security feeds.” He tapped the basement. “We’re looking for Dr. Jericho. Artifact expert. Last seen at the museum before Kain’s people grabbed him.” Sophie Clement’s name on that list had cost a donation to Kain’s PAC, and Firas had signed it out of his sister’s estate.

“If he’s anywhere, he’s in the basement research level. That’s where Kain keeps his collection.” That was Firas, from the chair.

“What if he’s not there?” Tess had asked.

“Then we get whatever intel we can and extract. Hard drives, documents, anything that tells us what Kain’s doing with those artifacts.”

Now Victor’s apron cam swung through a service door held open by a man with a clipboard who never looked up, and two caterers walked into a billionaire’s house on two rented aprons and a stack of warming trays. “We’re in. That was . . . easier than expected.”

“Don’t say that,” Ben said. “You’ll jinx it.”

Tess had filmed the loadout while Ben walked the table. “Thermal goggles under the tablecloth. Smoke grenades in the champagne ice bucket. Data drives in the bread basket. Zip ties in the napkin dispenser. Everything hidden in plain sight.”

“We’ve done the catering staff thing before. Guards never look twice at waiters.” Victor had said that cracking knuckles, taking comfort where comfort was cheap.

The kitchen was four cooks and a sous chef screaming at a salamander, and nobody had time to be curious. On Ben’s feed the service stair went down past the wine room, and the light went industrial. The corridor was concrete and cold, with a door at the end of it like the front of a bank. “Vault should be at the end of this corridor,” Ben said.

Firas had put the cloner in his hand the night before, holding it like anything he built. “RFID cloner. Get close enough to any guard’s security badge, this’ll clone the signal. Should open most of the electronic locks including the basement vault.”

“Should?” Victor had asked.

“Ninety percent chance. If there’s biometric locks too, you’ll need to improvise.”

On the feed the cloner’s light went green, and the panel with it. The door did not move. “Shit,” Victor said. “It’s not working.”

“Biometric?” Ben was already on one knee at the panel.

Victor had the thermal goggles up. His feed washed out and came back in false color, and Tess leaned over the crate to see it: heat where heat had no business being, a long cold room, a shape in the middle of it putting out warmth on a slow regular pulse. Nothing about the shape was a person. “Something’s in there,” Victor said. “Artifacts, maybe. But we can’t get in without an actual guard’s fingerprint or retinal scan or whatever.”

“So we improvise,” Ben said.

Victor held up both hands and looked at them. There was a whole day in it. “My powers are more, like, momentum based . . . ?”

Nobody laughed. Ben put his palm flat on the vault door, like you check a hood for heat. Whatever sat behind it was warm and there was a lot of it, spread out in rows, a collection on shelves, and none of it was moving. Cases, then. The intel had promised a research level and given them a warehouse instead, which meant the work had gone somewhere else and taken the man with it. “Yeah, this isn’t happening. We need to find another way. Let’s search the rest of the basement.”

They took eleven minutes on the rest. Storage, a wine cellar, empty offices with the chairs still wrapped. No cell. No cot. No Jericho. “He’s not down here,” Victor said.

“Then we go upstairs. Get what intel we can from his office.”

* * *

The clutch feed had been climbing the steps. Kain came down his own staircase in shirtsleeves, doing her the favor of being early. “Ms. Clement,” he said, and took her hand and kept it past any schedule Tess recognized. “Welcome. I’m sorry the place is in such a state. We weren’t expecting you for a few more hours.”

“I should have called ahead. But I was in the area and thought, why not?”

“Not at all, not at all.” He held on a beat longer than politely necessary. “It’s a pleasure anytime.”

Leah got her fingers back and turned toward the clasp. “He’s so gross.”

She had asked the practical question the night before. “So I just . . . keep him occupied? Talk about rich people stuff?”

“Art,” Firas had said. “He collects it. Especially ancient artifacts. Egyptian, Greek, Amazigh. If you mention you’re interested in antiquities, he’ll talk your ear off. Guy loves showing off his collection.”

“Great. I know nothing about art.”

“You know it when you see it, right?” Tess had offered, which felt helpful for a second.

“That’s porn.”

The clutch came out onto the west lawn, past caterers laying a hundred and forty covers, and stopped at nine feet of bronze Harding Kain gazing nobly at nothing. “It’s . . . magnificent,” Leah said, in an accent that was holding.

“I know,” Kain said, as other men say the time. “It’s nothing, really. You should see my place in the south of Marseille. Ever been?”

Ben’s whisper came flat and fast from a linen closet downstairs. “He’s fishing. That’s in Sophie Clement’s cover story. He might’ve read up on her.”

“Often,” Leah said. “But I prefer to stay in Paris, where I can keep a close eye on my business.”

“A woman who understands priorities. I like that.” He put a hand on the small of her back, and the frame jumped where her arm went tight and then loose. “Come. Let me show you my office. I have something that might interest a woman of your . . . refined tastes.”

“Wait, that worked? He’s taking you to the office?”

“Lead the way,” Leah said.

“Second floor office,” Tess relayed, hands flat on the crate.

The office ran the whole west end of the house, and the window ran the office. On the far wall hung a painting of a monument Tess did not recognize, with a long bright tear opened in the sky above it. “What a view you must have.”

“To see the sunset,” Kain said. “Very nice choice, isn’t it?”

Ben came onto the net. “We’re coming up. Stall him.”

A man sat at a workstation in the corner with three monitors and a cold coffee, older, thin, wearing a cardigan in a house kept at seventy-two degrees. “Dr. Jericho, take a break. We have a guest.”

“But the resonance calibration—”

“Can wait,” Kain said, and nothing in his delivery moved, and that was how you knew. “Give us the room.”

“The temporal stability is still fluctuating. If I leave now—”

“Ten minutes,” Kain said. He gave the second half to Leah, smiling, sharing it like a joke about staff. “Then you can get back to making me a god.”

Jericho went. The door closed. Nine seconds later Tess heard a soft double thud in her right ear, hardly louder than a book landing, then Ben breathing hard, and she let out air she had been holding since the gates.

“So,” Kain said, crossing to a glass chamber alone on a plinth. “You mentioned an interest in antiquities?”

“I did,” Leah said. The chamber lit as he neared it, and Tess’s screen bloomed white and then found its level, and even through a pinhole two blocks out it was hard to look at. Red, then gold, then red, on a rhythm slower than a heartbeat and considerably more patient. “This . . . what is that?”

“The Tamois Heart,” Kain said. He set one ringed knuckle on the glass above it, fondly, like a man tapping a fish tank. “The Amazigh worshipped it as divine. A gift from gods. But it’s technology. Dr. Jericho has been researching its properties. Learning how to replicate it. Scale it. Imagine—power like this, mass-produced. Distributed to whoever I choose.”

Tess wrote the word on her hand. Replicate. He did not want to be the one who had it. He wanted to be the supplier, and he said so out loud to a woman he had known eleven minutes.

Leah moved. “Sorry about this.”

“What—”

The clutch went sideways and showed carpet, then wall, then Kain’s face at an angle no photographer had been allowed. She had his wrist behind his shoulder blade and her knee in his back. She got him down unhurriedly. She broke nothing, and Tess watched her decide not to.

The door opened on Ben and Victor in catering whites, and Ben went past Kain to the workstation. “Holy shit. This is it. The artifact from the museum. And all his research.” Drives came out of the bread basket, three of them, and the monitors filled with directory trees. “Resonance patterns, cellular integration protocols, temporal manipulation theories . . .”

Victor had stopped a yard short of the plinth, hand half raised. “That thing’s . . . it’s doing something. I can feel it.”

“Don’t touch it,” Ben said, without turning around.

Kain worked his cheek off the carpet. “Who are you people?”

“Concerned citizens,” Victor told him, kneeling to run the zip ties. “And we’re taking Jericho and all this research.”

“You have no idea who you’re robbing—”

“TRIOMF executive, presidential candidate, corrupt billionaire experimenting with cosmic artifacts,” Leah said, from above him.

Victor pulled the last tie tight. “Yeah, we know.”

Ben read the summary with his thumb on the eject. “Got it. Research files, ledgers, everything. Including proof the police chief is on your payroll.”

Tess had already switched screens. The house system carried twelve cameras and eight went busy at once. Stairwells. The service corridor. Two men crossing the lawn past the bronze at a jog, four more moving steadily behind them.

“We need to get Jericho and go,” Victor said.

“Wait—I’m seeing security mobilizing. Multiple guards heading to the second floor. Did someone—”

On the clutch feed Kain’s shoe lay flat against the leg of the plinth, on a plate the size of a light switch. His heel was still on it. “Shit,” Ben said.

Leah saw it too. “He called security.”

“How many?” Victor asked.

Tess did not believe the shapes on her feed. She looked again. They were still there. “All of them,” she said. “You need to get out NOW.”

Both office doors opened at once. Ben’s camera swung and filled with men. Twelve weapons at minimum, more along the corridor behind them, one at a balcony window the office was not supposed to have. Nobody in that room had powers. Tess sat on the parking structure two blocks away with four feeds and no way in, and watched every screen stop moving.

## Chapter 22

The order reached her twice, thin on Ben’s apron cam and a half second late on the house system, and Tess heard both from two blocks east.

“Open fire!”

Twelve weapons at the near door, more in the corridor behind it, one man at the balcony window the office was not supposed to have. The west wall was mostly glass, and past it lay thirty feet of nothing, then gravel, then the fountain at the bottom of the drive. Between the bookcases on the long wall stood the plinth, and the thing from the vault sat lit inside its case on top of it, doing what it had done all night.

Leah reached the same conclusion first and solved it sideways.
