She got behind the wheeled chair and ran it out into the middle of the floor, and Harding Kain went with it, wrists zip-tied and turning slowly under his own chandelier, a nine-figure obstruction nobody had authorization to shoot through. The first volley went high, and the ceiling came down across the feed like snow.

Whoever hung the cameras in that office had hung them for a man who enjoyed being looked at. Twelve angles and every one of them pointed inward. He sat at the center of all of them now, revolving at whatever speed the casters gave him, and the room would come past him a piece at a time whether he wanted it or not.

“Gloom Girl,” Ben said from behind the desk, which was oak and the only honest cover in the room. “We’re pinned. Need an exit.”

She had one and it was bad. Four trips minimum, one body each, every arrival slower than the last, and the room still full of men when she came back for the last. Jericho lay out cold on a linen shelf. On her third laptop the copy bar sat at thirty seconds and declined to hurry.

“Copy’s not done,” she said, and her thumb pressed into the corner of the crate hard enough to leave a mark. “Hold them off.”

Victor tipped the champagne bucket. Goggles came out from under the catering linen. A dozen men lost the room in one breath, and only three did not.

Leah’s heels came off first, and they came off as ordnance. She got a hand down and took one by the strap on the way, put it through the eyebrow of the man nearest the door, and sent the second after him into the smoke without waiting to watch it land. She fought the rest of the night barefoot in the gown. She had told them the slit was a tactical decision and nobody at the table had believed her, and Tess was going to owe her about that for a long time.

Battlea took the chair apart and kept two armrests. The second one went back over her shoulder without a glance, into a hand that was already open for it. That was the part Tess would think about afterward. The hand had been open before the armrest got there.

After that the smoke gave Tess nothing to see by, and she read the fight like you read a storm from indoors: a rifle going up and away, a shoulder finding a sternum, men walking into furniture they could not see and into people they could see considerably less.

The audio carried better than the picture. She put the house feed in her good ear and listened to a dozen professionals lose an argument with a room. Chairs went over. Somebody called a corner and somebody on the far side of it called the same corner back. A man walked into a bookcase and said sorry to it. Under all of that the team made no sound whatsoever, and that was the trick, and it had cost four years of somebody standing at the mats saying do it again, softer. In a room full of smoke the guards were the only thing generating noise, and the noise told Ben and Victor where each of them was standing.

Ben came out from behind the desk and set his feet, and put his left forearm out flat at hip height, and held it there like a rail he expected somebody to use. Leah used it. She came at him out of the gray with her hand already reaching, took the forearm at a dead run, and he turned his hips into her and let go. She crossed that office in one continuous piece of movement, at a speed her own legs could not have produced. Tess had watched her do it on a flat track with a helmet on and a whistle going. She had never once seen it done in a gown.

The man at the far end took a bare heel in the sternum and the wall behind him did the rest.

Ben worked the room from the door end and Victor from the window end, and they met in the middle and stayed there, back to back, turning like one slow animal. There was nothing improvised about it. They had a name for that shape and had walked it on tape and drilled it in a gym with no smoke in it, and in the smoke it looked no different.

She should have felt better than she did. They were holding. They were holding without her and without Gale and without whatever it was that had been doing this for them since the docks, and they were good, and it turned out that had its own cold under it. On the third laptop the bar moved a little and remained in no hurry.

She kept the center camera up the whole time, because he was the center of it. He was not frightened. He sat in his chair inside his own smoke with his chin up, following the noise around the room, and his face did what her father’s face did at a car dealership. He thought he was watching equipment. Somebody had sold these people something, and he would find out who, and then he would buy two.

Smoke buys a minute. It had never bought more than that, and every person in that office who had planned anything had planned for the end of it.

The man at the balcony window had spent that minute finding a firing position and had found one. Ben ran three steps up the paneling and came off it sideways, and a guard went through the west glass and stayed audible longer than she wanted.

The hole he left was four feet wide. Smoke poured out of the office and up the face of the house, and on her screens the room resolved into furniture and a dozen men getting their eyes back. Their advantage had been the weather. They had just broken the window it lived behind.

And they kept winning.

That was the thing Tess could not find a shelf for. They were in the clear now, in a lit room, against men who could see them and had rifles and expensive training. Three people with two chair arms between them went on taking the office apart at the same rate.

Victor made a step out of his hands and Leah went up it to the top of the bookcase. She came off the bookcase into two men who were still looking at the floor where she had been. Ben threw a guard, and the throw was not an attack, it was delivery; Victor caught him at the far end and set him down. Leah got a knee onto Ben’s shoulder and Ben stood up under her. She went over the desk from six feet in the air with the second armrest already swinging.

The room worked with them. Leah pulled a drape down over a man’s head and left him wearing his own window. Victor took an armrest to a knee, then to a wrist, then laid the man out on the carpet with something close to courtesy. Ben got a shoulder into a bookcase and let it settle the argument for the two men sheltering behind it. Somebody went hard into the plinth. The case on top jumped on its base and came down starred across one corner. The plinth stayed upright, and Tess clocked it and lost it inside a second.

None of it ran in a straight line. Ben’s shoulder opened a lane and Leah was inside the lane before it finished existing. Leah crossing pulled two men off Victor. He spent the seconds that bought him stripping a rifle off a third and putting it out through the hole in the west wall. Every weapon that came loose went the same way. Nobody on any of her screens held one longer than it took to throw it.

By then the men still standing had stopped closing. They had drifted to the walls with their backs against the paneling, and a couple of them were not aiming at anything any more.

Firas built this. He built it in a gym with sour mats and a whiteboard nobody could read, and tonight the whole of it was working at full size, on the one night he could not get up the stairs. Tess sat on a crate two blocks east with her hands shaking and was proud of them in a way that was difficult to hold.

On the center camera, Harding Kain stopped moving his head.

He had tracked all of it until then, chin swinging with the noise, a man at a demonstration keeping up. Now he sat still while the room went past him, and he was looking at one place, and the place was not the fight. It was the plinth between the bookcases with the light going in its case.

He looked at it for a long while. Tess read that as a rich man taking inventory in the middle of a burglary, and went back to the copy bar, which said eleven seconds.

“Battlea! Incoming!” Victor called it from the far end, and the fresh ones came through both doors clear-eyed, taking their time about the angles.

A guard put his forehead into Victor’s face. Victor went down on one knee in the open. Leah got an arm around the nearest man’s throat and his rifle neatly into her free hand, and walked him to the wall, which bought ten seconds and cost her both hands.

“Gale?”

Nothing came back. Gale had been dark since the docks and everyone in that office knew it, and Leah asked anyway, because four years of asking does not switch off in a day.

Ben swept the roofline through the broken glass and found gutter and chimney and cold slate. Then he remembered. Nobody had known where she was since the water.

“Little help?” Leah’s hostage was working his chin down toward her forearm and getting somewhere with it.

Ben went under a man with a knife. The knife came up. Victor stood cornered against the plinth with a dozen barrels finding him, and there was nothing left to try. He put his hands out where they could be seen and came off the plinth into the open, three steps, because that was the only thing left that the barrels wanted.

Out on the carpet one of the new men got around at last to the obvious service. He got a knife under the ties at the back of the chair and sawed, with his eyes on the fight the whole time. It took him a moment. He went back to the line without once having looked at the man’s face. Kain did not get up. He put his freed hands flat on his knees and went on looking at the plinth.

Then the lights died.

All of them at once, the house and the lawn and the floods around the fountain, and the moon over the roofline seemed to dim with them out of politeness. One second of total dark.

She had left the crate two minutes before that. The first jump was the long one, structure roof to the boxwood on the west lawn, and it went as everything had gone since the docks: the finger up, the eyes set, a pause where no pause used to live, then the world stepping a foot to the left without her. She came down badly in the hedge with her stomach still two blocks east. The kitchen door took the second and the service stair took the third. The fourth put her at the wire-glass window of the electrical room, the fifth put her inside it, and she pulled every breaker in the box by feel.

The sixth took her to the linen shelf. The seventh took her and Jericho three blocks east to a loading dock. She left him on his side with his glasses folded into his shirt, and came off that dock tasting pennies. The eighth put her in the office, eight feet up, on the crown molding.

She crouched there with the goggles on while the second ran out. Below her the room lay open in green and gray, and she read it like a wall: the knife nine feet down and two feet left, the desk, the chandelier chain, the whole room a route instead of a fight. She had spent four years going around things, and from up here going around was the answer.

There were two things down there and one second to spend on them. The knife was the near one. The other was past the desk, where a man who had sat in a chair all night was on his hands and knees at the plinth with his face turned up into the light.

She picked Ben.

She came off the molding onto the man with the knife and put him into the carpet with her whole weight, and the ninth had Ben out from under him and across the room before his hands understood they were empty. The chandelier she did not strictly need, and took anyway. It hung there and swung where she wanted, and the tenth dropped her off it onto the two men under the doorway, who went down without ever seeing what arrived. Then Battlea, off the wall and onto the desk, her hostage left blinking. Eleven.

The emergency circuit came up yellow and half-hearted. The chair by the desk lay on its side and empty, the ties cut through, and on the plinth the light had changed color while she was not watching it.

“Jericho?” Ben asked, on his feet with his back to the window.

“Got him,” Tess said. “Data drive?”

“Downloaded.” He had it closed in his fist.

“Then we’re leaving.”

“Hands up!” The near door had re-formed into a dozen men with weapons leveled.

There were two ways out of that office. One of them ran through those guns. The other was a four-foot hole in the west glass with thirty feet of cold night under it and a fountain still running this late in the year. She took Ben’s collar in one hand and Leah’s in the other.

“Now!”

They went through together and the night took them. Fifteen feet down she left them and came back up through the glass for Victor, who had stopped at the frame with the drop in front of him, doing the honest math of a man with nothing left in his hands.

“Trust me,” she said, and took his sleeve, and jumped them both off the sill.

After that it was only parkour, which is the art of refusing the straight line down. She did not stop the fall, because nobody stops a fall. She bent it. Two jumps put the four of them sideways across the drive, the next took the worst of the speed off the angle, the last dropped them into three feet of green water at a slant, and the fountain did what water does with anything handed to it at a slant.

She surfaced last, ears ringing, mouth full of pennies, hands not willing. Victor got her over the rim.

* * *

The alley ran three blocks east, and they reached it at a limp, soaking, in the first cold snap of the year. It had arrived that week and meant it.

“What the hell just happened?” Victor asked the alley generally.

“Gloom Girl happened,” Ben said.

“That was insane,” Victor said, and sat down heavily on somebody’s milk crate.

“That was fun,” Tess said.

Leah wrung a pint of fountain out of the ruined red dress and laughed.

Firas came into the alley mouth on foot, slower than she had ever seen him move, like a man carrying something breakable. One flat hand stayed pressed over his ribs the whole way. He had walked it. Walking was the only way any of them ever went.

“Everyone alive?”

“Mostly,” Ben said.

“Jericho?”

“At the extraction point,” Tess said. She got the words out in the right order, privately proud of it. “Unconscious but breathing. Got all his research too.”

The back door of the row house opened, and a woman in her sixties came out into the wet in a bathrobe and slippers, arms folded tightly.

“Go Squad?”

Nobody answered her. In four years nobody had ever had to.

“I knew it. I told my daughter you operated in this neighborhood.” She got a phone out of the bathrobe pocket. “Can I get a picture? She won’t believe me otherwise.”

“Ma’am, we really need to—”

“Please? Just one? You saved my nephew last month. The thing with the guys and the—” Her hand made a small circle in the air that finished the sentence for her. “You know.”

Leah crossed the alley in the wrecked dress, stood next to the woman, and put her head close while the phone came up unsteadily and did what phones do.

“Thank you,” she said, and gave the rest of it to all five of them. “For everything you do. We see you. All of us. We see you.”

Then she went back in. The screen door shut, and the latch eventually caught on the second try.

“That was weird,” Victor said.

“That was nice,” Tess said.

“Then let’s get out of here before—”

The light came up behind them, red and gold, across the whole east face of the house. Tess felt it in her back teeth and in the marrow of both shins, and she knew it, because it was hers. Space folding, run backward: an arrival with no leaving in front of it.

“What—”

Harding Kain came out through the front wall of his own house at a walk. The stone stayed stone and he came through it regardless, without a door and without a hole. Tess knew what she was looking at. That was space folded and let go, the same trick her own body did, done to a building by a thing sold to her as a clock. The Heart sat sunk in the middle of his chest, red, then gold, then red, patient about it. His eyes flickered rapidly between one state and another. He looked up at the sky for a while, unhurried, a man checking the weather before a drive. Then he turned his head three blocks east and found them in the dark, and smiled, and walked off through the side of an apartment building.

“We need to call someone,” Ben said.

“Who?” Leah asked.

“The cops? The cops who work for him?” Tess had her phone out already, holding it in both wet hands with absolutely nowhere to send it.

It rang. Unknown number.

“Hello?”

“No better place than here,” Ruth said in her ear.

Four years she had answered that call in a gym and on a rooftop and once with her mouth full, and it had never made her want to sit on the ground. Gloom Girl was a name she had picked at nineteen to be funny about the worst thing in her life. Standing in an alley in wet armor with her hands shaking, she decided she wanted a different one.

Ruth did not wait for the response.

“I saw what just happened. Get somewhere safe. I’m sending help.”

The line went. Then the air developed an edge it had not had, and something the size of a bus stopped being invisible forty feet up, and the engine pitch her ribs had been reading arrived at her ears. A ramp came down. Ruth stood at the top of it in full tactical gear, matte black and armored, none of which anybody had seen her wear.

“Need a ride?”

## Chapter 23

The ramp came up and took the alley. Firas did not board. He had walked three blocks on a shotgunned torso and would be useless in the air, so he took the drives from Ben’s fist and turned east toward the dock. He looked up once while the ramp closed and she made herself hold it. He asked nothing, which was worse than the four questions coming up the aisle behind her. The hold smelled of ozone and webbing. They came up dripping, Leah barefoot in the wreck of the red dress, Tess tasting pennies and not saying so, Victor holding his ribs like Firas held his. Four years of running on her callouts, and now they waited in a transport to be told what she was. “Sit,” she said. “We need to move fast.”

They sat. Victor took the bench nearest the door, the seat you choose when you think you may need it. “Okay,” he said. “What the hell is happening?”

“Long version or short version?”

“Medium version,” Ben said. “With explanations.”

“You know how your powers stopped working after the docks?”

“Yeah,” Leah said.

“They were never yours to begin with.” Nobody moved for a second and a half.

“Explain,” Victor said.

“You’ve been enhanced. Temporal manipulation distributed through drone networks. Someone with actual powers was amplifying you. Making you faster, stronger, more accurate. You thought it was you. It wasn’t.”

Ben’s color went. She had watched that drain a hundred times in an ER, where it meant blood leaving the face for somewhere it was needed more. He was recalculating four years of tactics built on a lie, every plan in which a man could clear thirty feet. “The whole time?” he said.

“Every fight. Every mission. You were being helped.”

“By who?” Tess said.

The sentence had no pronoun in it. She had built it that way, out loud, alone, twice. “By someone dying to keep you safe,” she said. “Someone who thought giving you powers was worth their life.”

“That’s not an answer,” Victor said.

“It’s the answer you’re getting.” She did not soften it. “What matters is the enhancement stopped because the person providing it nearly died. They’re in treatment now. Recovering. And once they’re stable, the drones will resume operations.”

Tess’s head came up on the last word. “Drones,” she said.

“FAERIS.” The question reached Tess’s face and stopped. “Yes.”

“I thought those were just surveillance.”

“They were that too.”

“Who are you people?” Ben asked.

“CADENS. Cataclysm Activity Detection and Engagement Network Sentry. Shadow government organization. International black budget funding. We monitor and respond to cosmic-level threats that normal governments can’t handle.”

“Like Kain,” Victor said.

“Like Kain. Like the artifacts. Like the things that left them here.” She put the mansion feed up on the bulkhead. “He used the Tamois Heart. Integrated it. He’s transforming into something else. Getting bigger. Stronger. If we don’t stop him in the next few hours, he’ll either die or become something worse.”

“What’s worse?” Leah asked.

“Fully transformed. Unstoppable. The kind of thing that takes armies to bring down.” She heard Tess laugh, badly. “A monster with a grudge.”

The comms panel opened and a woman read coordinates through it. “HAZARD to command. Target is mobile. Heading northeast through the city. Moving fast. Smashing through buildings, vehicles, everything. Trail of destruction.”

The map replaced the feed. Caledonia in gray, the twin rivers paler, a red thread stitching itself northeast a block at a time and slowing nowhere. “Where’s he going?” Ben asked.

“Nowhere. Everywhere.” She let them look. “He’s razing Caledonia. Block by block.”

“So we slow him down,” Victor said.

“If we can.” Ben pushed. “Who?”

The honest answer was a name and she did not give it, and the withholding cost what she expected. “First, we need intel. Jericho knows how the Heart works. How Kain’s abilities function. What his limitations are.” She turned toward the cockpit. “Set down at the extraction point. We’re picking up our prisoner.”

* * *

Firas had gotten him upright and put a jacket on him, because Firas is constitutionally unable to leave anybody on cold concrete. Ruth came down the ramp in gear he had never seen her own, did not look at him longer than she could afford, and that was the second thing she spent that hour. Jericho sat against the wall zip-tied, awake now, glasses on, terrified. She crouched to his level automatically, as she had ten thousand times. In the ER that was a kindness. In armor it read as something else, and she let it. “Dr. Jericho,” she said. “We need to talk.”

“I don’t know who you are,” he said. “But I’m not—I didn’t—Kain made me—”

“Save it.” His file had taken her four minutes at eleven thousand feet, and that was generous. “Resonance calibration. Cellular integration protocols. Temporal manipulation theories. You’ve been helping him weaponize the Heart for six weeks.”

“He kidnapped me from the museum!”

“And then you kept working. Enthusiastically.” His mouth opened and produced nothing. “You wanted to understand it. Crack the code. Publish the breakthrough of the century.” She had been awake past thirty hours. Under a government office building a person lay in a bed with a needle taped into their arm, and that had burned off what she kept in reserve for frightened men. “Here’s what’s going to happen,” she said. “You’re going to tell us everything. How the Heart works. How to stop the integration. How to prevent this from happening again. And then you’re going into protective custody until we clean up this mess.”

“And if I don’t?”

“Then Kain wakes up. And the first person he’s going to blame for his transformation is the scientist who told him it would make him a god.”

He took less time over it than she had allowed. “Protective custody sounds great,” he said. She stood, and her knees reported in. She was not proud of how efficiently that had worked, and she noticed she was not, and put the noticing away with everything else.

* * *

They flew north with him strapped in across from her team, and he was better at this than she wanted. “The Heart isn’t technology,” he said. “It’s organic. A seed from something vast. It integrates with biological systems, enhances them. Kain thought he was gaining power. Really he was being consumed.”

“Can it be reversed?” Ruth asked.

“No. Once integration begins, it’s irreversible. The Heart is rewriting his biology.” He turned the tablet around. “Massive muscle growth. Bone density increasing exponentially. He’s becoming huge. Strong. Durable.”

“So he’s just . . . big and strong?” Leah said.

“Exactly. Like the enhanced officers you’ve fought before, but scaled up. Fifteen feet tall, maybe more. Strong enough to tear through concrete. Durable enough that conventional weapons barely scratch him.” He scrolled, and the next frame was worse. “But there’s a problem. The integration is accelerating. His body is rejecting the transformation even as it’s completing. He’s dying. Fast.”

“How fast?” Ruth asked.

“Hours. Maybe twelve at most. The cellular stress is catastrophic. Eventually his biological mass will fail and the Heart will consume what’s left.” She knew the shape of that disease. She had lab notes on it and a pale blue vial four hundred feet underground, and kept that off her face.

“So he dies anyway,” Victor said.

“Or completes the transformation first.” Jericho pushed his glasses up with a bound wrist. “Becomes fully integrated. Permanently enhanced. A twenty-foot monster that won’t stop.”

“What are the odds he completes before he dies?” Ben asked.

“Based on his current acceleration? Sixty percent. Maybe higher if he continues using his abilities.”

“So we have to stop him before he completes,” Ben said. “How?”

“You can’t,” Jericho said. “He’s fifteen feet tall and growing. Stronger than anything you’ve faced. More durable. There’s no conventional weapon that works on something that size.”

“Then what do we use?” Victor asked.

“Another Temporalist. Someone who can manipulate time. Slow him down. Freeze him. Create windows where he’s vulnerable.”

Ben got there in one step and did not need the pause the others took.

“The person who’s been enhancing us.”

“They’re recovering,” Ruth said. “Not cleared for field operations.”

“But they could do it?” Ben said. “Stop Kain?”

“Theoretically.” The word tasted like a coin. “At significant cost.”

Victor stood up in a moving aircraft, which nobody is supposed to do. “The person enhancing you,” he said. “They’re at your facility. Recovering. And now you’re asking them to fight a temporal god.” Every one of those sentences was correct. That was the trouble with Victor. “Who are they?”

“Someone who’s been protecting you since the beginning. Someone who thinks you’re worth dying for. That’s all you need to know.”

“That’s not enough,” Victor said.

“It’s going to have to be.”

“This is insane. This whole time, someone’s been watching us. Helping us. Like a ghost.” Tess had both hands flat on her knees. “And we don’t even get to know their name?” Ruth let the engine noise stand in for an answer, a thing she had learned to do lately.

“That doesn’t help,” Victor said. He came up the aisle and stopped nearer than she would have permitted anyone else. “You know them.”

“Yes.”

“And you’re not telling us because they asked you not to.”

“Yes.”

“Even though we have a right to know.”

She said nothing at all. It was the worst answer available and the only honest one, and she let it sit in front of four people who had gone into gunfire on her word for four years while she sat dark in a bunker and watched.

Leah had been silent a long time, still barefoot. “How long?”

“How long what?”

“How long do they have? If they fight Kain.” Ruth looked at the deck plates, and Leah did not let her have it. “How long, Ruth?”

“Weeks. Maybe less.” The hold went completely quiet under the engine note, and the red thread added another block.

“Then we need another option,” Ben said. “We can’t ask someone to die for us.”

“It’s not your choice,” Ruth said. “It’s theirs.”

The panel opened again before anybody could take it apart. “Command, target is downtown. Main Street. He’s destroying everything.”

“Then what do we do?” Tess asked.

The deck tilted and settled, the landing lights came up green, and Ruth was already unbuckling. “Wait here.”

* * *

Bourn was on the pad, hands behind her back, her suit unwrinkled at an hour when nobody’s should be. “Ryu says she’s awake,” she said. “Asking what happened. Saw the feeds from the mansion.”

“Does she know about Kain?”

“She knows.” Bourn’s delivery did not change. “And she’s already asking when she can deploy.”

Both sentences had come off a status line hours old. Ruth heard the timestamp under it. Awake was an event and not a condition; the girl had surfaced, asked for the one thing that would kill her, and gone back down without keeping any of it. “How long does she have if she fights?”

“Ryu estimates days. Maybe a week if we’re lucky. The temporal strain of freezing something that size, that strong . . .” Bourn let that hang. “It’ll accelerate everything.”

Ruth looked back at the ramp, where four wet people sat in the dark. “Bring them to the briefing room,” she said. “They deserve better than sitting in a transport.”

“And you?”

“Give me ten minutes,” Ruth said. “Then I’ll join you.” Bourn let it go. Ruth took the left-hand corridor, and the ten minutes went where they went.

* * *

The briefing room ran two floors and a wall of live feeds, thirty-one crises on four continents, and a gallery of consoles staffed by people who hardly looked up. Tess turned slowly in place with her arms out from her sides. Ben found the meta-human tracking board and read it standing. “This is insane,” Tess said. “They’ve been monitoring meta-humans worldwide. Hundreds of them.”

Victor had found the personnel wall, and on it a file with his own face and legal name. Leah sat in a chair that adjusted itself under her. “They know everything about us.” She made a small approving noise and adjusted it slightly further. “I could get used to this.”

Four cases came in on a cart and opened flat. Matte black armor cut to measurements nobody had ever given anybody; a collapsible staff; a grapple spool; a belt of smoke and flash; a pair of boots with the derby laces already run through them by somebody who had noticed. Bourn let them look. “You’ve seen what we do. You know what’s at stake.”

“And you want us to join,” Ben said.

“I’m offering.” She did not oversell it. “You’re free to walk away.”

“Just like that?”

“Just like that. What you’ve seen here stays here.”

“Can we think about it?” Ben asked.

“Of course.”

Victor closed the lid of his case with one finger. “Hell of a sales pitch,” he said. “What—”

The room’s light went amber. The gallery came alive, twenty voices layering into one, and a technician spoke evenly over them. “Director Bourn, we have a situation. Downtown Caledonia. Multiple cosmic-level threat signatures.”

The main wall took the feed. Kain stood twenty feet in the middle of Main Street, still growing while they watched, skin gone translucent, the Heart burning red and gold under it like a coal in a paper bag. Behind him came four men in torn CCPD uniforms, seven feet each, unhurried. A bus lay on its side across two lanes. The camera was mounted high on a bank and did not look away, and Ruth watched three of them not make it. “Jesus Christ,” Ben said.

“Response authorization required,” the technician said. “Waiting on clearance from—”

“Override that,” Mack said. “Deploy all available units. NOW.”

“Sir, protocol requires—”

“I said NOW!”

Seven minutes to muster. Eleven to the site. Ruth did that math, and so did the four people beside her, who had spent four years arriving in ninety seconds because nobody ever asked them for clearance. They looked at each other. Ben to Victor, Victor to Leah, Leah to Tess, Tess back to Ben, and no one said one word about it. Ben picked his case up off the cart without hurrying about it. “We’re not waiting.”

“The suits aren’t calibrated—” a technician said.

Tess already had her belt in one hand and was not looking at anybody about it. “Don’t care.”

“You can’t,” Bourn said. “Without powers, you’re—”

“We’ve been without powers before,” Ben said. He had the armor out of the case already. “We’ll manage.”

“This isn’t street gangs,” Bourn said. “This is—”

“Our city,” Victor said. “Our people. Our fight.”

“You’re making a mistake. You’ll die out there.”

“Probably,” Tess said. “But at least we’ll die doing something. Not waiting for authorization.” They took the cases and went, and their footfalls got smaller very quickly down the corridor.

“Dr. Carter—”

“I’m going with them.”

Bourn’s chin came up. She looked at the corridor, then at Ruth, and said the name low, sure it would work. “Ahdia—”

“Ryu can handle it.”

Then Ruth was gone too, and Director Harriet Bourn stood alone in her briefing room, thirty-one crises on the wall behind her and one in front, watching five people with no powers between them run at a twenty-foot god because nobody else would go without a signature.

## Chapter 24

The rain was going up.

She stood on a street she half knew, and the rain rose off the pavement in long silver threads and climbed back into the sky. A pigeon crossed the intersection tail-first, wings folding neatly where they should have spread. On the corner a man’s coffee jumped out of the air and poured itself back into his paper cup, and he lowered it unsipped and walked briskly away in reverse.

She reached for the Seed and nothing answered, the reach coming back empty, like knocking at a house you had already moved out of. Then the cold in her chest moved. It was not a voice and it did not use words, but it had a direction and it leaned.

The feeling said: Wrong direction.

The whole street ran backward, a tape rewound by somebody with nowhere to be, and under her hoodie the blue light pulsed in time with her heart, and every pulse took the world back another second. The pulse was hers, her light and her heartbeat, running without permission asked.

You only ever pull. Slow. Stop. Backward. What if you pushed?

She tried to stop the street instead. She grabbed for the rain and the pigeon and the man with his coffee, held the whole rewinding block the only way she knew how, and for a moment everything hung obediently still. Then the moment ended because moments do, and the street went on running backward through her hands. The feeling: You can’t freeze moments. Only delay them.

The pavement quit pretending to hold her, and she fell. She fell through stacked moments, frozen flat like photographs in a box: the warehouse caught mid-blast with its fire solid and orange, Gloom Girl hung above the bridge with her arms wide, the ambush with its bullets parked in the air, patient as pins. Every one of them was hers, and every one of them had waited, and none had ever stopped waiting.

She landed in a hallway. Blue doors ran down the left wall, red doors down the right, and the hallway went out of sight both ways. She understood, as you understand rules in dreams, that she had only ever opened blue.

One blue door stood open on Montana. Pine, woodsmoke, the campground, herself at eight years old on a log with a stick she was too proud of. The girl looked up without surprise.

“You’ve been stuck here. We all have.”

The fire popped and its sparks went up slowly and stayed up.

“You keep looking back. What if you looked forward instead?”

“I’m scared,” Ahdia said.

“Good. That means you’re moving.”

Behind her, on the right-hand wall, a red door had swung open. Warm air came through it, and light, and a low sound that could have been traffic or blood or applause. Everything she had spent years arranging her life to avoid was on the other side of that door, and all of it was alive and none of it was safe.

Something came down the hallway then. It was too big for the hallway, and the hallway held it anyway. It moved slowly and it was old and it was curious. It was not hostile and it was not kind. It looked at her like she had looked at the photographs, and then it passed and every door shivered in its frame.

She put her hand out for the red door and everything cracked: red light poured through the seams, hot and forward and fast, and she was spread across every possible moment at once, and all of them were moving, every one, forward.

Another voice answered from nowhere near the warmth, cold and final, and it did not belong to the Seed.

“Wake up.”

* * *

She came off the bed gasping. White ceiling, monitors, a room loudly disagreeing with itself. The IV was out of her arm and she had no memory of tearing it.

“Hey, hey—” Ryu was there, glasses crooked, wearing a week without sleep. “You’re okay. You’re—”

She swung her legs off the bed anyway.

“Whoa, easy.” His hands hovered uselessly and landed nowhere. “You’ve been out for twenty-six hours.”

“What happened?”

“You passed out. Ruth said—” He caught himself on the name. “You were in pretty bad shape when they brought you in.”

“Where’s Ruth?”

He looked at the door before he looked at her. The look took too long and he knew it, and knowing it made it longer.

“Where’s Ruth?”

“She left. With the others.”

“Left where?”

He did not answer. The wall monitor behind him had been answering the whole time, and she saw it now. Downtown, from a rooftop feed: smoke, sirens crawling faintly between the buildings, and in the middle of Main Street a shape that read as a man until the scale resolved. Twenty feet of him and more, still person-shaped and wrong everywhere, translucent at the edges, too many joints working, and in his chest the Heart pulsing like a second sun.

“What the hell is that?”

“Kain.” Ryu said it flatly, a diagnosis with a name. “He used the Heart. The Tamois Heart. He’s been growing for the past hour.”

On the feed, the shape put a fist through the corner of a building without slowing down.

“Ruth and the team went after him,” Ryu said. “About thirty minutes ago.”

“They don’t have powers,” she said.

“I know.”

“They’re going to die.”

He did not argue. In a room full of bad information, that was the worst piece.

Her bare feet found the floor. The cold of it climbed her ankles and she stood up into the sway and let it pass through her.

“Ahdia—”

“I need the treatment.” She was already moving. “Now.”

Ryu got to the door ahead of her and put his back against it. “You can’t. The treatment isn’t stable yet.”

“Using your powers will—”

“Give me the treatment.”

The door opened behind him. Bourn came in wearing the suit she had stood in while five people ran at Kain without her, and she read the room in one pass.

“You don’t understand,” Bourn said. “You need eight more doses. Three weeks between each one. That’s six months.”

On the monitor, Kain took a sedan off the street by its roof and threw it through the front of a bank.

“I don’t have six months,” Ahdia said.

“You collapsed at the docks. Cellular degradation. You’ve been unconscious for twenty-six hours.” Ryu dealt the facts out with open hands, a man laying down cards he hated. “Ruth developed a treatment. We administered the first dose. You’re stabilized, but still severely damaged. Your cells need time to integrate each dose. If you take them too soon—”

“We’re out of time,” Bourn said.

She had come in to say no. The whole shape of her had come in to say no, and everyone in the room heard the sentence change sides. The cabinet stood against the far wall, and nobody stopped Ahdia from opening it. Eight autoinjectors sat in their foam like rounds in a magazine. She carried them to the bed and lined them up in a row and looked at them until they were only a procedure.

Then she closed her eyes and found the Seed and pushed. The light through the hospital gown went red. It had never been red. Blue was the whole history of it, blue at the warehouse, blue over the bridge, blue in her sleep. The cold stayed the same cold. Only the direction was new.

The first dose went into her thigh. The room dropped into slow motion around her, the alarms stretching into long low notes, and the cold poured outward from the Seed into every corridor of her.

“She’s accelerating!” The shout arrived pulled out of shape, low and long.

“She’s giving herself the three weeks!”

The second dose, the third. Her cells took the payment as fast as she could offer it, and the red haze came up at the edges of the room like weather.

Ryu had the tablet up against the glass of her speed, writing in block capitals:

RATIO = 1:2000

His fingers counted along with it, too slow and hopelessly behind her, and his pen tapped a metronome she heard as if from underwater. The fourth dose went in, and the alarms stretched past hearing.

TOO FAST = CELLS BURN

Somewhere beyond the red, Ryu went clinical. “Energy expenditure is exceeding regeneration. It’s a net loss.”

The fifth. The taps were fading steadily now, wall by wall, a man knocking from farther and farther away.

“It’s not like there’s a dial I can turn!” The shout went out of her into air gone thick. “Or a needle gauge to monitor!”

TRY 1:1500

The sixth dose, the seventh. The haze stopped being haze and became the color of the room, and somewhere out past it the tapping no longer mattered.

“Even at perfect ratio, she’s only getting fifty percent max,” Ryu said. He was talking to Bourn now. “It’s a zero-sum problem.”

SLOW DOWN

She never read it. The eighth went to her thigh, same press, same sound, and nothing went in. Her fist took it under the gown. The world came back at full speed, sound first, then the shaking in her hands. Fifteen seconds had passed on the wall clock.

“Did it work?” Ahdia said.

Ryu was already at the screen. “First few integrated. Last ones barely worked. Maybe thirty percent total regeneration.”

“Is that enough?” Bourn asked.

“I don’t know.”

Ahdia stood up in the hospital gown. Her legs held, barely, and she let nobody see what that cost.

“It has to be.”

She reached for the Seed and was gone.

The monitors flatlined over an empty bed. The cabinet hung open, its foam stripped bare. Ryu looked at Bourn, and Bourn looked at the place where a patient had been, and then they both turned to the feeds, because the feeds were where she would land, and waiting was the only procedure left.

* * *

The FAERIS drone’s light came first, dropping down through the smoke over the intersection, white and steady. Then space folded. It creased at the middle of Main Street, held for a breath and opened. Ahdia stood in the intersection. Hospital gown, bare feet on broken glass, hair loose, hands spread from her sides, and through the thin fabric the light of the Seed was blue again.

Kain froze mid-step at twenty-three feet, fist raised. His other hand stopped closed around the teenagers it had caught, and they hung inside the cage of his fingers, alive. The four giants in torn CCPD uniforms froze with him. Edgar with Victor in his grip. Ogden, his fist an inch from Leah. Philips and Orlansky mid-charge, boot soles off the ground.

Everything else kept moving. The smoke moved, the sirens moved, loose paper moved along the street, and the civilians ran the moment they understood that running worked again, pouring away down the side streets without looking back. The Go Squad did not run. Ben pulled Leah up off the asphalt without taking his eyes from the intersection. Victor stayed against a wall, an arm across his ribs. Tess stayed on her knees where Ogden’s shadow had been. Ruth stood beside them, the bruise across her throat gone darker.

“Is that—” Ben said.

“Ahdia,” Ruth whispered.

The name barely crossed the ruin of her throat, and it landed in front of four people who had grieved that name for somebody else’s sake. Nobody asked her to repeat it. What they saw, and could not stop seeing, was this: the girl who should be dead, small at the center of a wrecked street, holding five giants still. Her arms shook. A thread of blood ran from one nostril, and she did not wipe it. The blue light beat through the gown with her heart, and every line of her body reported what the stillness was costing, and she paid it standing, and nothing she held moved.

## Chapter 25

The drone came down through the smoke to her shoulder. “Go,” she said, and bumped it off with her hip. “Go on to your mama.”

It crossed the intersection and did not leave. It settled over Tess’s shoulder, close enough to touch. Tess went rigid.

Nobody spoke. Four people who had buried this woman stood in the wreck of Main Street and looked at her. Nobody looked away. She broke first. Ruth watched it happen: shoulders up, chin sideways, a hand rising in a wave.

“Uh . . . hi, guys, first-time caller, long-time fan, I’m Ahdia, Firas’s big sister.”

Firas was east of here with a bandage under his shirt and no idea, and Ruth had chosen that too.

Leah’s fist tightened in Ben’s sleeve. Ruth read the panic. “What the hell is going on? Are we dead? We’re dead, right?”

“Definitely not,” Victor said.

“Oh yeah? How do you know?”

“Because if we were dead, this would be a tropical beach and you’d be wearing . . .” He let his hand finish it slowly, a pass at the armor. “. . . not that.”

Ruth crossed to her, and every step gave less of the girl and more of the patient: bare feet on broken glass, sweat through the gown, the blue light beating faster than a heart. Blood ran freely from one nostril. Both hands shook badly. She should not have been vertical.

“We’re not dead,” Ruth said. Three words out of a bruised throat, and they cost more than three should. “How are you feeling? You took the rest of the treatment, didn’t you?”

“Yeah, what gave it away?”

“Well, you’re here at all for one.” Ruth watched the blood and not the face. “But couple that with this demonstration of this whole new ability . . .”

“Oh, about that, yeah, turns out I could do it all along.” She tipped her head at the giants without looking. “Fun fact, I’m not actually freezing Marky Mark and the Funky Bunch over there. I’m doing the other thing. Who knew?”

Ruth had let the jokes past and kept reading the patient. Now she understood what she had been standing inside. Kain was not stopped, and Edgar and Ogden and Philips and Orlansky were not stopped, and nothing over there was touched, and the difference was here, in her own arms and the four beside her.

“You’re speeding us up?” Tess said.

“Oh, yeah, look at you, Gloom Girl, penny for the pretty lady,” Ahdia said.

“So why haven’t you?”

“To be fair, I just figured out that I could about fifteen minutes ago.” She rocked back on her heels and made it look deliberate. “Would have gotten here sooner but I had to find one of those guys to hitch a ride. They do a really good job of cleaning up their toys when they’re not using them, over there at CADENS HQ.”

The word landed in a street where nobody had heard it, and Tess picked it out of the air like a stone and came off her knees, and did not stop.

“You knew about her?”

“This whole time?”

“You’ve been working with CADENS.”

“You knew. You’ve been lying.”

“I—” Ruth started.

“How long?” Victor said. His hand slid down the rebar until it found balance. The knuckles went pale. “How long have you been lying to us?”

“Okay, whoa, hold on.” Ahdia put both palms up, and the shake was visible. “This is not on Ruth. She didn’t know about me until, like, two weeks ago? Maybe three? Time’s been weird.”

One day. The docks last night, the labs this morning. She let the wrong number lie where it fell. Correcting it would buy nobody anything. The honest answer was worse, because one day had been enough.

“And she’s been trying to help me not die, so maybe we could table the whole trust-issues intervention until after we deal with Godzilla over there?”

“This isn’t—”

“It’s not her fault,” Ahdia said. Blood had reached her lip. She wiped it with the back of her hand and looked at the hand. “CADENS approached her. She didn’t ask for this. I’m the one who’s been—”

She stopped. Then she picked it up again, evenly, and gave it to all four.

“Look, I know what you’re thinking. Why didn’t she tell you? Why all the secrets? And the answer is . . . me. I asked her not to. Because I’m Firas’s sister, the one who couldn’t even leave her apartment for dinner, and you guys—you’re out here actually doing things. Saving people. Being, like, actual heroes.”

The sway went through her and out again and she rode it standing, barely.

“And I didn’t want you to know that someone like me had powers when you didn’t. That felt . . . wrong. Like the universe screwed up the distribution or something. Like I didn’t deserve it.”

The nosebleed had found her chin, and nothing in the street moved but her mouth.

“But you guys? You’ve been doing this with no powers at all. Just . . . guts and gear and giving a damn. So yeah, I kept it secret. And yeah, Ruth helped me do that. At first it was about being painfully, audaciously, anti-social and my anger towards Firas but then it became—”

The sentence went out from under her. Her hand stayed up where the rest of it went.

“I started wanting you guys to look all enhanced and amazing because . . . because even without me, you guys are, well . . . super.”

“We trusted you.” Ben said it.

Four years of tactics rested on that sentence, and Ruth felt the floor tilt. “You still can—”

“Can we?” Leah was upright now, holding her ribs, and the second one came sharper. “Because it seems like you’ve been keeping a lot of secrets.”

Nobody was asking Ahdia anything. Ruth had been the voice these people went through doors on, and she felt them stop hearing it that way.

“We don’t have time for this!” Victor said. He was pointing at Ahdia, not at Ruth. Her knees were traveling, the blood came steadily onto the gown, and the light in her chest pulsed out of sequence and skipped. She smiled at them with red teeth.

“I can hold this,” she said. “But not forever. So if someone wants to, I don’t know, make a plan? That would be great.”

All four of them turned to Ruth. Nobody offered her anything. They looked at her because she built the plans, because nobody else was standing, and because a liar with a plan beat four honest people without one.

“Wait.” They had been treating the stillness as a wall. It was not a wall. Kain stood inside his own second like a photograph of a man, fist up, and they stood outside it for as long as she could give them. “So you’re saying we’re all moving at unimaginable speed while they stand still in normal time?”

Ahdia nodded, and the nod nearly took her down. Ben went to the giants and read the gaps quickly. Leah let go of her ribs slowly. Victor’s grip shifted until the weight sat right. Tess came last, because Tess had to carry her fury the whole distance to the same place, and it cost her.

“Then let’s run.”

## Chapter 26

The asphalt gave her feet everything it had been saving up all night: cold, wet, and studded with small sharp things. She was barefoot in an intersection on Main Street, in a gown the color of a hospital’s idea of calm. Blood came down her arm and onto the pale blue and spread there freely.

She watched it happen to the girl in the gown. That was the trick and it had never once failed her. You stand slightly to the left of yourself and you do the voiceover. The girl in the gown was in serious trouble, and somebody really ought to help her.

Kain stood twenty-three feet up and was not done. His skin had gone translucent, so the light of the thing sunk into his sternum came out through the whole torso, red sliding to amber and sliding back, a coal in a paper lantern. His bones moved while he stood still. They made the sound a house makes settling, sped up and turned up, and every so often the street got a little smaller around him. He had left “human” behind somewhere around the fifteen-foot mark.

Four of them waited at his heels in the ruins of CCPD uniforms. Ogden, with the scar running cleanly through his left eyebrow. Philips, thick through the neck, whose hands she had watched close on Ruth’s throat on a monitor four hundred feet underground. Edgar, bald, knuckles already wet. Orlansky, the young one, eyes too wide for the work. Ryu had put their faces on a tablet and talked through them rapidly, and now here they all were, off the screen and out in the weather.

“Well,” Kain said. The word arrived slowly, with a delay in it, like thunder that had to travel. “This is unexpected.”

“Yeah,” she said. “I’ve been getting that a lot tonight.”

The two kids were out of the intersection. Somebody in black had carried them off during the run and she never saw who. Her hands shook badly enough to be interesting. She let them.

“You look like you escaped from psychiatric.” His head tipped, and the movement traveled unhurriedly out to the far end of him. “Or possibly intensive care. Hard to tell with that fashion choice.”

She plucked the gown out at the hip and gave it a small model’s turn, badly.

“It’s called ‘hospital chic.’” The turn nearly put her on the ground. “Very in right now.”

“She’s delirious,” Philips said. His hand had drifted casually to rest near his holster. “Blood loss. Look at her.”

“I’m looking,” Kain said. “I’m looking at the girl who should be dead. The one who took a bullet to the chest. Who fell three stories into an explosion. Who by all rights should be a smear on warehouse concrete.”

“I got better,” she said.

“Clearly.” He let the word sit and then leaned heavily on the rest of it. “But not much better. You’re barely standing.”

While he was enjoying himself she read the roofs. Gloom Girl lay flat along the bank cornice with her chin on the stone. Battlea crouched behind the overturned bus, weight forward on her toes. Night Knight had the fire escape above the pharmacy and a canister in each hand. Crimson Sable held a length of rebar low and easily in the alley mouth. Ruth stood at the corner in gear nobody in this city had ever seen, talking steadily into her collar, and all four of them moved when she spoke, and not one of them looked at her while they did it. Ahdia found that with her attention like a tongue finds a chipped tooth. She had absolutely no idea what she was looking at.

“I’ve had worse Tuesdays,” she said. “There was this one time I tried to make pad thai from scratch and ended up setting off the smoke alarm for like, six hours straight. My neighbor called the fire department. Twice. This is maybe top five bad Tuesdays. Top three if you count that thing you’re doing with your face.”

“You’re stalling,” Kain said.

“Am I?” She rocked back onto her heels and stayed up, mostly. “I thought I was making conversation. You know, pre-fight banter. It’s traditional. I’ve seen it in like, every action movie. The hero and the villain exchange quips. Usually the hero has better one-liners but I’m working with limited material here. Blood loss and exhaustion aren’t great for comedic timing.”

“You’re no hero,” Kain said.

“No,” she said. “I’m really, really not.”

It went by him like a breeze. He had come here to insult her and she had handed him the sentence cheerfully, and there was nothing in it for him at all. It was the truest thing she had said out loud in weeks.

The Seed was cold in her chest, as always. The other thing that arrived was warm, and it hurt worse.

Because he had stood in a room and told everybody in it that she had not made it, and then said the other part, the part about her being already dead inside, and she had heard all of it from behind a foam vault with her hand over her mouth. She had filed it away under closed, permanently. And here came the dent in the front quarter panel, faster than the street deserved, aimed at a thing the size of a church.

She kept her eyes where they were and did not let them travel.

“What are you?” Kain said. She gave him nothing, so he supplied it himself, generously. “A girl in a hospital gown who should be dead?”

“Something like that,” she said. “I’ve definitely had better days. My feet are cold. This gown doesn’t cover nearly enough. And I’m pretty sure I’m concussed. Or possibly having a stroke. Hard to tell the difference at this point.”

She smiled up at him with red teeth.

“But you’re about to have a really bad fifteen seconds.”

Kain moved two fingers, and the four of them came.

Getting angry on purpose used to be work. Now the anger lay right there on the top of everything, and she picked it up like a dropped set of keys, and the street stopped.

Sound went first. Then the cold came up her forearms and set its teeth in her elbows, and it cost her more than the last one had, because it always did now.

She walked. She went in among them, past Edgar’s shoulder, under Ogden’s arm, into a forest of enormous men leaning into a charge and going nowhere at all. She moved Ogden’s forearm gently, a few degrees left. She turned Edgar’s hip fractionally, until his weight sat wrong. Orlansky she left alone, because the kid was already going to miss. Philips she barely touched, since Philips was carrying the most speed and the rest of it would take care of itself.

Getting back out of the way took longer than everything else put together. Then she let go.

Saturday morning, a bowl of cereal going soft: the anvil, the tunnel painted onto a rock face, the coyote arriving at a wall with a fresh idea. Four very large men ran into each other in the middle of Main Street and made a sound like a filing cabinet coming off a truck. Ogden went down under Edgar. Philips took Orlansky off his feet, and the two of them slid into the base of a mailbox and stopped there.

The cold left her elbows and went off to do damage somewhere less convenient. Her nose was running. She did not look at her hand afterward.

Kain came around slowly, a foreboding weather system coming around, and the fist that had been full of teenagers a few minutes ago came down at her out of the dark.

She stopped the street.

Then she walked out of the strike path, ten feet, fifteen, in no particular hurry, around a hydrant and past the bus, and stood there with her arms folded to keep her hands from being visible. Everything had gotten remarkably heavy. Her legs had opinions. She let go anyway.

The fist finished its trip and took a bite out of the pavement where she had been standing, and Kain’s arm went in past the wrist. He stumbled forward onto a knee, and the whole block rang like a struck pipe.

Philips came up off the mailbox with his sidearm out.

“Stop—” Kain said, because nothing that size wants lead flying around the thing it is about to pick up.

She took the word out of the air along with everything else.

Getting the pistol away from a frozen man is mostly a problem of levers. She worked his fingers open one at a time, dropped the magazine into her palm and set it on the sidewalk, racked the slide, and let the round that came out of it hang there in the air like a wasp in amber. Then she put the empty weapon back into his hand, closed his fingers around it, and squared his grip up neatly like she was fixing a kid’s hold on a bat. She stepped clear. She let go.

Click. Click.

Philips looked at the gun with the honest bewilderment of a man whose entire personality had failed him.

Then her body quit arguing.

The gray came in from the edges of everything, and the intersection went narrow and soft, and she was suddenly down on her knees with her hands sliding off them. Air everywhere. None of it useful. She was drowning standing up in a street that was full of the stuff.

Kain did not swing. That was the genuinely frightening part, that he had learned something. He came in close, folded down, and closed a hand around her middle and lifted her off the ground, and then he squeezed. Her ribs creaked and complained and something in there let go with a noise she felt more than heard. She reached for the cold and the cold was a long way off with the lights out.

“Not so clever now,” Kain said.

Something moved at the far end of Main Street, past the barricade of dead traffic. Headlights, low and quick, coming the wrong way up a street the whole city had agreed to leave alone. A gray sedan, civilian, with a front-quarter dent nobody had ever gotten around to fixing.

The headlights kept coming.

“You . . .” she said.

Kain leaned down to hear it, delighted.

“you . . . had . . . enough?”

The sedan jumped a ramp from broken asphalt and crushed into him.

She pushed. She knew this one now, like a stove you have already put your hand on: red, going forward, expensive past discussing. She had done it in a hospital bed and she had done it standing in this street, and never for longer than it took, and never inside a fist with her ribs coming apart, and never with somebody else to drag along with her, and never on this little. It went anyway. It took her and the car and the idiot driver and shoved all three of them out ahead of the world.

Everything held. Steam stood up off the crumpled hood in a shape that had no business staying put. Glass hung in the air in a spray, each piece with its own little light in it. Kain’s face was caught partway through working something out, mouth open, eyebrows on their way up. The Tanks lay scattered wherever they had put themselves. The hood was folded against his shin, and the whole tableau sat there being nothing but a photograph of the loudest moment of her life.

The impact had done one useful thing before the world quit. It had taken Kain forward off his heels, and the hand around her had gone loose in the same instant everything stopped, so the fingers stood there in stone, not quite closed. She worked an arm out, and then a shoulder. She braced both feet against the thumb and shoved, and the rib that had let go earlier told her about it in a voice she could not turn down, and then she was out of the hand and falling. The pavement came up and took her hands and knees. Glass stood around her in the air, unbothered, and cut nothing on the way down.

The job wasn’t done. The driver, whoever they were, she needed to get them before the impact force arrived. She got up slowly and climbed to the driver door and did a double take.

It was her brother. It was Firas.

Of course it was him. He wouldn’t miss a kaiju even if he were dying in a hospital bed.

She got a fist into her brother’s jacket through the open window and brought him with her. He came out of the car sideways and landed hard on pavement that had stopped caring about time, rolled once, and came up on his hands with a dead earbud swinging at his collar, looking at a world where nothing dripped. He looked at the glass. He looked at the steam. Then he stopped looking at any of it, because she was standing there in a paper-thin gown, bleeding heavily.

And the voiceover cut out. She was not standing to the left of anybody now. She was in her own feet, on her own street, being looked at by her brother.

“Hey, Ahdia.”

“Hey, little bro.”

His mouth worked. There was a bandage showing at his collar and he was breathing wrong, and he was doing the thing Ruth does, running his eyes methodically over her ribs, her arm, her bare feet, filing injuries.

“You look—”

“Gonna stop you right there.” She held up a finger. “I just have a pet peeve when family you haven’t seen in a while immediately start with how fat or skinny you’ve gotten.”

“That’s not what I—” He stopped. He put a hand over his face and left it there, and when he took it down he had gone somewhere much worse. “I’m sorry. This . . . this is all because of me. I shouldn’t have come to your place, I should have just gone to the hospital.”

“I know,” she said.

She did not tell him it was okay, because it was not okay. What he had said in that room went through her like rounds through drywall and confirmed the whole case she had been building against herself since she was a kid. And he had driven his car at a kaiju anyway.

“We need to talk about it,” she said. She looked at the hanging glass rather than at him. “Later. When we’re with more polite company.”

“Later.” He nodded once, slowly.

“Later,” she said.

He was already pulling his jacket off, and then his shirt, and under the shirt was black armor, plates and matte panels and a harness gone shiny at the buckles from years of use.

“Why are you—”

“Habit.” He got the shoulder strap open with his teeth on one glove. “Wore it under my clothes. Just in case. Stupid, probably. But—”

“But you’re the guy who packs a go-bag,” she said. “Even when you’re benched.”

“Yeah.”

He got the last of it off and stood there in a t-shirt, thin, bandaged, a little ridiculous, holding out the only thing he owned that anybody had ever taken seriously.

“You can’t fight in a hospital gown.”

“Firas, you need—”

“I’m benched. You’re not.” He pushed it at her, and then he heard how that sounded and reached for something better, and what he came back with was a shrug and a small awful grin. “It’ll be loose. You’re smaller. But it’s better than nothing.”

She took it. It weighed more than she expected and smelled like her brother, sweat and cheap detergent and the inside of a gym bag. Lifting it hurt on the left side, low down, in a way that arrived late and then stayed. She held it up at arm’s length between them, considered it, considered him, and swung it down hard to slam it onto the frozen street.

She was already wearing it.

It sat too big in the shoulders, and the harness hung low on her hips, and there was a hard plate lying over the thing in her side that had let go, so that every breath now had to come in through it, and her feet were still bare on the cold pavement, and Firas made a sound like the beginning of a question and then did not ask it. Nobody explained anything. The street stayed stopped around the two of them, glass hanging, steam standing, everything patient.

“The team?”

“Positioned. Rooftops. Fire escapes. Ruth’s coordinating.” He was backing away toward the sidewalk with his eyes still on her, unarmored, wearing a t-shirt in a street with a monster in it, wholly untroubled by the arrangement. “Waiting for your signal.”

“Okay,” she said.

“Okay?”

“Yeah.”

She took her air in small careful helpings while he crossed. He went into the doorway of the sandwich place across the street and put his back against it, and there was nothing between him and the rest of the night but a door frame and his opinion of her.

She walked out to the middle of the intersection, into the space between the man on his knees and the four on the ground, in armor built for somebody else, breathing shallowly around the left side of herself, and let the whole street go.

Sound came back in a wave. Steam broke, glass fell, and Kain finished the thought his face had been halfway through.

“Let’s give them an opening.”

She pulled a breath that hurt everywhere it went and put everything she had left into her lungs instead of her legs.

“No better place than here!”

And it came back off the roofs, the other half of it, screamed down at her from the cornice and the fire escape and the alley and the wreck of the bus, and the Go Squad came down off the buildings, all of them, yelling.

## Chapter 27

They came off the buildings at him, four of them dropping out of four directions at once. One off the bank cornice, one off the fire escape above the pharmacy, one out of the alley mouth, one up over the roof of the dead bus. The fifth did not drop. She came in low across the pavement from the corner, at a dead run. The answer came down ahead of them, screamed raggedly and out of time with itself, the other half of whatever the girl had put into the air.

“No better time than now!”

Kain did not adjust his stance. Five people throwing themselves at something twenty-three feet tall priced out as suicide, and he had watched men buy it before, generally at the end and generally cheap.

The girl stood under it in black armor he had not seen her put on. It sat badly on her. Too wide at the shoulders, the sleeves eating her hands, the harness slung low, the collar gone black where the blood had run down into it. Her feet were still bare. Somewhere in the noise she had acquired a costume. He set it down without turning it over.

Then the drop stopped behaving like a drop. Between the second-floor windows and the pavement the four of them gathered speed the fall had never given them. The fifth ate ground at a rate no stride buys. And this time he could see where it was coming from. The girl swayed. Her hands shook inside the long sleeves, her jaw worked, and her breathing had gone shallow and quick and visible from forty feet away. Whatever this cost her, she was paying it openly. Then the Go Squad hit the street as blurs.

Battlea took Ogden at the hip like a car coming through an intersection, and Ogden left the ground. Crimson Sable landed on Philips with the rebar coming down two-handed, and the asphalt broke cleanly in a ring around them both. Edgar read Night Knight’s line and moved off it correctly, and Night Knight corrected mid-strike at an angle no shoulder should offer and caught him center mass. Orlansky got a guard up. Nightingale went through it as though it were a door somebody had left unlatched.

Gloom Girl hit nobody. She went out of the air halfway down, arrived neatly on the sidewalk beside a man in a t-shirt who had no business standing there, raised her middle finger at the whole intersection, and took them both out of it.

Four down. Five standing in a ring around the girl in the middle, and the girl had not thrown a punch.

“What are you?”

She gave him nothing. He watched her fail to answer and understood that she had nothing left to answer with.

Ogden came up out of his crater and Crimson Sable’s rebar found his temple on the way. Philips crawled toward the nearest pair of legs and went down under a low sweep, and Gloom Girl arrived on top of him out of clear air. Edgar surged at Nightingale and did not make three steps; they closed on him before he had chosen his stride, Night Knight into a restraint hold, Crimson Sable following it in, the medic left standing behind them. Orlansky was last. The young one had watched all of it happen and swung wild anyway, and all five converged on him, each of them opening the next one’s line. It was competent work. They were taking people down rather than killing them, and he found the care faintly sentimental.

Then it was over, and they were standing there breathing hard and bleeding in several places and alive. Ozone. Copper. Something burnt. Pavement dust hung over the intersection and would not settle.

The light came up out of Ogden first. Sickly green, the shade of the thing sunk in Kain’s chest, and it left the body like marrow leaves a bone. Ogden’s back arched off the asphalt, held, then went slack, and what lay there afterward was smaller than the man had been. The light crossed the pavement like iron filings crossing to a magnet. It hit Kain wet. It was organic, and it went in.

Philips, then Edgar, then Orlansky. Four streams converging on one body, and the air chilled as each of them passed.

He had cut the Heart and distributed the fragments himself. He had made four weapons out of four men and put them in the street to be broken open by whoever proved strong enough, and the Go Squad had done it beautifully. There was no pleasure in it worth the name. There was the flat satisfaction of delivery on a thing already paid for. The Heart came back together behind his sternum, whole.

Nightingale saw it before the others did.

“NO! STOP!”

Her arm was out toward Crimson Sable, who still stood over Philips with the rebar raised. There was nothing to stop. The light was already moving and it would move whether anybody swung again or not.

“You’re feeding him!”

Somebody in the ring said the other thing, low, without lifting their head.

“We killed them.”

He could not tell which of them it was. He did not much care to work it out.

The husks lay where they had been dropped, four sets of torn uniform with the men no longer in them. Before he had opened those men up they had been officers, with names and mortgages and wives who packed lunches. The Go Squad were looking at that now, and coming apart over it, and he watched a team lose the fight they had won three seconds earlier. It was inefficient of them. Grief is a luxury good, and they were buying it in the worst hour of their lives.

“Thank you,” he said.

The words went out slowly over the intersection with weather in them. He meant them, and he was polite about it, because courtesy is the cheapest instrument in the case and he had never understood why a man would leave it shut.

“I was wondering how to reclaim those fragments.”

He came up past twenty-five feet while they stood there. Twenty-eight. Thirty-one. The pharmacy sign went under his elbow and the second floor of the bank went under his ribs, thirty-five, thirty-eight, forty, and the growth ran on past the point where more of him did him any good. His skin had gone clear enough to read by. The bones stood in it, dark and shifting, and the Heart pulled at him harder than it had an hour ago, taking its fee out of the muscle nearest it. He had priced that before he ever put it in his chest. He had decided it was worth the fee.

Below him, in the middle of her people, the girl swayed. She was drowning in armor built for a bigger body, the sleeves over her knuckles, the collar dark and shining wetly, her bare feet planted apart on broken glass. She had made five ordinary people into weapons and thrown them at him, and every one of them was still alive down there. Nobody said the rest of it out loud. She had saved all of them, and she had fed him doing it.

## Chapter 28

Her legs went out from under her without any negotiation. One knee hit the pavement, then one hand, and the cold came up through her palm and stayed. The suit hung off her in every direction, the sleeves over her knuckles. Her lungs burned, and the edges of the intersection had gone soft and gray, and she let them go.

The four of them lay where they had dropped. Ogden. Philips. Edgar. Orlansky. Torn uniform with nobody inside it, flatter than men are, the shapes all wrong. Somebody in the ring had said we killed them, and Ahdia had been standing in the middle of that ring, close enough to know. It was Gloom Girl. Gloom Girl had translocated out with Firas before any of this, and the sentence was still lying in the street without her.

Kain stood over all of it at forty feet and had not stopped growing. His bones cracked like gunshots as they went, one and then another, a sound with no give in it. Under the clear skin the Heart pulsed in his chest like a dying star, red into gold and back, unhurried. Battlea and Crimson Sable had backed off the husks and were looking up at him.

“We have to move.”

Ruth had her by the arm and was already lifting.

“Now. Before he—”

Kain moved first: the arm came up, the whole intersection went dark under it, and the fist came down like a building falling.

Ahdia reached for the Seed.

She went down into the cold place behind her sternum and asked it for the thing it had always given her, and nothing came back. She had spent it getting them here, on surviving the impact, on changing her clothes in a blink.

Something took her in the hip and the pavement she was kneeling on stopped existing.

The fist landed where she had been. It took the asphalt out in a dish, the cracks ran twenty feet, and shrapnel came off the rim in a flat sheet. A chunk of road the size of a basketball went past her head close enough to move her hair. Leah was half on top of her, elbow in her ribs, already pushing off, already gone.

“Scatter!”

“Don’t let him track patterns!”

Leah went left because she was fastest and drawing him was the job. Crimson Sable broke right. Night Knight was up the face of the pharmacy toward something with an angle.

Ruth got a fist in her harness and hauled her up.

“Can you run?”

Ahdia nodded. It was a lie and Ruth took it, because there was nothing else on offer.

“Keep moving!”

“Don’t stop!”

The hand came down at Leah and closed on air. She had changed direction a half step before it committed, derby instinct, reading the momentum of something enormous and taking the angle off it.

Crimson Sable came in under the ankle with the rebar two-handed, a swing that should have taken bone out of a man. It bounced. The shock came back up the bar into his shoulders, and Ahdia watched his hands open and shut on the metal.

“It’s not working!”

There was grief in it, out where anybody could hear.

“He’s too—”

The backhand was almost courteous: Kain did not look down and did not wind up. The arm came across and Crimson Sable left the street in a flat low arc, went into the second floor of the pharmacy thirty feet away, and brick came down after him, and Victor did not move.

“Victor!”

Leah was already turning, and turning the wrong way: toward him, out into the open, straight across the front of the thing they had all been running from.

“Leah, NO!”

Ruth’s scream changed nothing. The hand came down and Leah went under it, took the landing on her shoulder, rolled out of it and came up running without losing a stride, and got to him. She had a fist in his collar and his ribs were moving.

“I’m okay,” Victor said, and it took most of what he had left.

“I’m—”

Night Knight came in from the blind side and did not go at Kain. He went at them, full body, both arms, and took all three of them out of the radius in a tangle of limbs and gear while the fingers shut on empty road.

“Futile.”

The word came down over the intersection and the glass left in the pharmacy front hummed with it.

“You’ve already lost. You fed me. Made me whole. Now watch what that bought you.”

Ahdia lay where Ruth had put her and listened to the sentence land on the people it was about. Night Knight had his head down over Victor. Leah had both hands full of his collar and had stopped moving. Nobody answered it, and there was nothing on the street that could.

She reached again.

The Seed answered her this time, cold and thin, a flicker, a fraction of what it had been an hour ago, and she held everything she had out to it, and the intersection did not slow. Ruth was running and was not going to get there. None of them were going to get anywhere, and the hands were coming down over them, and Ahdia knelt in the road with her whole self open and nothing coming through.

Then the sky screamed.

The air arrived before the sound, a shove of displaced pressure up through the pavement into her knees, and something crossed above the rooftops faster than the noise it was making. It went into Kain’s chest.

White, blinding, total, a small sun standing over downtown, and then the wave came out through the buildings and every window for three blocks let go. Forty feet of him hit the street like a falling building, and the road came up in a swell and put her down on her side.

She heard the boots before the dust gave them up, and then they came through it in a formation nobody had to think about, twenty of them, black kit and long guns, spreading around the crater and taking angles on a dead thing.

The man in front stopped at the lip and put two fingers to his ear. Ahdia knew him: she had sat across a table from him under four hundred feet of dirt, and he had been dry then.

“Overseer,” Mack said.

“Target is down. Repeat, target is down.”

The earpiece came back loud enough for Ahdia to hear it, a woman’s delivery with the affect sanded off.

“Confirmed kill?”

“Confirmed,” Mack said. He was looking into the crater when he said the rest.

“Kain is dead.”

Nobody had asked him for what came next. He gave it anyway, in the tone of a man reading a report he already knows the end of.

“Overseer wanted authorization before engaging,” Mack said.

“Pentagon wanted assurances. SecDef wanted contingencies. We stopped waiting.”

Then he turned his head an inch toward the comm.

“Some things are more important than careers, ma’am.”

Ahdia sat down in the road, legs splayed, breath going in and out too fast to do her any good, and looked across at the body in the crater.

Dead. They’d won.

A medic came at Ruth with a bag already open.

“Ma’am, we’re here to assist with—”

“I’m a doctor. ER. I’ve got this.”

Ruth did not look up from Ahdia’s arm.

“Where’s Firas? Where’s Gloom Girl? Did they—”

“Safe,” the operative said, checking his forearm.

“Translocated out before engagement. We tracked them to—”
