The ground moved.

It was smaller than the blast and it kept time, spaced out and even, like a heartbeat, coming up through her tailbone out of the crater. Down there the body had gone into motion with nothing alive in it, the chest folding inward first, everything traveling toward the Heart.

“What the—”

Mack was already moving.

“Fall back!”

The formation came apart and re-formed twenty yards out.

“Everyone back!”

Forty feet of him went to thirty. The arms folded into the torso and the torso went on folding. Twenty, and the temperature fell off a cliff, and Ahdia could see her own breath in the middle of the street. Fifteen. Ten. Five. Then there was no body at all, only a point turning two feet above the crater floor, no bigger than a basketball, so dense the air bent going past it.

“What’s happening?”

Ruth had both hands on her again.

“I don’t know.”

She was looking at it and pulling at the same time.

“But we need to move. Now.”

Then it pulled.

The debris went first: glass, brick dust, a torn length of uniform, the rebar lying where Victor had dropped it. It all came off the road and went in and was not anywhere any more, each piece faster than the last.

“Singularity!”

“It’s forming a singularity! Everyone out! NOW!”

Ahdia felt it take hold of her body. The suit rippled around her in a wind that was not wind, every fold drawn one way, and her hair went forward across her face. Ruth had her under both arms and was hauling the other way and losing, an inch and then another inch, boots skidding through glass.

“We need immediate evac! The artifact is collapsing into—”

Ahdia was not looking at the point any more, but past it, at the bank.

It was leaning. It did it so slowly that for a moment she could tell herself she had invented it, and then she could not. The windows went out of the upper floors in order, one row and then the row under it. The frame let out a sound she felt in her teeth. Steel does not scream. What came out of it was lower than that and longer, and it did not stop.

It was going to come down. It was going to come down across the whole intersection, and everyone still standing in it was underneath.

## Chapter 29

The bank leaned another degree and went on making its sound, low under everything else on the street, a note with no end built into it.

The pull found her hair first. It lifted the loose ends off her shoulders and carried them forward across her face, gentle about it, and then less gentle. Her suit went next, every fold of it pointed one direction, the sleeves emptying toward the crater like sails.

Then the pavement gave up what was lying on it. Glass came off the road in a sheet and crossed the intersection at waist height. A parking sign tore out of its footing. Brick and grit and a torn strip of black uniform crossed the road and went into the point where Kain had compressed, turning two feet above the crater floor, and nothing came out the other side of it.

Her body went third, tipped at the hip and the shoulder, her weight sliding off her heels and onto the balls of her feet without anybody asking her.

“Everyone back!”

“Fall back! NOW!”

Mack had his people moving before the second word finished. Twenty of them came off the crater rim in one piece, backward, weapons up, boots working against a floor that wanted them the other way. Nobody ran. They gave ground like men who had practiced giving ground, backward and in order, and not one of them turned around to look at it.

Ruth had her arm.

“We need to move. Can you—”

The rest of it went under the bank. The upper floors let their windows go in order, one row and then the row beneath it, and the glass did not fall. It flew, all of it, sideways, into the point. The steel behind the glass made a sound she felt in her teeth. Then the whole face of the building came off vertical and stayed there, leaning, working out how it wanted to do this.

Ten stories of it, and every person on the street standing underneath.

She could see all of them from where she was. Leah had Victor half off the pavement with his arm across her shoulders. Night Knight was coming down the front of the pharmacy with one elbow locked against his ribs. Gloom Girl held position by the overturned bus, arms out from her sides, leaning back against the drag like somebody on the deck of a boat.

“Go,” she said, and got both hands into Ruth’s harness.

“What?”

“No. We’re not—”

“GO!”

She shoved, and there was nothing kind in it, no measure in it at all. Everything left in her shoulders went into Ruth, and Ruth went back three steps and sat down hard in the glass.

“Get them out! All of them!”

“No. Don’t you—”

Ahdia turned around and walked at the crater.

“Stop her!”

Ruth came off the glass in a dead run and got four strides. Then her feet stopped being hers. The pull took her at the hip and turned her, and she went down and caught the base of a parking meter with both arms and stayed there. Leah made it as far as the light pole and stopped for the same reason. To cross the last of that ground either of them would have had to let go of what they were holding on to.

“AHDIA!”

“Don’t you DARE do this!”

The walking got harder, not easier, and that was the part she had not planned for. The current wanted all of her at once. She would not go all at once, so every foot of it was work, the soles of her bare feet dragging over grit and broken glass, the cold coming up through them, the suit hammering flat against her body.

At ten feet the ground under her opened in a spiderweb, and she planted herself in the middle of it and stopped.

Arms wide. Right hand out at the point, left hand back at the city behind her.

She went down into the cold place behind her sternum and asked for the red.

It came out of her right palm and into the core of the thing, and it was not a beam and it was not a wave, it was permission. Whatever the thing at the center of the crater was doing, it could do it now. Whatever it had left to burn, it could burn all of it in one breath and be finished with the street.

The point brightened, and the ground under her jumped hard enough to take her off her heels.

It was not enough. It was drinking the road and it would take the bank next, and the red on its own was going to need longer than the building had.

So she opened her left hand and gave the city the other direction.

The blue went out behind her and wrapped forward, and she felt it close over the crater like a palm coming down over a struck match. The pull she had used her whole life, running as a shell around the acceleration, holding the edge of the thing still while the inside of it raced.

Both directions at once, through one body, in opposite hands.

The Seed could do it. That was the thing nobody had tested. The Seed could do it, and she was only the wire it came through, and the wire was not rated for this.

Her skin opened. The fissures came up both forearms and across her chest under the suit, splits with edges to them, and what showed inside was not blood. It was white. It had structure in it, and it went down further than a body goes.

She heard herself screaming and could not feel it in her throat.

Her vision came apart into panes, and at the edge of every pane there was a corridor with doors in it. She shut her eyes and refused it. It stayed where it was, behind her eyes, waiting.

Then there were hands over her hands.

Warm ones, human ones, and she knew before she turned her head, because there was one person on this street who would cross the last ten feet of it to hold on to her instead of pull.

“I’m not losing you again!”

He had both palms over her knuckles, pushing her hands out further into the shapes she was holding them in, and he had nothing on him. No Seed. No field. Skin, and everything he had.

The cracks came up his forearms, the same splits, the same white underneath.

“No—”

“You can’t—no Seed—you’ll—”

He did not move, and nothing in his hands changed.

“Let go!”

“Firas, let GO!”

“No.”

“We do this together.”

He said it without strain, and that was how she knew the argument was already over, because he had decided it somewhere back before he ever touched her, and he had never once reopened a thing he had decided.

Her left hand knew what to do before the rest of her caught up. It went to her hip, to the thing that had been riding there since the medical bay, the one she had faked, the one that had gone into her palm instead of her leg. It came free into her fingers. It was for him. It had always been for him.

She could not turn her hand over.

The fingers were going. She could see the crater through them, and the injector sat in a hand that was in more places than one, falling and floating and lying still, all of it at the same time, and no version of her hand could close on every version of it.

“Firas—”

Then the street was gone.

The corridor was not bleeding in at the edges any more. It was all of it, floor and ceiling and both directions, and the doors ran away from her past where seeing stopped, and every one of them was lit a different color.

She looked for her hands and there were no hands. There was light in the shape of where she had been, and inside the light there was structure, depth going down through her that had nothing to do with meat, time laid open along it, older versions of her overlapping the present one like frames of film stacked out of order.

No mouth. No lungs. Nothing to scream with and nothing that needed to.

The doors were lives, and she knew it without being told. Behind one of them was an apartment she never left, a couch, a television, forty years of that and nobody hurt by it. Behind another was a warehouse floor and her own body cooling on it. Behind another, close, standing open a crack: Firas alive, and no Ahdia anywhere in it at all.

Something turned its attention on her.

It was at the far end of the corridor and it was directly in front of her, and both of those were true. It had never been human. It had never been anything that had to work its way up to being what it was.

It looked at her, and she looked back, and there was recognition in it going both directions, arriving with no words attached to it.

She had questions, all of them, everything she had wanted to ask since the warehouse.

It acknowledged her. Then it took its attention elsewhere, and whether that was dismissal or whether it was permission she could not tell, and nothing in the corridor was going to tell her.

Then something took hold of her and put her back.

It was violent, and it arrived all at once: she was torn open and reassembled inside the same instant, the corridor shattering, dimensions folding down and down, everything she had been able to see crammed back into three directions and one clock.

Back into a body. Back into flesh, which hurt, and weighed something, and had edges.

Air went into lungs she had forgotten she owned. Pavement came up under her knees, cold and solid and stupid with reality.

Firas was inches from her face.

There was a sharp pain low in her abdomen, bright and specific, and she looked down. The autoinjector stood against her with his thumb still on the plunger.

He looked up at her, still smiling, and there was nothing in his face that was fighting anything.

Ruth had both hands locked on the arm that had come back far enough to hold, her face pulled out of shape with it, screaming something the roar took before it got anywhere. Behind Ruth: Victor with both arms around Ruth’s waist and all his weight back. Leah holding Victor. Ben anchoring Leah, boots braced, shoulders down. Tess at the end of it with her arms around Ben and her legs jammed against the overturned bus.

All of them hauling. All of them on the wrong side of the pull and refusing to be on any other side of it.

They had come for her, all of them, and nobody had asked her first.

“NO!”

She got a hand on his wrist and tried to tear it off the injector, and she tried to stand, and the chain let her do neither.

“Ahdia, we can’t—the pull—”

He was still smiling.

His mouth moved, and she heard him. The roar stayed out at the edges somewhere, and the words came in under all of it, close and unhurried, arriving in her before they had any distance to cross.

“Oh hey,” he said.

“I knew you were in there somewhere.”

“Firas, please—”

The cracks went up his neck and across his jaw and the light came through them from inside, more of it every second and less of him.

Nothing dragged him. He moved, and the space he was standing in shifted over and took him along with it, and his feet never left the ground so much as the ground stopped being where he was.

He kept his eyes on her the whole way.

At the edge he looked back once.

Then he went in, and the light and the energy and all of it, everything that had ever been her brother, went down into a space smaller than a fist and was absorbed.

Gone.

The point pulsed once more, brighter than the street could stand, and then it was not there. The red had finished what it was doing. Whatever had been in the middle of it to burn was burned.

Air came in from every direction at once to fill the hole where the thing had been, and the pressure hit like a wall going past, and everything the intersection had put in the air came down and clattered and rolled and stopped.

The bank groaned. It held. Then there was no sound anywhere.

The chain came apart. The line of them went down at once, people who had been pulling against something that stopped existing, and they landed in a heap in the glass and lay there breathing.

Ruth had not let go of her arm. She had not let go of it once, and she used it now to drag Ahdia into the middle of the pile, into the elbows and the knees and the shaking, and somebody’s hand closed on the back of her neck and stayed.

Ahdia sat in the center of all of them and let them hold on.

Across the intersection, where the crater had been, there was nothing. No body. No mark on the ground except the scorch, and the scorch was going pale at the edges already.

She was alive because he decided she would be.

She never got to say goodbye.

## Chapter 30

The command center glass ran floor to ceiling, and held the cold of four hundred feet of rock behind it. She put her forehead on it. The fog came up around the print of her skin, thinned, went, and she made another one and watched that go too.

He came up on her left and stopped a polite distance short of her shoulder.

“You holding up?” he said.

“I’m standing,” she said.

“That counts for something.”

“It does.”

His reflection stood in the glass beside hers with a tablet in it he was not reading. Twelve hours of debriefing had worn the Dr. off his name somewhere in the middle of the night, and neither of them had gone back for it.

“Overseer wants to see you. When you’re ready.”

“How’s the team?”

“Checked out clean. No temporal anomalies, no residual Seed energy, no cellular degradation.”

She kept her eyes on the glass and let that sit where it landed.

“Victor’s arm is in a sling but that’s from the pull, not the temporal forces. They got lucky.”

Luck. She turned that one over, found nothing in it she could use, and put it down.

“Let’s get this over with.”

Bourn had not moved a chair. Same desk, same wall of nothing behind it, same woman who had sent her downtown alone and then written it up as a result.

“Auerbach. Have a seat.”

She sat. The chair was still too low for the desk, and she was still not going to mention it.

“How are you recovering?”

“Fine, just dandy.”

“Good.”

Bourn squared a folder she did not open. “I’m very sorry for your loss. I wanted to make sure I told you that.”

“Sure,” she said.

“Thanks.”

It came out flat and she let it stay flat. Sympathy from this desk arrived stamped and dated, like a form with a box on it that had needed ticking.

“That was quick thinking yesterday,” Bourn said.

“Using opposing temporal forces to burn out the Tamois essence while containing the collapse. Impressively, you did it on your own.”

The cold went through her fast and went out again. “I had the Go Squad,” she said.

“They formed the chain. Pulled me back. They almost died doing it.”

“Of course,” Bourn said, and moved along the page.

“They held their ground against a Terminus-level gravitational anomaly. Unarmed. That takes remarkable courage.”

She saw where the road went and got out in front of it. “It probably shouldn’t give you ideas,” she said.

“It just happened to work out this time. They used timing and positioning. Next time, they die.”

“And yet, you let them help. That took guts. Leadership. How did you know the temporal acceleration wouldn’t tear them apart?”

There was a version of this in every workplace comedy she had ever watched, the little room with the chair and the camera and somebody explaining themselves to nobody at all. She kept her hands in her lap.

“Oh, you know. Most of what we do is guess and theorize because cataclysm science is still in its nascence.”

“Yes, it is.”

“I guessed,” she said.

“The Seed was channeling through me, not them. They were pulling on flesh and bone, not exotic energy. As long as they stayed outside the acceleration field’s direct path, physics would mostly apply.”

Bourn did not write any of it down, and that was worse than a pen moving. “That’s a hell of a shot in the dark. And your brother—”

“Don’t.”

She heard how it came out of her. She did not go back for it.

“Don’t analyze that.”

Bourn let the silence run its whole length. Then she opened the right-hand drawer, set something on the wood, and pushed it across.

It was a badge. Silver, a cracked shield, the agency’s letters around the rim, and under them the name they had issued her and she had never once answered to in her own head.

“It’s yours if you want it,” Bourn said.

“I can’t use my powers anymore. The Seed’s dormant. Maybe permanently.”

“We don’t value that nearly as much as we value what you bring to the table regardless. Your methods are not CADENS ways of doing things, but that’s where you’d be an asset.”

She picked it up. It had weight. More than the size of it promised. A whole life came attached: a desk, a clearance, a door with a number on it, a folder walked in every morning for the rest of it.

She set it back down on the wood. She took her hand away. “I’m flattered,” she said.

“But I’m ready to go back to my life. The couch has been booty calling me.”

“Let me know if you ever change your mind. Until then, I trust you can keep CADENS under your hat.”

“It’s not like you won’t have FAERIS tracking me anyway.”

“We have no reason to monitor you anymore, Ms. Bacchus.”

Filed and closed and shelved with the assets that had stopped producing. She stood and put her hand out. Bourn took it. The grip was dry and short and correct.

“It’s been a pleasure and a privilege,” she said.

“One last thing.”

Bourn had not let go yet. “How close did you come to draining all of your life force yesterday?”

Behind her eyes there was a corridor with doors down both sides of it, lit in colors she had no words for, and a thing at the far end that had turned its attention on her and then taken it somewhere else. She could put all of that on this desk. It would go into a folder with a review date and it would belong to them, and then she would sit here while a stranger explained it back to her.

She smiled instead, the one she used on cashiers, and got her hand back, and went out.

The intercom clicked behind her before the door swung shut. “Please send in Dr. Ryu,” Bourn said.

“Yes, ma’am,” the voice on the intercom said.

* * *

“Welcome to Carl Tucker Tonight,” the anchor said from the TV. Nobody had turned the television off in a day and a half. It ran on the far wall over the fireplace nobody used, throwing a man in a good suit across four exhausted people and a coffee table covered in gear they had not put away. “When the nation’s first response to prank calls from some yahoos with too much time on their hands is to scream like chicken little that the sky is falling, we have to wonder what kind of leadership we have currently steering the ship. Just because officials have a hint of something possibly happening, in this case a supposed critical gas leak in the city’s center, all of Caledonia gets completely evacuated? The National Guard gets called in? Where were they while crime has been running rampant and overwhelming our city’s noble police force . . . ?”

Ruth reached over and killed the sound. The man went on talking into the carpet.

The gear on the table was theirs again: ham radios with tape on the battery doors, a coil of line, black cloth washed so many times it had gone the color of weather. The armored cases had gone back down the elevator that morning with two men who signed for them.

“I wish they let us keep the suits,” Victor said.

“Lot of good it did you,” Leah said.

“My arm might have been taken clean off without it!”

“We don’t need them,” Ruth said.

“Right,” Tess said, and turned a radio over in her hands.

“We already have ‘uniforms.’ Still . . . being bulletproof . . .”

Ruth put her palms on the table’s edge. There was a way to walk them up to this, and she had no fuel for the walk.

“I mean we don’t need them because we’re done,” Ruth said. “The Go Squad. It’s all over.”

Tess stopped turning the radio. “What? Why?”

“Guys . . . come on. Do I have to spell out why?”

“We have the proof to expose the cops,” Ben said.

“And my consumer advocacy group has filed a lawsuit against the money we paid to the Kain PAC,” Victor said, and shifted the sling with his good hand.

“Ahdia may not get her money back but neither will Kain. That money will get frozen.”

“And?”

Nobody answered that, so she did. “You guys know Kain is no worse off than before, right? He’s already rebuilding his mansion. His campaign is just rolling right along. What we do . . . what we’re done with now is and has been insignificant.”

“When we expose the Police Chief, it’s gotta connect to him, though,” Ben said.

Victor shook his head. “Wrong,” he said. “Ruth’s right. It’ll connect to the PAC. His hands will be clean.” Victor said it to the table, because he had gotten there ahead of the rest of them and had been hoping to be wrong.

“And what about Ahdia?” Tess said.

“We haven’t seen her since yesterday. Since downtown. Where is she? She just sends me access to her penthouse and bank account and doesn’t make contact? No, there are too many reasons we need to keep laying low. The Go Squad is too dangerous. Anyway, we’re not the Go Squad without Firas. He started all this. It was always his.”

Leah had not moved through any of it. She sat forward now with her elbows on her knees.

“Ruth . . .”

“What?”

“You’ve been holding us together,” Leah said. “Since it happened. Since he—”

Leah stopped and took a different road at it. “You’re the one who made sure we all got out. You organized the chain. You’ve been carrying us.”

“Okay,” Ruth said.

“Then listen to me now . . . this world . . . doesn’t need us. We’re just a bunch of crazies running around, hopping around rooftops in our pajamas in the middle of the night. And we just lost—”

The name went out from under her. She stood in it. Hands still flat on the table, she made herself take the next breath through her nose.

“We just lost Firas. Yesterday. He’s gone and that’s—”

She got the rest out level, because there was a thing to say and saying it was work and work she could do.

“That’s as much of a price I’m willing to pay to keep doing this. I can’t lose any of you, too.”

“You won’t,” Ahdia said.

She was in the doorway with her hands in her coat pockets. There was a duffel bag on the floor at her feet, packed and zipped, sitting there like something that intended to stay.

* * *

He fixed his tie outside the door, which was a thing he did before every meeting on this floor and had never once helped.

“Dr. Ryu, come in,” Bourn said. “Have a seat.”

“Are you anxious, doctor?”

“No,” Ryu said. “Why? Do I seem anxious?”

“It’s why I asked,” Bourn said.

“Well . . . I thought maybe you were calling me in here to admonish me for letting Auerbach leave the facility early.”

“No, I understand why you did.”

Bourn turned her monitor so he could see the column of medical panels on it. He knew the shape of that column. He had signed the top half of it himself.

“But we need to discuss what happened with the treatment protocol.”

“Ma’am?”

“She lied to us.”

“The eighth treatment. The one she told you she’d completed before leaving the facility.”

He finished it for her. “She didn’t take it.”

The room stayed where it was. He put his hands on his knees.

“Medical analysis confirms she left at approximately one-third cellular recovery. Far below combat readiness, let alone channeling opposing temporal forces through her body.”

“She walked into a Terminus-level event critically compromised. It’s a miracle she’s alive.”

He put it against what she had told him in the corridor and it came out where it had to. She had said the eighth was in. She had said it with her sleeve already down, and then walked past him into the worst hour of the city’s life.

“That was . . . tactically unsound,” he said.

“It was reckless. And effective.”

Bourn let that stand a moment, then closed the panels. “Which brings me to the second issue. Kain.”

“I’m sorry, ma’am. I thought—downtown, the singularity—”

“You saw the press conference this morning?”

“I was dealing with equipment inventory,” Ryu said.

She turned the monitor the rest of the way around. A podium, flags behind it, a man with heavy rings on both hands looking gravely into the middle distance.

“—tragic incident involving what appeared to be an experimental weapon. We believe this was an attempt by criminal elements to impersonate me and destabilize my campaign. I was, thankfully, nowhere near downtown at the time. The authorities are investigating—”

“He’s spinning it. Claims the entity at the singularity was a lookalike. Some kind of bioweapon or cataclysm construct.”

“That’s . . .”

He got up out of the chair and sat back down in it. “That’s absurd. We have footage. Thermal signatures. Energy readings from the Tamois Heart in his chest.”

“And he has lawyers. And a story that doesn’t require the public to believe in cosmic artifacts and temporal singularities.”

“We can’t touch him. Not officially.”

She took a sheet off the folder and turned it face up between them, and he had seen that seal maybe twice in three years.

“The authorization,” Ryu said. “The authorization that never came.”

“I requested executive clearance to intervene six hours before Auerbach went downtown. Do you know when I received it?”

He did not. “Forty-seven minutes ago,” Bourn said. “Fourteen hours after the event concluded. With a note thanking us for our ‘patience and restraint during the review process.’”

Something in him went cold and stayed there. “They wanted us hamstrung.”

“They wanted plausible deniability. If Auerbach failed, no connection to CADENS. If she succeeded, no political fallout from officially acknowledging cataclysm threats.”

“Either way, Kain walks free. We’re left cleaning up gravitational anomalies and explaining to city officials why downtown looks like a meteor strike.”

“So what do we do, ma’am?”

“We document everything. We build the file. And we wait.”

“For what?”

“For Kain to make a mistake. For the authorization to mean something. For whoever’s pulling strings above us to realize that threats like this don’t go away just because we pretend they don’t exist.”

She squared the sheet with the edge of the desk and did not look up from it.

“In the meantime, we do our job. Monitor cataclysm activity. Track the Seed-bearers. And hope that next time, a depressed girl with time powers and a death wish isn’t our only line of defense.”

* * *

The duffel stayed by the door a long while as everyone talked at once. Victor wanted to know how she had got up here, and Tess wanted to know if she had eaten, and Ben stood up and then had nowhere to go with it. Ahdia let all of it run at her.

“Guys,” Ahdia said. The room came off the boil. “I just wanted to say . . . thank you.”

“You guys saved my life yesterday. Downtown. The singularity.”

“When I was channeling those opposing forces, when I went . . . somewhere else . . . you pulled me back. Formed that chain and hauled me out of a collapsing dimension.”

“Even without me helping you in the background, you guys are real, genuine heroes. You inspired me to get off the couch and do something worth a damn. At first, I thought it was just to prove to Firas that I wasn’t a screwup. I’ve learned so much about you guys and about myself since then. And what I’ve learned . . . is that this city needs us. This world, this universe, this dimension and all others. Between the corrupt cops, to Kain and the people who support that madman, and things happening that people don’t know about, cataclysms that either provide unthinkable advances for humanity or threatens it entirely. I know I can’t go back to my life the way it was before you guys but you’re the only family I have and my family is unbeatable.”

Leah put both hands over her face and left them there a second. “How can we say no to that?”

“We don’t,” Ben said.

“We keep up the fight. We stay vigilant. We stay ready.”

“And we help the people who need it the most,” Victor said.

“Because there’s no one but us,” Tess said.

Then all four of them turned and looked at the woman with her palms still flat on the table, and Leah dropped her hands.

“This doesn’t work without you, boss,” Ahdia said.

Ruth looked at the muted television, and at the gear, and at the girl in the doorway who had gone somewhere yesterday and come back with a bag.

“If we do this,” Ruth said, “we start over. Build it up from the bottom because we’re not just beating up muggers and getting old ladies safely across the street anymore.”

“We need to be ready for cataclysms, cops and Kain,” Ruth said.

Victor got his good arm around Ahdia’s neck and pulled her in and held on.

“Oh, my,” Leah said. “So . . . ?”

Ruth put her teeth in her lower lip and kept them there, and the room let her have the whole length of it.

* * *

Sunset went across the city sideways and put five long shadows down the gravel of the highest roof in it.

All of them were in the matte black armor, cut to their measurements, the same suits that had come off them after downtown. Ben had his old vest on over his. Leah had the pads she skated in strapped over hers. Tess had her hood up. Victor had rebar taped at one end. Ruth had a harness she had rebuilt twice with her own hands. Thirty blocks south, forty feet of nothing hummed on a residential roof and threw no shadow at all.

“All clear,” Ahdia said in their ears. “Happy hunting.”

“Copy,” Ruth said.

She went to the ledge, put a boot up on it, and turned around.

“This is it,” Ruth said. “Yesterday, we lost someone. Today, we keep going. Because that’s what Firas would’ve wanted. Because people still need help. Because we’re the Go Squad, and we go when nobody else will.”

Nobody made a sound. The wind came up the face of the building and moved the loose ends of everything they had on.

“It’s not just Caledonia anymore. Not just muggers and corrupt cops. It’s singularities and cosmic entities and things we don’t have names for yet. No better place.”

“No better time,” they said back to her.

“You got it, Ahdia?”

“Everyone’s in position,” Ahdia said. “Ready when you are.”

Ruth stepped up onto the ledge. Arms out. She went off it backward.

The others came after her in order, four seconds apart. They had drilled the spacing on a stairwell in a school with no roof access at all. The wings caught and hardened. The four of them fanned out over the grid, north and west and two south, and the city came up like a floor rising to meet them.

She flew her lane down the avenue, the streetlights coming on underneath her in a wave. She tucked at the last. She flared. Her boots came down on a gravel roof across from a corner store with its door standing open and a man inside it holding a bag.

Then the gravel let go of her. An inch, maybe. Her heels came up off it. They hung there. Then they settled back down. There was nothing in her hands, no cable, no wing out, no reason for it anywhere in the equipment she was wearing.

Thirty blocks south, a machine that belonged to nobody hummed on a roof.

“Thank you,” Ruth said into the radio, and went over the parapet.

“Go get ’em. I got your back.”

Ahdia sat in the middle of a horseshoe of monitors she had no clearance to touch, in a machine she had taken out of a hangar without filling in anything, wearing a coat and socks and holding a cigarette she had not lit.

Down there they had a whole building for nights like this one. They would log it, and file it, and wait for the right signature to come through the right channel, and there was a man on the third monitor backing out of a corner store with a bag in his hand.

Nobody down there was going to get to him tonight.

## Epilogue

“—and I want to assure the good people of Caledonia that I was nowhere near downtown during this tragic incident.”

The lights were low and warm, and set where he had asked, no shadow under the eyes. Grief photographs badly. Steadiness photographs well, and steadiness in a man who has been attacked photographs better than anything money can otherwise buy.

“We believe this was an attempt by criminal elements to impersonate me and destabilize my campaign.”

He let a beat go, because the room wanted one. “The authorities are investigating. I have full confidence in our law enforcement and our intelligence agencies to get to the bottom of this terrorist attack.”

Thirty cameras. The polling would come in overnight and it would be good, and the good would be worth more than the mansion, which was insured.

“That’s all for tonight. Thank you.”

The aides took him off the riser and down a hall and into a service elevator, and the doors closed on the noise and cut it off like a switch. At the bottom of the building a man in a gray uniform looked at a screen and stepped aside.

“Clear,” one of the aides said into his earpiece.

Then a second elevator, which went down. Past the garage. Past the mechanical floors with their pipes and their painted numbers. Past four levels that appear on no drawing filed with any office in the city. Forty-seven seconds, lobby to bottom. He had counted it once, back when this part still made him nervous, and had not needed the number since.

The corridor at the bottom was seamless white, with no doors in it, and the sensors sat in the walls where the seams would have been. His shoes made no sound. He had priced this corridor. It came in over. Worth it.

The tank room hummed at a pitch you felt in your teeth. Rows of them, glass and fluid and a soft green light coming up from below, and in every one of them a body floating with its chin down and its hands open. A boy of nine. A young man with hair. Himself at forty, at fifty, at the weight he carried now and at a weight he had not been in twenty years. All of them asleep. All of them paid for.

Halfway down the second row a cylinder stood open and dry, the cradle empty, the number stenciled at eye height. Forty-seven. An acceptable loss. The accounting on it was clean.

“You look tired,” a woman’s voice said.

She was standing at the end of the row and she had not been there a moment ago. White coat, no badge on it, no age on her at all, and the proportions of her a degree off in a direction he had long ago stopped trying to put a word to.

“Forty-seven was our best integration,” he said.

“The Tamois Heart had fully bonded. Years of work. Gone.”

“And yet you’re here.”

“That’s the point of redundancy.”

“I know the point.”

He looked at the empty cylinder and not at her. “I’m living it.”

“Are you?”

She came down the row without hurrying. “Forty-seven thought he was special too. Thought the Heart made him more than a vessel. Look where that got him.”

“How long until we can reintegrate a Heart?”

“We have three more in storage. We can begin the bonding process within a week.”

“But that’s not what you should be worried about.”

“What should I be worried about?”

“Agent Auerbach saw Bellatrix.”

The hum went on around them. He took his hands out of his pockets and put them back.

“During the singularity?”

“According to the readings CADENS picked up, yes. She transcended. Briefly. Made contact with higher-dimensional space.”

“She saw the hallway.”

“That’s not possible. The Seed was dormant, we made sure—”

“Nothing is ever certain with the Seeds. That’s why they were scattered in the first place. To see what would happen. Who would find them. What they’d do with them.”

She stopped in front of the empty tank and looked into it as though something were still in there.

“Bellatrix doesn’t interfere. She observes. But she acknowledged Auerbach. That means something.”

“What does it mean?”

“It means the girl is more important than we thought. It means her death might have consequences we can’t predict.”

“It means we proceed carefully.”

He had a file on the girl. It was thin and it was cheap and it had a line through it as of yesterday afternoon.

“The plan was always to eliminate her once the Seed went dormant.”

“Plans change.”

She said it as an item already decided in a room he had not been invited into. “Focus on the election. Win the presidency. That’s your role. Let me worry about the girl.”

The door took her and closed, and the corridor beyond it made no sound.

He stood where he was, in the hum, among the rows of himself, every one of them asleep and waiting to be worn.
